[105] In the Environmental Dimension: Environmental Policy of Defense, the Armed Forces, and the Environment Chile shares with many countries, especially those in the Americas, a series of institutional, social, and cultural traits; however, it also possesses characteristics that distinguish it not only among them, but, in general, within the community of nations

[106] These peculiarities are a product of the historical experience of the Chilean people, their geographical environment, their cultural heritage and traditions, as well as the evolution of public institutions since their inception with national independence

[107] As a state, Chile participates in the international system like other countries, but it is precisely through its unique characteristics that the Chilean state defines the particular way in which it integrates into it

[108] Like the rest of the States in the international system, the State of Chile sets goals and objectives

[109] In general, countries seek to achieve this in a political environment characterized by phenomena of cooperation with other countries and international competition

[110] This requires, in any case, a state leadership effort in which the various relevant public bodies are coordinated in decision-making and implementation

[111] THE STATE OF CHILE Chapter I. THE STATE I

[112] Basic Principles of the State The Political Constitution of the Republic (CPR) establishes the principles that guide the legal and political order of Chile, and express the continuity of the republican tradition of the national State

[113] These are principles also recognized by other countries that share the premises of a democratic regime and respect for human beings and their rights, but each country puts its own unique stamp on them in the task of giving them daily validity

[114] In Chile, there is broad recognition of the human person, their dignity and rights, as well as of the communities in which they develop, starting with the family

[115] Full respect for the rights of individuals imposes on the State the objectives set forth in Articles 1 and 5 of our Constitution (Box

[116] The inherent dignity and equality of all people also underpin priority socioeconomic objectives for the country, such as social cohesion and integration and the eradication of poverty

[117] Just as the constitutional text recognizes rights for individuals, it also establishes duties

[118] Among these, those outlined in Article 22 are of particular relevance to the country's security and defense; they underpin the concept that defense is the responsibility of all Chileans (Box

[119] The principles of Chile's republican and democratic institutional order and national objectives play a crucial role in the foundations of national defense

[120] They support the architecture of defense and guide the State's actions toward the country's external security

[121] From these, it is possible to appreciate the importance of the security challenges the country faces in the international environment and the process of State leadership to successfully overcome them

[122] • The State is at the service of the human person and its purpose is to promote the common good, for which it must contribute to creating the social conditions that allow each and every member of the national community to achieve their greatest possible spiritual and material fulfillment, with full respect for the rights and guarantees established in this Constitution

[123] • It is the duty of the State to safeguard national security, provide protection to the population and the family, strive to strengthen it, promote the harmonious integration of all sectors of the Nation, and ensure the right of people to participate with equal opportunities in national life

[124] Article 5 (paragraph 2): The exercise of sovereignty recognizes as a limitation the respect for essential rights that emanate from human nature

[125] It is the duty of State bodies to respect and promote these rights, guaranteed by this Constitution, as well as by the international treaties ratified by Chile and currently in force

[126] Article 22: • Every inhabitant of the Republic must respect Chile and its national emblems

[127] • Chileans have a fundamental duty to honor their country, defend its sovereignty, and contribute to preserving national security and the essential values of Chilean tradition

[128] • Military service and other personal obligations imposed by law are mandatory under the terms and forms determined by law

[129] • Chileans who are able to carry weapons must be registered in the Military Registries, unless they are legally exempt

[130] Institutional Order: Democracy and the Rule of Law Chile is a democratic republic, as established by its Political Constitution, where the rule of law prevails, along with the principles of constitutional supremacy, legality, judicial protection, and oversight of state administration acts

[131] In this democratic and pluralistic context, the State guarantees the right of people to participate with equal opportunities in national life and the full exercise of individual freedoms

[132] The State of Chile is unitary and is administratively organized into regions, provinces and communes (chart

[133] State bodies will promote the strengthening of the country's regionalization and equitable and supportive development among the regions, provinces, and municipalities within the country

[134] Its sovereignty and jurisdiction are exercised throughout the Chilean mainland and insular territory, as well as in Chilean airspace and maritime areas. Its responsibility extends to broader airspace and maritime areas in accordance with international treaties that establish special functions

[135] Chile has sovereign rights over its Antarctic territory, based on historical, geographical, and legal grounds, which it exercises in a manner consistent with its membership in the 1959 Antarctic Treaty and other instruments of the Antarctic system

[136] According to the Political Constitution of the Republic, sovereignty is exercised by the people, either directly (elections and plebiscites) or through the authorities that represent them, recognizing the essential rights that emanate from human nature as the limits of their actions

[137] In its foreign relations, Chile fully adheres to the principles of peaceful dispute settlement, non-intervention in the internal affairs of other States, respect for international law, and the inviolability of treaties

[142] Constitutional Assumptions of Defense Along with the purposes of the State considered in the Political Constitution of the Republic, there are constitutional assumptions that contribute to guiding and giving coherence to the overall activity of the State, particularly defense

[143] Among them, the following can be mentioned: the preservation of the nation, the maintenance of its political independence, the maintenance of its sovereignty and the maintenance of the integrity of the national territory

[144] These assumptions are inherent to the existence of the Chilean State as such and are configured as a manifestation of a historical experience that begins with the emergence of the Chilean nation and its political independence

[145] Although they are not expressly established in the text of the Constitution or in any specific public or legal document, they are part of the foundations on which our country's constitutional regulations are based

[146] Aside from the aforementioned constitutional assumptions, Chile, like any state, has aspirations that are part of its general political interests or that are part of the historical purposes that motivate some of its public institutions, such as maintaining international relations aligned with national development, the needs for cooperation, and the country's international projection

[147] Likewise, certain aspirations are found in the political and cultural heritage of the Chilean people

[148] This category includes maintaining peaceful and supportive civic coexistence; preserving and enriching national identity and cultural heritage; achieving high, sustained, and sustainable economic development; and achieving social development in line with economic development, based on individual capacity and equal opportunities for all

[149] Each government, based on its own principles, promotes a public agenda that materializes and advances aspirations such as those mentioned

[150] Governments express these policies in public policies for a variable period of time, but generally do so aiming at a time horizon that does not necessarily expire within their own periods

[151] Naturally, it will be easier to achieve citizen commitment to the country's defense and security the greater the awareness and consensus regarding the goals to be achieved

[152] THE STATE OF CHILE

[153] THE NATIONAL TERRITORY

[154] The territory, together with the population, is a basic condition for the existence of the national State

[155] It constitutes the space where the state legal system is applied and geographically individualizes the Chilean population

[156] It also establishes the scope of state action materialized in public policies, one of which corresponds to defense policy

[157] The characteristics of the territory and its fundamental elements entail needs that the State must address, and which impose the duty to act responsibly in its protection and defense

[158] Chile's territory is defined and delimited by borders that are the result of current treaties, freely and legitimately agreed upon, and by arbitral awards

[159] Considering its South American continental and insular parts, it has an area of 756,096 km

[160] Of its total land border of 6,630 km, it shares 180 km with Peru, 850 km with Bolivia, and 5,600 km with Argentina

[161] These figures do not include the Chilean Antarctic Territory, nor the maritime borders with Peru and Argentina

[162] Figures are approximate

[163] The protection of the territory and its population is, ultimately, the objective of defense

[164] For this reason, the particularities of the national territory and the Chilean people constitute fundamental references to consider in the formulation of national defense policy

[165] The following pages describe the most relevant characteristics of our territory, its geography, and the people who inhabit it

[201] The national territory is located in the western and southern parts of South America, reaching as far as the Antarctic continent and Easter Island in the Western Pacific

[202] The continental part of the national territory has an area of approximately 755,776 km

[203] This vast land area—or continental Chile—has diverse geographic characteristics and a wide range of natural resources

[204] It therefore constitutes one of the essential factors in defining defense objectives

[205] From this perspective, three strategically important zones can be identified: a. The northern zone, with large and small-scale, sparsely populated areas and predominantly desert climate conditions, concentrates large- and small-scale mining, constituting the primary economic activity in the national export strategy, contributing a high percentage to the country's Gross Domestic Product

[206] This area has a specialized industrial and transportation infrastructure whose importance lies in its ability to meet our country's international commitments

[207] b. More than 85% of the population lives in the central and southern regions, which are characterized by their agricultural, livestock, and significant forestry industries

[208] This area represents the administrative and industrial heart of the country, with the capacity to serve as a platform for generating services

[209] c. The southern region is characterized by its geographic fragmentation, although this is mitigated by road connectivity projects developed by the government and the Army

[210] Its potential is linked to its freshwater resources, its privileged location close to interoceanic passages, and its projection to the Antarctic continent

[211] Each of these zones represents a safeguard for the country's essential land space—and, therefore, territorial integrity—in situations of crisis and war

[212] In addition to the mainland, Chile's territory also includes oceanic islands and the Chilean Antarctic Territory, with their respective maritime and airspace, giving the country a tricontinental character

[213] The insular part of the national territory has an area of approximately 320 km2 and includes several islands in the Pacific Ocean

[214] The closest are the Juan Fernández archipelago, 360 nautical miles (667 km) from the continent, and the San Félix and San Ambrosio islands, 500 nautical miles (926 km

[215] The furthest away are Salas y Gómez Island, 1,870 nautical miles (3,463 km), and Easter Island, 2,000 nautical miles (3,700 km), or further in the case of the extended continental shelf (Box

[216] This maritime space corresponds to the "Sea of Chile", which was declared on May 30, 1974 by Supreme Decree No. 346, indicating that it consists of "those waters of the sea that bathe or surround the national territory", a designation that in no way prejudges or alters the legal regime, internal or international, of the waters referred to in the previous paragraph

[217] Official Journal of June

[218] A significant event in the determination of maritime spaces was the 1947 presidential proclamation of sovereignty over the 200 nautical miles of maritime zone

[219] BOX 5: CONTINENTAL SHELF By virtue of its insular areas and continental land projection into the sea, the United Nations Convention on the Law of the Sea recognizes the inherent right of States to a continental shelf extending beyond 200 nautical miles, when the natural and legal requirements for such an establishment are met

[220] Based on this authority, in May 2009, the Chilean State completed the process of submitting the corresponding preliminary report on five areas of extended continental shelf to the United Nations Commission on the Limits of the Continental Shelf

[221] This document includes references to the Antarctic territory, highlighting the validity of the rules of the Antarctic Treaty

[222] Considering that Chile has a coastline of more than 4,300 km, this results in a surface area of 3,409,122.34 km² of sea, not including the maritime projection of the Chilean Antarctic Territory, over which our country exercises jurisdiction in accordance with the international legal instruments applicable in the area (Chart

[223] In addition, it is necessary to mention the aquatic spaces that are generated in inland, river and lake waters, particularly south of Puerto Montt

[224] Our maritime territory extends, to the north, from the boundary parallel that passes through latitude 18°21'03" of marker No. 1 on the border with Peru to the shores of the Antarctic continent to the south, except for the portion of the high seas that forms the Drake Passage, between Cape Horn and the Antarctic Peninsula

[225] Chile's maritime areas comprise the ocean waters extending from the baselines on the continental coast to the western limit of the Exclusive Economic Zone and the extended continental shelf

[226] In these spaces the following territorial and legal concepts apply: Territorial Sea of 12 nautical miles (NM) in length (22 km), measured from the baselines, where full sovereignty is exercised, recognizing the right of innocent passage of foreign vessels exercised in accordance with the United Nations Convention on the Law of the Sea

[227] • Contiguous Zone of 12 nautical miles (22 km), extending from the outer limit of the territorial sea to 24 NM from the baselines, where inspection is carried out both to prevent and to sanction violations of customs, tax, immigration or health laws and regulations that are committed in the territory or in the territorial sea

[228] Exclusive Economic Zone (EEZ), understood as the maritime area that extends up to 200 NM (370 km), measured from the baselines, from which the territorial sea is measured

[229] Here, the State exercises sovereign rights with regard to the exploitation, exploration, conservation and management of the living and non-living natural resources of the waters overlying the seabed and of the seabed and subsoil, and with regard to other activities aimed at the economic exploration and exploitation of the area

[230] The State also has jurisdiction over the establishment and use of artificial islands, installations and structures, marine scientific research, and the protection and preservation of the environment, along with other rights provided for in the United Nations Convention on the Law of the Sea

[231] In the southern sector, the national maritime territory includes the maritime spaces belonging to the Chilean Antarctic Territory, starting from the Antarctic shores, between the meridians 53° W and 90° W, where Chile exercises its rights in accordance with its own regime of the Antarctic Treaty and the norms of the Antarctic system; in the sea of the southern zone (Sea of Peace), according to the Treaty of Peace and Friendship between Chile and Argentina of 1984, to the east and south of the straight baselines, the effects of the territorial sea in relation to Argentina are limited to 3 nautical miles, being 12 nautical miles for the rest of the international community

[232] It should be noted that, in accordance with the United Nations Convention on the Law of the Sea and international custom, Chile has a large area of inland waters in the southern and southern sectors, which form part of its maritime territory

[233] These inland waters are those found within the straight baselines, being fjords, bays, channels and other features or indentations typical of our territory

[234] The continental waters that make up navigable lakes and rivers are part of the land area they border, although, for the purposes of national legislation, they are considered part of the administration of waters that form part of maritime territory

[235] In internal waters, the State exercises full sovereignty in accordance with the general rules of international law

[236] To the spaces described above, we must add the maritime spaces of oceanic islands, which have territorial sea, a contiguous zone, an exclusive economic zone, and a continental shelf, which are defined according to the criteria of the United Nations Convention on the Law of the Sea

[237] Between these limits and the spaces adjacent to the continental coast of Chile, there is a part of the high seas that has been called the Presence Sea, which exercises presence and also assumes a series of international responsibilities related to the protection of human life at sea, the control of maritime traffic and the conservation of the environment with unrestricted adherence to international law

[238] Thus, Chile seeks to project its own maritime interests in this maritime space and respects its status as an asset of the international community, where every State has rights and obligations, including environmental oversight and the conservation of marine resources

[239] Chile has no claims to sovereignty over the Sea of Presence; its interest and willingness are to participate in international agreements that will protect this area from abusive uses or activities that, due to its proximity, could harm the resources of Chilean waters

[240] Defined in Supreme Decree No. 346, of May

[241] As shown in Chart 3, the legal regimes applicable in Chile's various maritime spaces vary depending on whether they are spaces under its full sovereignty, where it has sovereign rights and exclusive jurisdiction, where national legal regulations predominate and where the principles of international law and the treaties to which Chile is a party are certainly applied, or spaces where common principles and rules apply to the entire international community, which our country respects and with which it actively cooperates because they are the high seas or the area governing the common heritage of humanity for the resources of the seabed and ocean floor

[242] Since its entry into force, the 1954 Convention on the Special Maritime Border Zone, in force between Ecuador, Peru and Chile, establishes a special zone, starting 12 nautical miles from the coast, 10 nautical miles wide on each side of the parallel that constitutes the respective maritime boundary between the aforementioned countries

[243] This provision has made it possible to manage border zones on the basis of the full validity of the maritime boundary

[244] • In the Chilean-Peruvian border area, this Special Zone has facilitated maritime fishing activity, overcoming the difficulties encountered by small vessels crewed by seafarers with limited nautical knowledge or who lack the necessary instruments to determine their position on the high seas and who frequently, innocently and accidentally, violate the maritime boundary between these two neighboring States

[245] GRAPHIC 3 LEGAL REGIMES OF THE OCEANS INTERIOR WATERS BASELINE HIGH SEAS LPI TERRITORIAL SEA CONTIGUOUS ZONE EXCLUSIVE ECONOMIC ZONE CONTINENTAL SHELF EXTENDED CONTINENTAL SHELF DEEP SEABED MN MN MN Article 126 bis of the Political Constitution of the Republic, incorporated by constitutional reform Law No. 20,193, published in the Official Gazette on July

[246] The Insular Territory The insular part of the national territory includes several islands in the Pacific Ocean, the closest being the Juan Fernández archipelago, 360 nautical miles (667 km) from the continent, and the San Félix and San Ambrosio islands, 500 nautical miles (926 km

[247] The furthest are Salas y Gómez Island at 1,870 nautical miles (3,463 km) and Easter Island at 2,000 nautical miles (3,700 km

[248] • Easter Island (Rapa Nui or Te Pito Te Henua) In 2007, the State of Chile approved the declaration in the Political Constitution of the Republic that Easter Island and the Juan Fernández Archipelago are “special territories”, that is, they are parts of the Chilean territory governed by a “special statute” of decentralized administration in order to strengthen their integration with the continent and promote development with respect for their traditions and culture

[249] In 1870, the Chilean government sent the corvette O'Higgins to Easter Island, with First Lieutenant Arturo Prat Chacón as Second Commander

[250] During his stay, a hydrographic survey of the island was carried out

[251] In 1875, the Chilean Navy sent the corvette O'Higgins again, and on this occasion Lieutenant Policarpo Toro Hurtado was on board

[252] Later, the Navy arranged for the 1886 training voyage for midshipmen to be made to Easter Island, assigning the corvette Abtao to this task and taking the then Lieutenant Commander Policarpo Toro Hurtado as instructor

[253] Based on the knowledge acquired, Toro presented a report on the island to his institution in

[254] This report highlighted the possibility of Easter Island becoming part of our country

[255] In addition to its status as a firm territory in the middle of the Western Pacific, a maritime space of natural interest to Chile, there was also the need to provide protection to its inhabitants under the flag of our country and thus prevent the abuses to which they were being subjected by foreigners who landed there

[256] The Navy supported Captain Toro's work and presented it to the government of President Manuel Balmaceda, who expressed support for its implementation

[257] The following year, in July 1888, a group of 12 Chileans arrived on the island aboard the transport Angamos, marking the beginning of a new era in the evolution of our oceanic territory

[258] It is noteworthy that, by 1877, the native island population barely exceeded one hundred people

[259] On September 9, 1888, Chilean Lieutenant Commander Policarpo Toro Hurtado took possession of Easter Island on behalf of the Chilean government, and from that day on, the island became incorporated into Chilean sovereignty

[260] This act, which was agreed upon by the native population and their representatives, was preceded by efforts by Chilean institutions to develop a broad and comprehensive understanding of this territory and establish respectful relationships with its inhabitants

[261] In 1995, Easter Island was declared a World Cultural Heritage Site, in accordance with the UNESCO Convention on the Protection of the World Cultural and Natural Heritage

[262] Airspace Airspace results from the projection of imaginary lines into the atmosphere from maritime and land territories under national sovereignty or national responsibility in its different degrees

[263] With regard to this national space, the State of Chile exercises sovereignty, rights, and jurisdiction in the spaces overlying its continental and insular territory and territorial sea

[264] This airspace extends from the northern limit 18°21'03'' S to the South Pole, following the mainland and the territorial sea

[265] Our country has been assigned airspace under its control, exercised by the Chilean Air Force in accordance with international standards, from the international political boundary to the east to the 131° 00' W meridian. The total airspace entrusted by international organizations to be controlled by Chile covers an area of 31.9 million km², which includes its continental and insular territory, its maritime spaces and the high seas (chart

[266] The upper limit of this space, which lies in the range of 80 to 100 kilometers in altitude and constitutes the border with outer space, is a common good of humanity

[267] The countries bordering Chile, in terms of this 360º space, are: Argentina, Bolivia, Peru, Tahiti (French Polynesia) and New Zealand

[268] In the South Pacific, the boundaries with Auckland Oceanic (NZ) are established from latitude 30° S and longitude 131° W. The principle of the sovereignty of States in the airspace above their territory is recognized by the International Aeronautical Community

[269] This principle was confirmed at the International Civil Aviation Conference, known as the “Chicago Convention,” in

[270] Chile's responsibilities in its national airspace are defined in the Chicago Convention and the agreements that gave rise to the International Civil Aviation Organization (ICAO

[271] It is at the ICAO, of which our country is a member, that the international community agrees, as a duty of States, to provide safety and protection to air navigation in a standardized and regulated manner

[272] AIR CONTROL SPACE II

[273] The Chilean Antarctic Territory In relation to the Chilean Antarctic Territory, this is located south of the Drake Passage and forms a spherical triangle that projects to the South Pole between the meridians 53º and 90º West longitude, with an approximate area of 1,250,000 km

[274] Chile in Antarctica and the Antarctic Treaty The Chilean Antarctic Territory constitutes a relevant element for the definition of Antarctic policy

[275] In November 1940, Chile, through Supreme Decree No. 1,747, specified the boundaries of its territory in “the part that extends towards the polar region called 'American Antarctica'.”

[276] This Decree establishes that "the Chilean Antarctic or Chilean Antarctic Territory is made up of all the lands, islands, islets, reefs, glaciers and other known or unknown, in the respective territorial sea existing within the limits of the ice cap formed by the meridians 53° and 90° longitude West of Greenwich

[277] Chile's Antarctic rights are based on historical, geographical, and legal foundations, consolidated and communicated to the international community, with the year 1906 being relevant to demonstrate this position

[278] With this background, Chile participated in the negotiation of the Antarctic Treaty in 1959, along with eleven other States

[279] It should be noted that two other countries have made claims of sovereign rights in part of the same geographical area where Chile asserts its sovereign rights

[280] However, Chile and Argentina, one such country, mutually recognized each other's rights to American Antarctica, between 25 and 90° West longitude, without delimiting their respective territories before signing the Antarctic Treaty

[281] In relation to territorial sovereignty rights, the Treaty establishes, in its Article IV, that none of its provisions may be interpreted as a waiver of territorial sovereignty rights or territorial claims in Antarctica previously asserted

[282] The Treaty protects the legal status of the Chilean Antarctic Territory, prohibits new claims of territorial sovereignty during its validity, and establishes an international regime that respects the positions of the parties on issues pertaining to sovereignty over that continent

[283] Thus, under the protection of the recognition enshrined in Article IV of the Antarctic Treaty, Chile irrevocably maintains its rights to territorial sovereignty in Antarctica

[284] Chile ratified the Antarctic Treaty on June

[285] Now that the 50th anniversary of the signing of this international instrument in 2009 has passed, the 32nd Antarctic Treaty Consultative Meeting was held in Baltimore and Washington DC, United States. The meeting established a special group to study matters related to the future of Antarctica, particularly challenges such as environmental protection and responsibility in the face of environmental emergencies, concerns about the increase in tourism, the development of scientific activity in general, climate change, bioprospecting, and safety in air and sea navigation

[286] Our country has a firm commitment to these matters

[287] As an original Consultative Member of the Treaty, Chile participates, with voting rights, in decision-making processes related to Antarctica and is willing to consider schemes for greater international oversight and cooperation that do not affect or undermine its sovereign rights

[288] Naturally, Chile's objective is to promote its national interests in that region of the world and fulfill its international obligations, strictly adhering to the various conventions, measures, and standards of the Antarctic System

[289] National Antarctic Policy The State of Chile has a national Antarctic policy that aims to achieve the following objectives: Protect Chile's Antarctic rights and strengthen them within the framework of the Treaty

[290] • Actively participate in the Antarctic system

[291] • Preserve the peace zone, scientific activities, and the ecological reserve. • Consolidate and modernize national Antarctic institutions. • Strengthen the participation of the Magallanes Region and Chilean Antarctica, in accordance with the administrative powers granted by national legislation, and promote the continental territory's capabilities to serve as a "bridge" to Antarctica. • Direct national Antarctic science to better connect with major trends in this field

[292] • Conserve marine living resources and develop southern fishing sustainably

[293] • Promote controlled tourism. • Encourage international cooperation

[294] In response to these objectives, in October 1991, Chile signed the Protocol on Environmental Protection to the Antarctic Treaty (Madrid Protocol) with the aim of strengthening the protection of the Antarctic environment and dependent and associated ecosystems

[295] Recognizing the importance of safeguarding the integrity of the ecosystem of the seas surrounding Antarctica, Chile had already signed the Convention on the Conservation of Antarctic Marine Living Resources in September 1980 in Canberra, Australia

[296] Chile, a bridge country to Antarctica Among the aspects considered by our Antarctic policy is Chile's capacity to be a bridge to Antarctica, being the closest country to this continent: about 700 km from the Chilean archipelago Diego Ramírez

[297] Due to this geographical position, Chile offers its support to the international community and enables the operations and activities of other countries and operators in Antarctica, in accordance with the principles of the Antarctic Treaty system and the common interests

[298] It must also ensure compliance in Chilean Antarctica with the standards and decisions adopted by the competent international institutions, such as the International Maritime Organization (IMO); the International Civil Aviation Organization (ICAO); the World Meteorological Organization (WMO); the International Oceanographic Commission (IOC); and the International Hydrographic Organization (IHO

[299] On the other hand, strengthening Chile's role as an Antarctic country imposes responsibilities, functions and rights linked to the concept called

[300] “Port State Control” in both the maritime and aeronautical spheres

[301] This concept gives rise to powers that Chile must exercise over activities and expeditions organized in the country, as well as those that use continental Chilean territory, primarily the Magallanes and Chilean Antarctic Region, as a means of access to the Antarctic continent

[302] Chilean Antarctic Bases A second crucial aspect of our national Antarctic policy is the presence of Chilean bases in the Chilean Antarctic Territory (chart

[303] It is fundamentally through these measures that our country can materialize its permanent presence and project its interests in the Antarctic continent, along with other measures, as well as fulfill its obligations to the international community

[304] Our bases in the Antarctic territory are the following: “Professor Julio Escudero” Scientific Base and other facilities of the Chilean Antarctic Institute (INACH

[305] • Arturo Prat Base, operated by the Navy since

[306] Open year-round, it was renovated in 2008 and is intended to serve as a scientific training ground and historical museum

[307] • O'Higgins Base, operated by the Army since

[308] Completely renovated in 2003, it is primarily intended to serve national scientific activities and territorial exploration activities

[309] President Eduardo Frei Montalva” Base, operated by the Air Force since 1969, on King George Island

[310] It is the most important air base in the Chilean Antarctic Territory, operating year-round

[311] This location houses the Regional Meteorological Center and the Chilean settlement "Villa Las Estrellas," which offers all the necessary services and is a symbol of Chile's sovereign presence in the Antarctic region

[312] NATIONAL BASES IN THE ANTARCTIC TERRITORY

[317] It has a significant Historical Museum and is used for scientific activities that take place in the summer

[318] • “Lieutenant Luis Carvajal” Air Base

[319] • “Arturo Parodi” Polar Station, operated by the Air Force

[320] It is the closest to the South Pole in the Chilean Antarctic Territory, along with the INACH “Antonio Hunneus Gana” Station, located in the same sector

[321] Among the maritime stations, the most notable are those of the Port of Fildes Bay, Covadonga Bay, Puerto Soberanía, and Puerto Paraíso, all of which provide support for the safety of human life at sea and fulfill functions of safeguarding the local marine environment

[322] Of these, Soberanía and Covadonga remain active year-round, being operated by the crews of the Arturo Prat Base and the O'Higgins Base

[323] The State of Chile has and operates its Antarctic bases and maritime stations through the Antarctic Operators of the Navy, the Army and the Air Force, as well as the Chilean Antarctic Institute

[324] BOX 7: CONTRIBUTION OF THE ARMED FORCES • Establishment of permanent bases in Antarctic territory

[325] Scientific and meteorological activities. Support for scientific activities carried out by other national and international organizations under the coordination of INACH

[326] • Exploration of the territory and projection toward the South Pole in the meteorological, glacial, geodetic, and hydrographic fields

[327] • Logistical support for Antarctic activities of other national and foreign organizations in accordance with official programs and in coordination with INACH regarding scientific activities

[328] • Humanitarian air assistance and evacuation. • Removal of contamination and waste from national and foreign bases

[329] • Safeguarding human life at sea, search and rescue. • Protecting the marine environment and emergency rescue through the Combined Naval Antarctic Patrol, a task carried out jointly with the Argentine Navy

[330] In this capacity, the Armed Forces institutions share the challenges posed by our permanent presence in Antarctica, the fulfillment of the obligations and commitments arising from the Antarctic Treaty, and particularly those future challenges related to the environment, the protection of human life, climate change, and the impact of tourism activities (Box

[331] For their activities, the Chilean armed forces require maintaining and developing up-to-date capabilities

[332] Armed Forces: Development and Application of Antarctic Capabilities In accordance with the above, the defense policy defines the development and use of specific Antarctic capabilities by the Armed Forces

[333] In general terms, in order to maintain its influence in the Antarctic system and to project the objectives that guide its Antarctic policy, Chile needs to effectively express its substantial commitment to the Antarctic Treaty system and the value it places on the Antarctic territory from the perspective of the Chilean State and its comprehensive development

[334] This commitment covers a series of activities and the capabilities associated with their implementation, which include, among others, the following: The ability to maintain a permanent presence, including wintering, on the Antarctic continent and its islands

[335] • The ability to ensure the conservation of Antarctic resources and prevent their illegal use by third countries or private individuals

[336] • The ability to coordinate and carry out a process of gradual integration of the work of all national Antarctic operators, strengthening the scope of the Antarctic Policy Council

[337] • The ability to conduct scientific research anywhere in the Chilean Antarctic Territory, at least during the summer

[338] • The ability to maintain a level of oceanographic research and observation in the Southern Ocean

[339] • The ability to conduct oceanographic studies aimed at collecting recognized scientific information on the continental shelf in Chilean Antarctica

[340] • The ability to manage and improve search and rescue systems in areas of responsibility and establish the necessary alliances with the southern countries that share those responsibilities

[341] • The ability to meet international obligations to monitor the atmosphere and oceans in Antarctic areas

[342] • The ability to develop, maintain and publish Antarctic cartography

[343] • The capacity to monitor and protect Antarctic natural and cultural heritage, including historic sites and monuments, protected and managed areas, and vulnerable terrestrial and marine ecosystems

[344] These tasks, which arise from compliance with national Antarctic policy and international agreements, require the Armed Forces to have specific institutional capabilities that prioritize strategic transportation and communications connections, links with the Chilean mainland, and deep land, sea, and air exploration toward the high inland plains or the less-explored coasts or maritime spaces of the Chilean Antarctic Territory

[345] In addition to the above, there are capabilities to cooperate in maritime and air security, as well as international cooperation in high-stakes joint efforts and projects

[346] Antarctica and Defense The effects of the geostrategic position of Antarctica, its essential geographical condition, which implies a direct relationship with the southern maritime passages and transpolar air routes, the existence of the largest reserve of water and renewable and non-renewable resources, its usefulness in the use of and contact with satellite observation, its role in the study of global environmental issues, in addition to the position of the Antarctic Peninsula as a fundamental reference point for the measurement and forecasting of climate change, are primary considerations that guide the goals and objectives of national Antarctic policy

[347] In this context, preserving the scope of the Antarctic Treaty as a demilitarized and denuclearized area is a priority for Chile

[348] Consequently, and in compliance with the provisions of the Antarctic Treaty, Chile does not carry out military activities of any kind and considers Antarctica to be exclusively for peaceful purposes, without prejudice to the fact that its Antarctic operators are military institutions, along with the Chilean Antarctic Institute

[349] Similarly, Chile complies with the prohibition on nuclear activity, both in terms of explosions and the disposal of radioactive waste in Antarctica

[350] Maintaining a demilitarized and denuclearized Antarctica is a priority for Chile's defense policy, both because of the principles that inspire the Chilean State's position regarding that continent and because it removes the possibility of an international conflict affecting southern communication routes from the rest of continental Chile

[351] In this way, Chile contributes to the goal of world peace enshrined in the Antarctic Treaty

[352] The development of maritime control capabilities for peaceful purposes in the adjacent maritime zone is, consequently, one of the tasks that the Armed Forces carry out to benefit the purposes of the Antarctic Treaty and the system derived from it

[353] Strategic Analysis of the National Territory The national territory has a close relationship with national defense: first, because its protection is one of the purposes of defense; second, because its shape and its particular geographical features determine strategic defense solutions

[354] Strategic Assessment Factors of the National Territory. a. The shape of the territory

[355] The fact that the continental territory is, for a considerable part, a long and narrow strip of land, and that, in the southern part, it has a large presence of islands and inland channels, is one of the most important factors to consider

[356] First, because it forces us to choose where to concentrate defensive efforts, advising the organization, on land, of operational nuclei that obey a centralized command, but that can act in a decentralized manner

[357] Second, because the need to allocate human and material resources increases as the distances between extreme regions make strategic maneuvers difficult

[358] Third, because the narrowness of the territory translates into a lack of transversal strategic depth

[359] And fourth, because it poses serious challenges to land communications routes, affecting operational and logistical transport via this route, which requires a significant portion of strategic transport to be carried out by air or sea

[360] At the same time, the longitudinal extension of our continental territory imposes serious logistical demands and the need for ground infrastructure to support air operations

[361] b. The rugged morphology of the national territory This feature restricts the use of detection elements located on the Earth’s surface and requires technological solutions that, however, may always present limitations in the face of a very demanding geography

[362] c. The breadth of maritime spaces This requires a great effort to safeguard sovereignty, exercise the corresponding jurisdiction, provide protection to human life at sea, protect marine resources, and protect the navigation of vessels whose cargo is vital to the country in conflict situations

[363] The breadth of aeronautical control airspace This characteristic advises the use of satellites that allow surveillance and control, and that facilitate navigation and communications

[364] e. The difficulty of access to extreme areas In addition to the great distances between the north (Arica and Parinacota Region to the Atacama Region) and the southern zone (Aysén Region to the Magallanes and Chilean Antarctic Region) with the center of the country, in the case of the southern zone, there is also its disconnection by land

[365] This requires securing maritime and air traffic as a permanent strategic imperative

[366] f. The difficulty of operating in extreme zones On the one hand, the north is home to the driest desert in the world and, on the other, the southern zone is a subantarctic and archipelagic region

[367] g. Island territory in a vast maritime space Island areas far removed from the continental coast (such as Easter Island or the Juan Fernández archipelago) constitute relevant strategic positions and, therefore, require an onsite presence and a considerable effort of logistical support from the State to adequately attend to and maintain the quality of life of the resident populations

[368] h. Bi-oceanic maritime passages The strategic importance of bi-oceanic maritime passages in the southern zone constitutes a challenge that must be considered in the face of any evolving situation that affects national security, since it is related to the strategic deployment of naval forces

[369] i. The difference in terrain and climate This feature requires that the training and acclimatization of personnel and material be different in the different regions of the country

[370] This entails logistical difficulties, as it requires maintaining equipment and materials that must adapt to different climate and altitude conditions

[371] The above represents an accurate forecast regarding personnel and materials

[372] Furthermore, harsh weather conditions hamper land and air communications, especially in the southern and austral regions

[373] In particular, these extreme conditions restrict the use of air weapons in some areas of the country

[374] This requires state-of-the-art equipment, both on land and on board, to ensure operation under any conditions

[375] j. The distribution of economic activity The vital resources for the life and existence of Chile are found throughout the national territory, both land and sea

[376] In the case of continental land area, the different distribution of these resources influences the creation of three strategically important geographic zones that characterize the national territory

[377] Focusing on the aforementioned assessment factors, Chile's territory is characterized by presenting very complex challenges for its defense

[378] The geographical setting is one of the most significant aspects for the strategic management of forces, because the unique geography of our country influences, favorably or unfavorably, any conception of their employment

[379] The geographical characteristics make it imperative to have permanent Armed Forces resources in the country's remote areas

[380] The importance of strategic zones requires that the ground force to obey a functional structure, at an optimal operational level, projectable, flexible, and powerful

[381] Furthermore, it is still necessary to emphasize maritime and air transport for logistical support and territorial connectivity, making coastal and overseas maritime communications routes essential for the country's survival in the event of a conflict

[382] The railway and road lines that extend longitudinally across the country provide greater flexibility for land transportation, but the national road network is conditioned by the geographical features of our territory

[383] The aforementioned conditions, along with the climatic and relief characteristics of the different areas of the national territory, as well as the vast maritime and airspace to be monitored and protected, require specific, different, and simultaneous ideas and approaches to solutions in each of the likely theaters of operations

[384] Each of these requires units with adequate personnel, equipment, and training, based on the specific geographic characteristics of each area

[385] In most cases, the forces present, in addition to fulfilling their military mission, constitute a valuable contribution to national development, especially in island or sparsely populated areas, considered isolated zones

[386] All these conditions must be taken into account when defining the structure, organization, deployment, equipment, instruction, and training of the Armed Forces institutions, so that they can effectively fulfill their fundamental mission

[387] Territorial Order Policies The concept of sovereignty assumes the existence of a territory as a physical space, circumscribed by boundaries whose scope is also geospatial, as well as the capacity to assert the exclusive jurisdiction of the State over it in accordance with international law

[388] This idea of exclusivity allows the government's administrative management to be oriented according to its policies and objectives, with the defense sector playing an important role in policies aimed at developing those areas of the country that, due to their characteristics and location, require special support from the State

[389] Border Zone Policy From a defense perspective, border zones are particularly relevant because they are the site of a convergence of special historical and population aspects, and because they require effective and tangible attention through the exercise of State sovereignty

[390] This perspective complements the development of these areas, integrating them into the rest of the country and valuing their capacity to interact with their neighbors

[391] Chile has defined its border zone considering special characteristics, generally linked to adjacency to international boundaries and the dominant characteristics of isolation or, in certain cases, where international treaties with permanent effects apply, subjecting it to specific legislation that provides for effective coordination between the Ministry of Foreign Affairs and the Ministry of National Defense

[392] Special decision-making mechanisms operate in this area and guide State action regarding fiscal real estate, water resources, natural resources, scientific and mountaineering expeditions, and the application of legal provisions for extreme zones

[393] Furthermore, with neighboring countries, the Integration and Border Committees have been one of the effective tools created to establish channels that bring vitality to border areas, facilitate the cross-border flow of people and goods, and foster normal, active, and impactful relations with citizens

[394] Countries can find through them an effective means of boosting the development of national communities located in these geographic areas, so that residents have new opportunities for well-being in their daily lives in areas that have traditionally been less fortunate

[395] In addition, the development of bioceanic corridors through land and other links between the various production centers and the Pacific and Atlantic ports constitutes a tool for intensifying human and economic exchanges

[396] Currently, the concept of "Integration and Development Axes" implies a comprehensive approach to improving access, achieving connectivity, and developing productive and service potential in the aforementioned geographic areas

[397] Chile shares this vision, which aims to achieve greater levels of integration, alongside all South American states, by valuing the relationship that exists between border areas under different sovereignties and populated by communities that, in turn, generate mutually influential economic activities

[398] Hence, the integrative processes in these areas have deepened to include economic, cultural, and political aspects in the 21st century

[399] It should be emphasized that integration, which normally begins with defined and functional objectives, can evolve into deeper levels of economic and even political interrelation, to the extent that the states participating in the integration process accept each other's national identities and mutually recognize each other's territory

[400] Development Policy for Isolated and Extreme Zones The low population density in certain regions of Chile, and a relatively slower development in them compared to other areas of the country, has been one of the characteristics of the isolated and extreme zones of our continental territory, a situation that constitutes a challenge to which national defense is not immune

[401] Under these conditions, the Chilean State has taken care to implement special plans, programs, and projects aimed at reinserting remote and isolated areas into national economic development

[402] This requires developing concrete measures to boost economic and productive activity in these areas, improve their connectivity, improve the provision of public services while respecting cultural heritage, and serve as an expression of appropriate migration policies

[403] Among the plans promoted are the Palena Plan, the Chiloé Plan, the Aysén Emergency Plan, and the Arica-Parinacota Plan

[404] These plans are being implemented by the Interministerial Committee for the Development of Extreme Zones (CIDEZE) of the Undersecretariat of Regional Development of the Ministry of the Interior

[405] The presence of Armed Forces units in these isolated areas, far from vital centers, creates a magnet for human life

[406] The various armed forces have cooperated in the development and flexibility of our country's road system, in territorial consolidation, and in connecting continental, island, and Antarctic territories

[407] Maritime Territory Policy

[408] The Navy is the State institution that houses the Maritime Authority. It contributes to its administration, performs maritime police duties, supports and controls navigation, oversees fishing and sporting activities, monitors the aquatic environment, and works in coordination with other public bodies with sectoral responsibilities, both in the activities and resources of the sea, rivers, and lakes

[409] The execution of the traditional defense functions carried out by the Navy and those carried out by the Maritime Authority, under a single superior command, has been enormously effective for Chile and has allowed the State to ensure proper administration of this territory

[410] a. Maritime interests Maritime interests correspond to national development activities that generate political, strategic, economic, scientific and social benefits for the country

[411] They are related to the exploitation, enjoyment and use of the resources or benefits provided by the sea, the seabed and the subsoil that are under the maritime jurisdiction of a State

[412] They develop in the high seas, the coastal zone and inland waters

[413] Chile's maritime interests include some specific objectives that the Navy, with its resources, contributes to meeting

[414] For example: • Ensure the most efficient use of the natural resources of the national oceanic territory

[415] Develop marine science and technology and all activities that support and sustain the use, exploitation, conservation, and management of the natural resources of the national oceanic territory. Improve the legal framework and strengthen the bodies that administer said territory and maritime activities, within the scope of national and international law. Protect and preserve the marine environment in the national oceanic territory and ensure that the activities of other States in the oceans do not affect Chile's interests or the quality of life of its inhabitants

[416] • Provide effective security to the national oceanic territory, maritime interests, and vital ocean routes for the country, considering the possible occurrence of threats, illegal acts, or aggressions that harm the jurisdiction and sovereignty of the State, and taking appropriate precautions to neutralize them. • Incentivize and develop the naval industry in Chile, leveraging technology and installed capacity in both private and state shipyards, and the active participation of the Navy's Shipyards and Workshops (ASMAR

[417] It is part of the country's maritime interests to use the sea route to project itself into the Asia-Pacific

[418] Furthermore, 90 percent of cargo entering or leaving the country is transported by sea, requiring constant support for this activity from defense institutions

[419] Our port network allows us to meet these demands and those of the adjacent inland region, including the Southern Cone, which finds a link for its products to the Pacific markets

[420] Likewise, the preservation of fish resources in national maritime spaces is of great importance to our economy

[421] This activity, as well as the development of aquaculture in inland waters, makes it essential to maintain the quality and purity of the environment

[422] At the same time, the potential for producing clean, renewable, and non-traditional energy that our seas can provide requires short-term research and experimentation for a country like ours, lacking oil and gas

[423] For the management of activities within the national maritime territory, there are several specific laws that regulate the actions of nationals and foreigners, as well as several international treaties signed by the State that create obligations for the authorities and users of this territory

[424] In the maritime zone where Chile has responsibilities to the international community, which exceeds 30,000,000 km2 measured from the 131° W meridian, our country implements the commitments assumed in a set of international instruments

[425] In this context, Chile has been a party to the International Agreement on the Safety of Life at Sea since 1929; it has had specific maritime rescue and salvage obligations in the southeastern Pacific quadrant since 1953; it has contributed to the General Bathymetric Chart of the Oceans since 1962; it has been the coordinator of the Southeastern Pacific Maritime Area for the International Hydrographic Office since 1987 and for Maritime Traffic Control since 1989; and it is an active member of the Pacific Tsunami Warning Network

[426] b. The national maritime strategy It is the State's strategy in relation to the sea and guides the role that the Navy plays in it

[427] In general terms, it directs the application of national potential, both in times of peace and in situations of crisis or war, to ensure the safe, free, and interference-free use of the sea for its own benefit in accordance with national legislation and international conventions

[428] It is essentially integrative and has the important characteristic of being national, since its implementation requires the support and cooperation of the other branches of the Armed Forces, as well as other state organizations

[429] At the same time, it cooperates with the State's efforts to preserve, enhance, and promote national maritime interests

[430] Aerospace Policy The State of Chile recognizes the need to have a realistic and uniform vision of national airspace expressed in a national aeronautical policy, since this constitutes a decisive factor for the country

[431] Aeronautical activity contributes to its economic and social development

[432] Its structure is complemented by the “open skies policy,” making our country a pioneer in this regard

[433] Our airspace is flexible and dynamic, where general and civil-commercial aviation activities complement military aviation activities on a daily basis, without interference and with great fluidity, which has allowed us to harmoniously enjoy the sovereign use of national airspace

[434] For this reason, the system is said to be mixed, as the military and civilian sectors coexist in coordination, creating synergies in its operation, maintenance, and development. It also efficiently utilizes the resources the country makes available to the National Aeronautical System (SAN), making full use of its infrastructure and capabilities

[435] The military segment of airspace use is the responsibility of the Chilean Air Force, while the civil segment is the responsibility of the General Directorate of Civil Aeronautics

[436] Both institutions coordinate, through multiple mechanisms, the flexible and permanent use of our jurisdictional space, with the sole purpose of operating the aeronautical system and safeguarding operational surveillance and safety

[437] This national multi-system aeronautical system is supported by the Chilean Air Force through the Search and Rescue Service (SAR), an agency responsible for the entire area defined above

[438] In 1996, airport concessions began, meeting the growing demand of the air transport industry and bringing Chile, as a whole, closer to the major development centers of today's world

[439] The structure of airspace complements the needs for greater connectivity and safety in aviation, and is structured in such a way that its flexible use allows for the harmonious coexistence of all actors operating within it

[440] In recent years, aeronautical activity has incorporated a new dimension: space

[441] Thus, all efforts have been consolidated into a "Chilean aerospace system," which integrates state policies, government leadership, the Ministries of Defense, Public Works, Transport, and Telecommunications, the Chilean Air Force, and the General Directorate of Civil Aeronautics (DGAC). It also includes public and private entities and individuals specifically involved in the use and exploitation of air resources and their use in the fields of national development and defense

[442] The aerospace system is a social asset that integrates aeronautical, space, and human capabilities, associated ground-based infrastructure, fiscal agencies, and specific policies or regulations designed and developed for the effective and efficient use of its components

[443] Air traffic is governed by national regulations, particularly the Aeronautical Code, Law No. 16,752, the Aeronautical Regulations, and the Aeronautical Standard

[444] The Chilean State assumes responsibility for the operational safety of air activities, encompassing not only the safety and security of flights, but also the safety and security of people and property moving through our airspace and airport terminals

[445] The "Category I" status granted to Chile by the Federal Aviation Administration (FAA) of the United States, and the international recognition of the DGAC's leadership in modernization and management, constitute a national asset that must be protected, sustained, and projected over time

[446] Budgetary balance and efficient financial management within a self-financing system are a benchmark that ICAO has recognized as a model to follow in the administration of other aeronautical systems

[447] The DGAC has developed a series of aspects related to airspace, which is currently in the midst of developing a fourth-generation structure, applying satellite technology to its infrastructure and design of airways and approach procedures for landing and takeoff

[448] This means planes can fly more precise, direct, and safer routes in shorter flight times, saving thousands of tons of fuel and mitigating greenhouse gas pollution

[449] Aeronautical activity has kept pace with global trends that speak of increasing concern for environmental protection

[450] Some of the criteria that have been adopted in recent years in this area are reflected in measures aimed at reducing noise levels, using less polluting engines, and using biodegradable materials in aircraft construction

[451] Space Policy Space technology in Chile has become a tool used by various organizations, universities, research centers, and companies

[452] However, our country is the sole user of this technology and, therefore, depends on other states for information, services, and satellite images

[453] Hence the imperative to participate with our own capabilities in space activity

[454] Reference Framework In the international sphere, the criteria that guide Chile's space policy are: • Defend the principle that outer space is the common heritage of humanity, as established by current international legislation ratified by our country

[455] • Promote the peaceful use of outer space to strengthen international peace. • Protect national sovereignty and security against the potential misuse of space technology by other nations

[456] Promote international cooperation and integration with the Latin American region in space activities

[457] At the national level, the criteria guiding our space policy consider that, in the area of security and defense, the use of space will be in accordance with current international standards, considering the use of space technology in the context of the peaceful use of outer space, promoting cooperation in joint projects between civilian and military entities

[458] b. Guidelines Priority will be given to the development of capabilities in the following areas specific to a space system: • Space elements

[459] Manufacturing of satellites, payloads, and remote sensing, telecommunications, and scientific equipment or instruments. • Space infrastructure

[460] In this field, emphasis will be placed on the development of specialized manufacturing, testing, and integration laboratories, as well as control and tracking centers

[461] There are no plans to build launch centers

[462] The development of rockets, vectors, space transport vehicles or other vehicles intended to transport space elements is not planned

[463] • Information systems

[464] A National Center for the reception, storage, processing, and distribution of spatial data will be established to avoid duplication in its acquisition and achieve national efficiency in the management of this resource

[465] Space activities

[466] They will primarily focus on building a critical mass of scientists, professionals, technicians, and specialists that will enable the operation of the aforementioned system elements

[467] Within this general framework, and with the stated purpose of having its own capabilities in space activity, Decree (Av) No. 338 of 2001 created the Chilean Space Agency, dependent on the Undersecretariat of Aviation

[468] The subsequent Decree (Av) No. 144 of 2008 changed the dependency of the aforementioned Agency to the Undersecretariat of Economy and modified the national space policy as follows: c. General objectives • Encourage, coordinate and regulate the use of space technology to increase productive and service capacities that ensure the development of the country

[469] • Promote cooperation and foster international, regional, and neighborhood integration in space matters. • Contribute to the strategic needs of national security and defense. • Lay the groundwork for the creation of a coordinating and regulatory body that represents space technology in Chile and before international organizations

[470] Guide the design and updating of the spatial development plan that responds to intersectoral national requirements

[471] d. Specific objectives related to defense • Maintain coherence and relevance of advances in space science and technology with national and sectoral development as well as with national security and defense plans

[472] • Promote and coordinate the participation of national organizations and institutions in the field of space science and technology, encouraging the development of national professionals in space disciplines. • Promote and coordinate scientific and technological exchange among national institutions, public and private, civil and military, facilitating the expeditious flow of information on space science and technology

[473] In a new effort to develop its space policy, Chile decided to promote an Earth Observation Satellite System (SSOT) consisting of the co-manufacturing of a satellite and contracting its launch into orbit

[474] The purpose of the SSOT is to provide the capability to implement a strategic project focused on territorial observation with a broad range of objectives, such as geological prospecting, ecosystem monitoring, disaster prevention and mitigation, and issues of interest to the country's security

[475] According to the program, the SSOT must be available within two to three and a half years of the contract being signed, which occurred in

[476] Book of National Defense of Chile 2010 THE STATE OF CHILE Chapter The population of the country, with the features that distinguish it, as well as the national territory and the institutions of the Republic, is the object of national defense

[477] The State's responsibility to protect the elements that are vital to it, including the sovereignty to make decisions that affect the country's destiny, has as one of its effects the need to make known what we are, who we are, where we stand, and what we want

[478] This constitutes one of the requirements for establishing the foundations of what we intend to defend. III

[479] Generalities In the process of evolution of a population into a nation, prevailing traits of unitary sense, collective consciousness and conviction of belonging to a community are acquired, which will distinguish it from other nations

[480] Normally, in this same process, the nation is politically organized into a State to which it assigns basic functions for its development: among them, the function of defense

[481] In this sense, one of the reasons for defense is precisely to protect the population

[482] All defense is ultimately based on the most basic components of the nation-state: state, territory, and population

[483] These pieces—collected or expressed, in our case, in the principles and objectives of the State described in the Political Constitution of the Republic—correspond to elements that make up part of the assets and purposes that Chile is willing to protect, because they are what animate its being and give it identity among nations

[484] THE POPULATION OF CHILE 69 Book of the National Defense of Chile 2010 THE STATE OF CHILE Chapter Chilean society is the specific result of a combination of ethnic groups; all of them have provided wealth, in the process of miscegenation, to the population of Chile

[485] National identity exhibits factors of continuity and change based on different narratives that combine, on the one hand, the appreciation of inherited traditions and historical events and, on the other, openness to external influences, transformations, and legitimate aspirations regarding the realities of the present, as well as visions of collective projects for the future

[486] However, national identity is conceived as an inclusive process in which the sensibilities of all social groups interact, thus expressing a diverse and plural cultural environment, yet compatible and united in its basic components

[487] It is the responsibility of the State's institutions to promote the conditions for the free development of these cultures, but at the same time, and without contradiction, to encourage the enrichment of the specific factors of national identity

[488] Contributions of Defense to National Identity The effects associated with military activity in the different periods of our history constitute an element that has influenced the described process of formation of national identity

[489] Historians, sociologists, anthropologists, and scholars of Chilean society in general agree that the conflict, beyond the human tragedy it represents, explains some of the features that contribute to Chile's formation as a unique national unit

[490] Additionally, in the long process of shaping the Chilean nation, defense institutions—and the Armed Forces, in particular—have contributed in various ways to fostering social cohesion as an essential foundation for the permanence of our nationality

[491] It can be said that the Armed Forces find a double meaning in their existence as such

[492] They formally emerge as organizations with a national sense prepared to assume the defense of the Chilean people, its territory and its sovereignty expressed in the national State, 70 Book of the National Defense of Chile 2010 THE STATE OF CHILE Chapter but, at the same time, they emerge as institutions associated with the values on which this defense is based

[493] In this sense, from their specific and main functions, they generate the external security conditions indispensable for the life of the nation organized in society, making, at the same time, contributions to its development and to the conservation and enrichment of its cultural heritage

[494] Data and Background Both the number of inhabitants and the different aspects of population distribution are relevant from a defense perspective, given the determinations they impose on the use of human resources in the provisions that the country must make to maintain its external security

[495] Population Growth Population indices have shown a gradual decline in the growth rate over the last twenty years, a decline that will continue for the coming decade

[496] Between 1990 and 2000, the national population reached 15,397,784 inhabitants, with a growth rate of 1.3%. The estimate for the decade 2000-2010 is 17,094,275 inhabitants, representing a growth rate of 0.97%

[497] The projection for the decade 2010-2020 is 18,549,095, with a rate of 0.74%

[498] Distribution by Sex The distribution of the population by sex shows relative homogeneity between men and women, a proportion that projected to 2010 is equivalent to 49% and 51%, respectively

[499] This circumstance is not uniform across all age groups; particularly in the 20-49 age group, the relationship is the opposite (51% and 49%, respectively

[500] National Institute of Statistics (INE), “Chile: Population Projections and Estimates, 1990-2020.” Santiago, August

[501] Chapter Distribution by Age Groups The study of population indicators anticipates the aging of the population as a general trend, drawing a scenario in which both the decline in crude birth rates and the infant mortality rate predominate

[502] Likewise, overall mortality rates and a sustained increase in life expectancy at birth remain constant

[503] Population distribution According to this criterion, the population distribution shows relative stability over time, with an approximate proportion of 86% urban and 14% rural; this proportion tends to remain the same when projected to 2020, although with a slight decrease in the rural population

[504] On the other hand, of the total national population, 40.1% is concentrated in the Metropolitan Region (Santiago), 12.3% in the Biobío Region, and 10.2% in the Valparaíso Region

[505] Population density The country's overall population density is 19.9 inhabitants per square kilometer

[506] However, this general ratio is diminished in the north; in fact, the Arica and Parinacota Region has 11.2 inhabitants/ and the Tarapacá Region has 5.7 inhabitants

[507] Similarly, in the southern region, the Aysén Region of General Carlos Ibáñez del Campo has only 0.8 inhabitants/km2 and the Magallanes Region (continental) 1.1 inhabitants/km

[508] National Migration Policy As a result of the work carried out by the Commission on Migration Policy, established within the Ministry of the Interior, Chile has had a national migration policy since 2008 that aims to address Chile's current reality as a host country for significant numbers of immigrants

[509] This policy, formulated from the perspective of citizens' rights, adheres to the principles outlined in Box

[510] Chilean National Defense Book 2010 THE STATE OF CHILE Chapter Guidelines for State Action • Chile, host country: Chile is a country open to immigration on the basis of non-discriminatory reception and applies the international treaties governing the matter, of which it is a party. • Integration of immigrants: Chile positively accepts migrants into society, respecting their cultural specificity, but also encouraging foreigners to integrate into various areas of our society

[511] BOX 8: PRINCIPLES OF NATIONAL MIGRATION POLICY • Residence and freedom of movement • Freedom of thought and conscience • Access to residence under equal and duly informed conditions • Access to justice • Integration and social protection of immigrants • Respect for the labor rights of foreign workers in Chile • Non-discrimination • Regularity of migratory flows • Family reunification • Citizen participation in migration management • International treatment of migration issues: Chile promotes the generation of agreements between countries of origin, transit and destination, of a bilateral and multilateral nature, that allow the administration of the migration phenomenon, considering the interests of immigrants, but also of the countries involved

[512] • Regulatory and administrative capacity: Chile does not waive its right and duty to develop legal frameworks to guide access to residency in its territory, when necessary for the benefit of the State and its population

[513] Promotion of Safe Migration Actions will be promoted to prevent and punish both illicit trafficking of migrants and trafficking in persons

[514] Furthermore, when negative effects derived from the migration phenomenon could generate internal tensions or crises at the bilateral or international level, the State may, through legal means, safeguard the security risks that could be associated with migration

[515] Conditions for Asylum Seekers and Refugees Current immigration legislation in Chile equates, to a certain extent, the concepts of “refuge” (a category of temporary residence) with any other category of residence

[516] In light of the above, it will be the responsibility of the relevant government agencies to develop formulas that allow them to adequately respond to the special conditions of vulnerability experienced by people seeking asylum in Chile

[517] Intersectoral Commitments The Migration Policy Council will call on national public services to sign agreement and coordination protocols for the fulfillment of the commitments established in the implementation strategy of this policy

[518] Impact of Immigration Compared to other countries in the region, Chile has a relatively low number of immigrants, despite the fact that an increasing number of foreign citizens have settled in the country over the last two decades

[519] Chart 6 shows that the percentage of immigrants in Chile's total population has never exceeded 5%
