[529] Part Chapter IV

[530] INTERNATIONAL CONFLICT. ARMED CONFLICT LIMITATION REGIMES

[531] ARMS LIMITATION REGIME The world is more interconnected and interdependent than ever before

[532] To a large extent, our country's development depends on opportunities that only thrive in conditions of international peace and stability, and likewise, our security depends on problems that arise in distant environments but radiate to us

[533] For this reason, the Ministry of Defense strongly supports Chile's foreign policy, which is based on the pursuit of a legally regulated world order and, consequently, on the commitment of all states, including our own, to the obligations arising from international law and the promotion of peace and security among nations

[534] However, given the dynamic nature of international environments, uncertainty remains a component of the conditions under which the world evolves

[535] Similarly, armed conflict, in all its forms, remains present in the world

[536] To limit or at least mitigate its effects, the community of nations has been perfecting, expanding, or creating various international regimes, particularly the rules governing the use of force and reducing the type and quantity of weapons, both conventional and weapons of mass destruction

[537] The features of modern international conflict and the contents of the main regimes that limit it are addressed in this section

[538] Book of National Defense of Chile 2010 THE INTERNATIONAL FRAMEWORK OF DEFENSE IV

[539] International Conflict and its Types In general, conflict between States assumes one of its two basic types, crisis or war, although it is perfectly possible for the first to lead to the second without interruption

[540] The absence of cooperation between States does not necessarily generate a relationship of conflict

[541] When a conflict situation arises, it is the community of nations that makes the decision to cooperate, especially through the United Nations system, to maintain or restore peace between the parties, using the various measures established in the founding Charter of that organization—primarily sanctions and military operations—or even on the basis of multinational organizations with a regional scope

[542] An international conflict is a relationship of conflicting interests between international actors, at least one of which is a State, and which is intentionally oriented towards imposing the will of one of the parties against the resistance of another

[543] It can arise from a variety of factors: historical, ethnic, social, religious, economic or ideological

[544] It is, then, geopolitical reasons, economic interests or political actions that most frequently produce an international conflict, even when the apparent or circumstantial cause may be different

[545] It will be an "armed" conflict when, in the relationship of opposing interests, military force is used in a violent manner to carry out one's own will

[546] Crisis A crisis is a conflict of limited intensity that seeks to alter the status quo and involves different actors in the international system

[547] It involves achieving certain objectives through pressure or negotiations without resorting to confrontation or widespread use of force, so it rarely arises spontaneously

[548] THE INTERNATIONAL CONFLICT THE INTERNATIONAL FRAMEWORK OF DEFENSE From a political perspective, the crisis can be motivated by causes of various kinds

[549] The factors that trigger it can be found in the sphere of relations between states, but they can also originate within a country and manifest themselves as an international crisis when the situation triggers intervention by third parties

[550] It is important to emphasize that not all of the causes that produce it are necessarily due to the control of the actors involved

[551] Hence the difficulty of crisis management

[552] However, it may be the case that some actors believe they will gain benefits or achieve objectives they value by steering their relationships with other actors toward a crisis situation

[553] In this sense, history records actions by states that have deliberately triggered crisis situations, using them as a political instrument to achieve their objectives

[554] History also teaches us that such conduct can only be successful if it does not lead to war, that is, if the interests at stake are not of vital importance to the challenged actor or actors, so that, faced with implicit or potential punishment, the latter resign themselves to yielding to the pressures or demands they receive

[555] Similarly, historical evidence shows that the direct involvement of the political leader is crucial to the success of the maneuver

[556] The use of force must always be anticipated in a crisis situation and its management, even though the use of force is not the defining condition of such a situation

[557] Similarly, there will always be the risk that, for various reasons, the crisis cannot be reversed and escalates into the widespread use of military force

[558] Indeed, one of the most important features of the crisis is the degree of uncertainty it produces, regarding its effects, outcomes, end, or escalation

[559] The use of the concept of crisis, with all its complexity, and the associated management methodology, requires a coherent and unified conceptual approach at the political and strategic levels of defense management

[560] Book of National Defense of Chile 2010 THE INTERNATIONAL FRAMEWORK OF DEFENSE War War is an armed conflict in which the vital interests of a country are threatened

[561] For this reason, it is the most extreme or severe expression of the conflict and involves the action of the country's entire potential

[562] War does not arise suddenly, but is usually preceded by the escalation of a crisis

[563] A lack of agreement between the parties in a crisis situation or the different perceptions they may have regarding the adversary's intention to use military force as a last resort can lead to the widespread use of force to achieve the intended ends

[564] In war, the use of military force becomes relevant as a central element of a country's war effort once hostilities have begun

[565] Ultimately, the purpose of one's own military force is none other than to break the will of the opposing forces to fight

[566] In its contemporary form, war manifests itself with features that differentiate it from previous periods: There has been an evolution from total war to limited war, both in terms of objectives pursued and the setting, duration and capabilities involved

[567] Strategic solutions have been consolidated that prioritize the joint and flexible use of military resources

[568] Non-state armed actors have emerged with the capacity to challenge the State

[569] The Dynamics of Armed Conflict States, the basic political unit of international relations, interact in an international system in whose regulation humanity has been gradually advancing

[570] However, conflict remains one of the elements that creates tension in the global environment, and therefore it is essential to understand the basic factors involved in its emergence and development, and how they interact

[571] THE INTERNATIONAL FRAMEWORK OF DEFENSE The Scenario In this context, it is not about the physical space where military operations occur or may occur

[572] Rather, it refers to events that may possibly occur and are constructed from a set of hypotheses guided by eventual events involving contexts, determining forces, and fundamental relationships between international actors, without any certainty that such hypotheses will materialize in the imagined manner

[573] It is concluded that, in the field of competition, a scenario can be configured, particularly in relation to a status quo situation judged by the person proposing it as detrimental to their own interests

[574] In this sense, the scenario is conceived with objectives to be achieved that have been previously analyzed and weighed, although there is no guarantee that they will be achieved, in part or in full, as planned

[575] Scenarios, as possible events, offer an important analytical model that can guide strategic planning tasks, the design of action plans, and the range of tactical deployments that can be anticipated

[576] Risk is the probability of receiving a certain damage in some area or aspect of interest

[577] Risks can be reduced or increased and therefore can be managed based on the realization of a desired scenario

[578] That is, there may be risk management or an action plan

[579] A risk is perceived as a threat when a potential adversary has the ability to manage the risk for its own purposes in a given scenario, a specific incident occurs that concretizes it as a threat, and at the same time, there is little or no reaction capacity on the part of the challenged party

[580] In this sense, a risk is not a threat, since it can exist without becoming a threat

[581] Risk hypotheses enrich conflict and scenario analysis and can be useful in helping prevent a situation from taking an undesirable course and in correcting inherent characteristics that increase risk or transform it into a threat

[582] THE INTERNATIONAL DEFENSE FRAMEWORK For this reason, an important independent variable to identify and manage risks are vulnerabilities

[583] Defined for these purposes as the inherent deficiencies that expose the State to a danger that could be exploited by another, they may be structural or circumstantial in nature, and not necessarily military, so overcoming them may involve action by different sectors of the State administration

[584] The Threat The relative geographical position occupied by States influences their mutual relations and their evolution

[585] Typically, in their relations, States incorporate perceptions and realities based on their respective relative geographical positions

[586] These perceptions and realities may crystallize as threats and persist for a long time, ushering in a prolonged cycle of relationships dominated by competition and even the possibility of conflict

[587] However, the threat that thus emerges does not crystallize once and for all, no matter how long the competitive cycle is

[588] From a strategic perspective, this way of conceiving the threat has heuristic and practical advantages

[589] The concept of “conflict hypothesis” has been used universally in planning processes

[590] However, the concept understood in this way also tends to generate a rigid and repetitive analysis of the strategic horizon, which foreign and defense policy should, instead, be able to shape more effectively

[591] Adopting a more flexible understanding of threat can also be useful for functions integrated into the planning process

[592] In this sense, a threat can be conceived as an action taken or an event provoked by a potential adversary, which is perceived as a promise to harm one's own interests, because that adversary is presumed, with some basis, to have the intention and the capacity to do so

[593] Three articles of the United Nations Charter, drawn up in 1945, mention the term “aggression.”

[594] Indeed, Article 1 states that the purpose of the United Nations is “to maintain international peace and security, and to this end: to take effective collective measures to prevent and eliminate threats to the peace, and to suppress acts of aggression or other breaches of the peace.”

[595] Article 39 of the Charter empowers the Security Council to determine the existence of any threat to the peace, breach of the peace, or act of aggression

[596] Finally, Article 53, part of Chapter VIII on "Regional Agreements," refers to the coercive measures that could be adopted against the "renewal of a policy of aggression" by countries that the Charter identifies as "enemy States," that is, "any State which during the Second World War was an enemy of any of the signatories" of the founding document of the United Nations

[597] Subsequently, in December 1974, the United Nations General Assembly adopted a definition of aggression according to which “aggression is the use of armed force by a State against the sovereignty, territorial integrity or political independence of another State” (Box

[598] According to this concept, the UN considers the State that first uses armed force in violation of the Charter to be a prima facie aggressor, and the Security Council may make the final determination of the nature and scope of the act

[599] In turn, and in accordance with Article 5 of the Rome Statute of the International Criminal Court, to which Chile is a party, aggression is a crime over which the aforementioned Court will have jurisdiction

[600] THE INTERNATIONAL FRAMEWORK OF DEFENSE BOX 9: DEFINITION OF AGGRESSION (Resolution No. 3.314/1974 of the United Nations General Assembly) Art

[601] “Aggression is the use of armed force by a State against the sovereignty, territorial integrity or political independence of another State, or in any other manner inconsistent with the Charter of the United Nations, as set forth in this definition.”

[602] ° “The first use of armed force by a State in violation of the Charter shall constitute prima facie evidence of an act of aggression, although the Security Council may conclude, in accordance with the Charter, that a determination that an act of aggression has been committed would not be justified in light of other relevant circumstances, including the fact that the acts in question or their consequences are not of sufficient gravity.”

[603] “Subject to and in accordance with the provisions of Article 2, any of the following acts, whether or not war has been declared, shall be characterized as an act of aggression: • Invasion or attack by the armed forces of a State of the territory of another State, or any military occupation, however temporary, resulting from such invasion or attack, or any annexation, by the use of force, of the territory of another State or part of it; • Bombardment by the armed forces of a State of the territory of another State, or the employment of any weapons by a State against the territory of another State; • Blockade of the ports or coasts of a State by the armed forces of another State; • Attack by the armed forces of a State against the land, naval, or air forces of another State, or against its merchant or air fleet; • Use of armed forces of a State, which are present in the territory of another State with the consent of the receiving State, in violation of the conditions laid down in the agreement or any prolongation of their presence in that territory after the termination of the agreement; • The action of a State that allows its territory, which it has placed at the disposal of another State, to be used by that other State to perpetrate an act of aggression against a third State; • The dispatch by a State, or on its behalf, of armed bands, irregular groups or mercenaries that carry out acts of armed force against another State of such gravity that they are comparable to the acts enumerated above, or their substantial participation in such acts

[604] “The list of acts mentioned above is not exhaustive, and the Security Council may determine which other acts constitute aggression within the meaning of the Charter.”

[605] THE INTERNATIONAL FRAMEWORK OF DEFENSE To this day, the concept of aggression established by the United Nations is the doctrinal basis for judging which State has been the aggressor and which the attacked in a conflict, with the international consequences that this could entail for the former

[606] Although international forums, including the United Nations, have explored the notion that the first use of force, particularly in a nuclear context, is a criterion that can render self-defense impossible or ineffective, it is undeniable that relaxing the criterion on aggression adopted in 1974 could entail conceptual, political, and practical difficulties

[607] Each State is responsible for having the necessary capabilities to detect a potential attack against it in time and to counter it appropriately

[608] In short, the Charter of the United Nations and General Assembly resolution 3314 remain the universal and most complete expression of States' renunciation of aggression against each other

[609] Chapter 86 Book of National Defense of Chile 2010 THE INTERNATIONAL FRAMEWORK OF DEFENSE V

[610] Charter of the United Nations The Charter of the United Nations, which was signed in June 1945 and entered into force in October of that same year, constitutes an important regime for limiting armed conflict arising from the suffering imposed on humanity by the First and Second World Wars

[611] The Charter declares the purpose of the peoples of the United Nations to “save succeeding generations from the scourge of war” and to “maintain international peace and security.”

[612] It advocates the use of armed force only in the service of the common interest and promotes the peaceful settlement of disputes or international situations that could lead to breaches of the peace

[613] The use of military force in the international environment is shaped or conditioned by international institutions that the global community has established over time

[614] These institutions, which are of various types, give rise to what has been generically called "international regimes

[615] This chapter sets out the main regimes for limiting war or armed conflict in general, and includes a reference to human security, also understood as a concept that, while complementing the security of States, requires them to commit to respecting the value and dignity of the human person, both in cases of international conflict and in the sphere of their internal affairs

[616] ARMED CONFLICT LIMITATION REGIMES THE INTERNATIONAL FRAMEWORK OF DEFENSE To materialize these purposes, the Charter calls for joining forces for the maintenance of international peace and security, and gives a Security Council various functions and powers to fulfill its primary responsibility of ensuring that end

[617] Based on the principle of cooperation among Member States with the actions undertaken by the United Nations to maintain international peace and security, the Charter specifies the role of the Security Council in situations susceptible to solution through the peaceful settlement of disputes and the actions, including the use of military force, it may deploy to maintain or restore international peace and security

[618] Notwithstanding the foregoing, and the United Nations being the only institution accepted by the international community to use force against the breach of peace anywhere in the world

[619] The Charter expressly recognizes the right of a State to self-defense, whether individual or collective, against an armed attack

[620] International Humanitarian Law (IHL) is the set of international rules intended to mitigate, for humanitarian reasons, the effects of armed conflicts, protect people and property affected or likely to be affected, and limit the use of means and methods of warfare

[621] With regard to individuals, IHL protects all individuals who are not participating or cease to be active combatants in the conflict: the civilian population; wounded or sick military personnel in land warfare; the wounded and shipwrecked in naval warfare; medical personnel of the armed forces; prisoners of war; religious personnel; and personnel of civil protection agencies

[622] With regard to property, IHL protects cultural property, especially historic monuments, works of art, or places of worship, as well as the environment, against widespread, long-term, and serious damage

[623] Also called International Law of Armed Conflicts (ILC) or simply the law of war

[624] THE INTERNATIONAL FRAMEWORK OF DEFENSE IHL is applicable to international or non-international armed conflicts, in which armed forces or organized armed groups participate

[625] In the case of terrorism, although the concept of a "global war" on terrorism has been coined, this is a notion of a political rather than a legal nature

[626] IHL does not define what constitutes terrorism, although it prohibits terrorist acts as a method of fighting an armed conflict

[627] Acts aimed at combating terrorism are subject to the rules of domestic law, international human rights law, as well as international criminal law or treaties aimed at combating terrorism

[628] Without prejudice to the rules established by IHL relating to situations of internal violence that do not reach the intensity of an armed conflict, domestic law and human rights regulations apply in this case

[629] IHL measures implemented by Chile Chile affirms that IHL is fully applicable in all cases where the existence of armed struggle between parties with opposing interests is evident

[630] Within this framework, our country actively participates in all instances that allow for improving the means and methods of teaching and efficiently applying IHL (Box

[631] In terms of IHL education, Chile is implementing a process of incorporating this subject into the general and specific teaching programs it is responsible for suggesting or implementing as a State policy

[632] At a general level, respect for human rights, and IHL as part of them, constitutes one of the cross-cutting objectives of Chilean education

[633] At a specific level, IHL teaching is carried out in a comprehensive and systematic manner in the Armed Forces

[634] IHL is also a relevant subject in the educational programs carried out at the Joint Center for Peacekeeping Operations of Chile (CECOPAC

[655] Human Security The main value of this concept lies in complementing the traditional concept of security focused on the State with a people-centered approach, in order to minimise the negative effects that arise for individuals in a context of insecurities and tensions, both to the commitment of States to operate within the framework of International Humanitarian Law in situations of international conflict, as well as to the commitment that States owe to their own citizens in matters that affect their rights and freedoms, even considering the effects derived from daily social violence, the availability of small arms and light weapons and organised crime

[656] THE INTERNATIONAL FRAMEWORK OF DEFENSE This approach serves as a reference for the management of problems characteristic of the era of globalization

[657] Many of these problems transcend the scope of international conflicts and cannot be addressed through defense policies

[658] For example, efforts aimed at narrowing the technological gap between developed and poor countries, reducing the impact of criminal violence in large cities, or addressing the consequences of the spread of pandemics (such as AIDS

[659] However, the concept does apply to United Nations peacekeeping operations and could be applied to other initiatives eventually adopted to manage conflicts that could lead to crimes against humanity, provoke regional crises, and increase the level of insecurity among those affected

[660] Chile has been an active promoter of human security in international relations and, as such, has incorporated it among the principles that guide the design and execution of its foreign policy, hand in hand with respect for and promotion of human rights, democracy as a system of government, respect for international humanitarian law, and human development, understanding that the former complement each other in a harmonious whole

[661] Since 1999, Chile has been part of the Human Security Network, which it chaired from May 2001 to July

[662] Our country also participates in the Informal Forum "Group of Friends of Human Security," established in 2006 at the initiative of the governments of Mexico and Japan

[663] Chapter 91 Book of National Defense of Chile 2010 THE INTERNATIONAL FRAMEWORK OF DEFENSE Chapter 92 Book of National Defense of Chile 2010 THE INTERNATIONAL FRAMEWORK OF DEFENSE Although there are no precise legal definitions for each category of weapons, from the point of view of national defense they can be broken down as follows: weapons of mass destruction, conventional weapons and, within the latter, light and small arms

[664] Conventional Weapons Conventional weapons are defined, by exclusion, as those that are not weapons of mass destruction

[665] In Chile, Law No. 17,798 on arms control limits the Armed Forces' ability to possess certain types of conventional weapons, such as heavy, high-caliber, and destructive weapons

[666] This law assigns the supervision and control of weapons to the General Directorate of National Mobilization, which exercises this function over conventional weapons in private possession, with the oversight of compliance falling to the Carabineros' de Chile

[667] Since its inception, the contemporary international effort to limit arms has focused on military spending, the conduct of belligerents in war, weapons of mass destruction, and certain types of conventional weapons

[668] This arms control regime consists of a set of international or nationally implemented measures, mechanisms and initiatives, implemented both globally and regionally, as well as bilaterally, and linked to non-proliferation, verification and transparency processes (Box

[669] In conceptual terms, the political and legal process aimed at limiting armaments and military activity in the world has materialized in the notions of "disarmament" and "arms control," following for these purposes the principles of the United Nations Charter considered in its Article 11, paragraph 1, and in its Article

[670] ARMS LIMITATION REGIME Chapter 93 Chilean National Defense Book 2010 THE INTERNATIONAL DEFENSE FRAMEWORK BOX 11: DISARMAMENT AND ARMS CONTROL • “Disarmament” measures aim to reduce the capabilities of States to produce, distribute, stockpile, transfer or use certain weapons and their components, as well as to reduce or totally prohibit, as the case may be, certain categories of conventional weapons and weapons of mass destruction

[671] • This approach to disarmament is based on the consideration that armaments are in themselves a source of tension and war

[672] Consequently, disarmament aims to avoid or at least reduce the possibility of a military conflict by depriving the belligerents, in whole or in part, of certain military capabilities

[673] • Disarmament measures may be adopted as a result of an armed conflict, as a result of a unilateral decision, or as a result of a negotiation process

[674] “Arms control” measures impose restrictions, whether political or legal, on the ability of States to produce, distribute, stockpile, transfer, or use weapons

[675] • Arms control does not necessarily seek a reduction in military capabilities

[676] However, it may entail restrictions on the quantity, characteristics, or performance of weapons, as well as on the areas of deployment or readiness levels of weapons possessed by States

[677] They can also consist of a combination of all these options

[678] This conceptual category of arms control regime includes non-proliferation agreements and export control regimes

[679] Given the characteristics described, these arms control measures aim to facilitate the evaluation of reciprocal wills between States and to limit the available military response options

[680] Therefore, they largely coincide with the concept of mutual confidence-building measures, and can also be understood under the conceptual umbrella of International Humanitarian Law, when they establish standards for the use of certain types of weapons in conflict situations

[681] Conventional weapons are therefore permitted, although they are subject to control by the competent authorities

[682] However, there are some classes or types of conventional weapons that are prohibited by the Convention on Prohibitions or Restrictions on Certain Conventional Weapons that are Particularly Harmful or Have Indiscriminate Effects

[683] Chile is a party to this Convention and all its Protocols (Box

[684] Chapter 94 Book of National Defense of Chile 2010 THE INTERNATIONAL DEFENSE FRAMEWORK BOX 12: CONVENTIONAL WEAPONS PROHIBITED IN CHILE • All types of antipersonnel mines, in accordance with the Convention on the Prohibition of Antipersonnel Mines

[685] Cluster munitions and submunitions prohibited under the Convention on Cluster Munitions (a ban that will be effective from the moment the Convention enters into force in Chile

[686] • Anti-vehicle mines are prohibited by the Convention on Certain Conventional Weapons

[687] • Weapons whose primary effect is to injure or kill by means of projectiles whose fragments are not detectable in the human body through the use of X-rays. • Incendiary weapons

[688] • Blinding laser weapons

[689] Given that the military capabilities of the Chilean Armed Forces are based on the use of weapons belonging to this category, it is important for national defense to consider capacity substitution measures for our Armed Forces, as well as a cooperation policy that promotes full adherence to the aforementioned conventions in the region and around the world

[690] As a demonstration of its commitment, Chile is promoting various initiatives aimed at increasing transparency in this area: it participates in the United Nations Register of Conventional Arms, which addresses transparency in imports and exports by states; it is also a party to the Inter-American Convention on Transparency in Conventional Arms Acquisitions within the framework of the Organization of American States; and the Ottawa Convention on anti-personnel mines

[691] Chile has a national defense industry capable of producing a variety of conventional weapons and munitions systems. It therefore created an Interministerial Commission tasked with analyzing and authorizing Chilean arms exports and implementing the United Nations restrictions and recommendations to its Member States on this matter

[692] Among the various measures to continue advancing this process, Chile also aspires to join the Wassenaar Arrangement, an export control regime established to contribute to international security and stability by promoting transparency and accountability in the transfer of conventional arms and dual-use goods and technologies (civilian or military) (Box

[704] Chapter 96 Chilean National Defense Book 2010 THE INTERNATIONAL DEFENSE FRAMEWORK Light Weapons and Small Arms Light weapons and small arms are a subcategory of conventional weapons; they are increasingly important on the global security agenda, and particularly on regional and national security agendas

[705] Light weapons are those whose weight and characteristics allow them to be carried by an individual combatant or a small crew or group of combatants

[706] Small arms are light, low-caliber weapons

[707] Given the number of victims their use has caused throughout history, they have the highest fatality rate, and their use is not restricted to interstate armed conflicts

[708] In Latin America, the proliferation of light and small arms is associated with organized crime and drug trafficking, constituting one of the main "security problems" facing the region

[709] Explosive remnants of war that remain unexploded or abandoned at the end of armed conflicts constitute a source of “security problems” to which the international community is paying increasing attention

[710] These munitions hamper peacemaking, reconciliation, and post-conflict reconstruction processes

[711] They also have a potentially destabilizing effect, as some of these arsenals or remnants may end up in the hands of non-state armed groups or criminal organizations

[712] Within the framework of the Convention on Certain Conventional Weapons, Protocol V on Explosive Remnants of War was negotiated. This Protocol establishes standards for the management of conventional arsenals and prescribes a series of measures that impact the doctrines for the use and training of the Armed Forces

[713] Cluster Munitions Cluster munitions have been the subject of intense negotiation within the international community, due to the indiscriminate effects they cause, particularly among the civilian population during and after armed conflicts

[714] However, neither the negotiation process underway within the Convention on Certain Conventional Weapons for a new Protocol on Cluster Munitions nor the Oslo Convention on Cluster Munitions fully prohibits this type of weapon

[715] Rather, they are intended to limit their characteristics and allow them as long as they do not cause unacceptable harm

[716] However, they effectively outlaw weapons of this type currently in storage

[717] Chapter 97 Book of National Defense of Chile 2010 THE INTERNATIONAL FRAMEWORK OF DEFENSE Antipersonnel Mines Chile's policy on humanitarian demining aims to strictly comply with its commitments, developing national capabilities to do so

[718] This has resulted in all minefields or danger areas in Chile being identified, delimited and marked in accordance with applicable international standards, in addition to the progress already described in humanitarian demining

[719] To finance this commitment to the international community, the Chilean State has allocated resources representing more than 90% of the total investment in mine action tasks, with the remainder corresponding to international cooperation

[720] The national mine action effort has been incorporated into Chile's international defense relations, particularly at the bilateral level, as a relevant issue

[721] It has also been important for our country to assume responsibilities in the permanent committees of the Convention, participating with specialist personnel in the OAS Mine Action Assistance Missions in South America (MARMINAS) and Central America (MARMINCA) (Box

[738] Weapons of mass destruction (WMD) are nuclear, biological, chemical and radiological weapons

[739] Currently, concern about this issue focuses on the possibility of these weapons being developed or acquired by international actors, including terrorist organizations, rather than on avoiding a global conflict between major powers armed with WMD

[740] This has meant a shift in emphasis from mechanisms specific to disarmament to more complex and diverse ones, more specifically those specific to arms control and regulation

[741] In this regard, the priority is the fight against proliferation and the implementation of export control regimes

[742] This opens the door for a greater number of countries to actively participate in the processes described (Box

[743] Without prejudice to the foregoing, and given the geographical characteristics of Chilean territory, the specific features of the mine situation in our country, and the resources available for the demining process, it will not be possible to complete the clearance and destruction of all remaining antipersonnel mines within the 10-year period provided for by the Convention to comply with this obligation

[744] Therefore, in due course, Chile will exercise its right to request an extension of the deadline, as authorized by the Convention itself

[745] Furthermore, the collaboration of civil society, both national and international, is an important area in mine action, especially in mine risk prevention and victim assistance

[746] In this regard, the Chilean humanitarian situation is relatively small compared to that of other countries, although there are limitations in access to health and rehabilitation systems, as well as to social security

[747] The State is working to improve access to these systems and establish mechanisms for reparation and reintegration

[748] Source: National Demining Commission (CNAD

[749] In Chile, fewer than 200 people have suffered accidents with anti-personnel mines, anti-vehicle mines or unexploded ordnance of military origin THE INTERNATIONAL DEFENSE FRAMEWORK Chapter 100 National Defense Book of Chile 2010 BOX 15: WEAPONS OF MASS DESTRUCTION • Nuclear weapons: any material, including the delivery vector, used to cause damage and destruction to living beings and their environment, whose action is based on the high-power explosion caused by the sudden release of energy as a result of a nuclear fission or fusion process

[750] • Biological weapons: are all microbial or other biological agents or toxins, regardless of their origin or mode of production, the types and quantities of which are not justified for prophylactic, protective or other peaceful purposes, and include weapons, equipment or vectors intended to use such agents or toxins for hostile purposes or in armed conflict

[751] • Chemical weapons: These are toxic chemical substances or their precursors, except when used for non-prohibited purposes, provided that the types and quantities involved are compatible with those purposes

[752] This concept includes munitions or devices intended to cause death or injury through the toxic properties of prohibited substances, and any equipment intended to be used directly in connection with the use of such munitions or devices

[753] • Radiological weapons: These are radioactive material dispersion devices (RDD) designed to cause damage through radioactive contamination, using the blast wave of a conventional explosive or other means of particle transport to cause a psychological effect on the population

[754] Chile does not possess weapons of mass destruction

[755] In this regard, our policy is oriented toward achieving three general objectives: 1) Moving toward the total prohibition of these types of weapons worldwide; 2) Supporting the international community's efforts to combat their proliferation; and 3) Protecting the population from the effects of such weapons

[756] To this end, Chile has joined and actively participates in international regimes on weapons of mass destruction

[757] It has also been incorporated into bodies such as the Proliferation Security Initiative (PSI

[758] Regarding export control regimes, Chile aims to formally join those related to WMD, in line with what it has already done with the Chemical Weapons Convention and the Biological Weapons Convention

[759] PROLIFERATION SECURITY INITIATIVE (PSI) The PSI is a set of commitments voluntarily undertaken by member states to prevent the trafficking or proliferation of WMD, their delivery systems and related elements, by both state and non-state actors

[760] The PSI contains four basic commitments contained in the initiative's Declaration of Principles: Undertake effective measures, independently or jointly with other States, to interdict the transfer or transport of WMD, their delivery systems and related elements

[761] • Adopt streamlined procedures for rapid exchange of information relating to suspected proliferation activities

[762] • Strengthen relevant national and international jurisdictional bodies to achieve these objectives. • Support interdiction efforts targeting WMD shipments, their delivery systems, or related data

[763] Export control regimes, while operating on a voluntary basis for participating States, tend to establish general international standards regarding trade and exchange practices for arms, technology, and precursor materials

[764] Control regimes are also forums for the exchange of information regarding scientific and technological developments in the different categories of weapons (Box

[765] THE INTERNATIONAL DEFENSE FRAMEWORK BOX 17: INTERNATIONAL NORMS AND FORUMS ON WMD Normative instruments: • Treaty on the Non-Proliferation of Nuclear Weapons (NPT, 1967). • Comprehensive Nuclear-Test-Ban Treaty (CTBT). • International Code of Conduct against the Proliferation of Ballistic Missiles Capable of Delivering Weapons of Mass Destruction (“Hague Code”, HCOC). • Convention on the Prohibition of Chemical Weapons (CWC
