[777] THE INTERNATIONAL DEFENSE FRAMEWORK Chapter 103 Book of the National Defense of Chile 2010 VI

[778] The Treaty on the Non-Proliferation of Nuclear Weapons. This treaty (NPT) is recognized as the cornerstone of the multilateral regime of “Disarmament, Non-Proliferation and Arms Control”

[779] Its objective is, first, to contain the proliferation of nuclear weapons and, second, to initiate a negotiation process that will eventually eliminate them

[780] It also recognizes the "inalienable right" of all its States Parties to benefit from the peaceful applications of nuclear energy

[781] These three elements make up what are known as the "pillars" of the treaty: nuclear non-proliferation, nuclear disarmament, and the right to peaceful uses of nuclear energy

[782] To date, the treaty has 190 States Parties, making it one of the most universally recognized multilateral instruments

[783] Chile ratified the NPT on May

[784] While the treaty's ultimate goal of eliminating nuclear weapons is realized, its provisions restrict legitimate possession of nuclear weapons to states that had conducted nuclear tests prior to January 1, 1967, that is, before its negotiation

[785] Such countries are China, the United States, the Russian Federation, France and the United Kingdom

[786] It should be noted that two non-NPT states – India and Pakistan – have developed military nuclear programs and clearly possess nuclear weapons

[787] Apparently, Israel would also possess them

[788] North Korea, for its part, indicated its willingness to withdraw from the treaty in 2003 and has conducted two nuclear weapons tests to date

[789] Compliance with the NPT is verified at Review Conferences held every five years, which are preceded by preparatory meetings

[790] Chile actively participated in the Review Conferences of 2000 and

[791] The NPT is built on a political compromise whereby a large majority of countries renounce the possession of nuclear weapons in exchange for a commitment by the five nuclear-weapon states (NWS) to eventually abandon them

[792] This perspective entails a global negotiation process aimed at establishing an international regime that provides security for all nations, reducing the number of weapons of this type to the lowest possible level

[793] The aforementioned transaction has received criticism for its asymmetry, as it maintains over time (although not indefinitely) a privilege in favor of the EPAN

[794] However, such asymmetry has been justified by the need to guarantee a legal right (and, at the same time, a global public good): international security, threatened during the Cold War by the nuclear arms race

[795] The significant reductions in the number of nuclear weapons since 1990 are a partial but important step toward the ultimate goal envisioned by Article VI of the Treaty

[796] The Impact of Non-Proliferation on the Region Nuclear non-proliferation is an essential objective for the international community as a whole, since the multiplication of nuclear-weapon States constitutes an objective threat to international peace and security

[797] Multilateral action to avert nuclear danger has both a universal dimension (reflected in the NPT or the Comprehensive Nuclear-Test-Ban Treaty, CTBT) and a regional dimension, represented in our hemisphere by the Treaty for the Prohibition of Nuclear Weapons in Latin America and the Caribbean (Treaty of Tlatelolco, TT

[798] The TT is the first multilateral disarmament instrument to establish a “Nuclear-Weapon-Free Zone” in a densely populated region of the planet

[799] The commitment assumed by its States Parties is symmetrical, that is, the renunciation of nuclear weapons is general and all States Parties are subject to a verification regime, currently entrusted to the International Atomic Energy Agency (IAEA

[800] Verification is carried out through Comprehensive Safeguards Agreements and Additional Safeguards Protocols that each State Party to the Treaty individually signs with the IAEA

[801] Furthermore, the treaty regime includes two Additional Protocols, through which the NPAs commit both to respect the status of military denuclearization that the TT provides for the Latin American and Caribbean region, as well as to not use nuclear weapons or threaten to use them (negative assurances) to the Contracting Parties to the Treaty for the Prohibition of Nuclear Weapons in this part of the world (Additional Protocol II, Article III

[802] Nuclear-Weapon-Free Zones (NWFZs) constitute an intermediate stage towards universal nuclear disarmament and an effective mechanism for achieving negative security measures of regional scope

[803] Five treaties establish NWFZs, the TT being the pioneer: the treaties of Pelindaba (Africa), Rarotonga (South Pacific), Bangkok (Southeast Asia) and Semipalatinsk (Central Asia) have substantially extended the scope of the denuclearized world

[804] CONCEPTUALISATION Chapter VIII

[805] CONDUCT OF THE DEFENSE Chapter IX

[806] DEFENSE AND SECURITY Chapter X. COOPERATION IN DEFENSE AND SECURITY

[807] The concept of defense relates to the action and effect of preserving possession of a good or maintaining a sufficient degree of freedom of action to achieve such good

[808] National defense is the set of material, human, and moral means that a nation can oppose to an adversary's threats against its interests, primarily its sovereignty and territorial integrity

[809] Its purpose is to achieve a condition of external security such that the country can achieve its objectives free from external interference

[810] Defense is a non-transferable function of the State, guided by clear state principles and led by a set of institutional authorities, who make up a system whose central figure is the President of the Republic

[811] In terms of public policy, this is reflected in defense policy, which considers the basic guidelines for the entire sector

[812] The State of Chile understands that in the field of international relations, cooperation and competition coexist, with conflict being a specific derivation of the latter

[813] Indeed, not all competition becomes a conflict between States or international actors, and in some areas, differences arising from conflicting interests are resolved through mechanisms for channeling and resolving disputes

[814] However, conflict is a relationship that constitutes the primary object and concern of defense

[815] Given the large and diverse range of intertwined interests among actors in the international system, it is foreseeable that, along with the processes of cooperation and integration, a variety of conflicts of varying nature and intensity will emerge

[816] Faced with this reality, Chile has explicitly stated, at least since the publication of its first National Defense Book in 1997, that the orientation of its defense policy is defensive and deterrent

[817] The security of the Chilean nation must be understood as a condition that varies according to the actions the State takes to advance the country toward its intended objectives and safeguard national interests with the least interference from risks, threats, problems, or other significant obstacles

[818] However, the pursuit of national security must be achieved through distinct and specific functions, which the State employs in conjunction with other complementary state functions for this purpose

[819] Indeed, the defense function is primarily combined with the diplomatic function to achieve external security; the public order function is carried out in conjunction with the administration of justice for the purposes of internal security, just as security from disasters is achieved through the civil protection and environmental protection functions

[820] NATIONAL DEFENSE POLICY

[821] The defense function exists to provide external security to the country, although it extends to all members of the national community

[822] Once produced, no one obtains it at the expense of others

[823] To guarantee this effect, the following conditions must be met: The monopoly of legitimate force must belong to the State

[824] • That the State organize it, provide it with resources and assign it objectives with a national sense

[825] • That the State carry out this task continuously over time, whether in peace or war

[826] • That the exercise of the function covers the sum of activities of the State agencies necessary for defense, since its scope exceeds the limits of the strictly military

[827] The defense function, like other state functions, requires an organizational structure capable of planning and executing it, management control mechanisms, supporting legislation, financing, and guiding policies

[828] A public policy, known as defense policy, encompasses all of these aspects

[829] In its aim to provide external security to the country, defense policy is complemented by foreign policy

[830] Similarly, in matters of internal security, it contributes to the policy of public order and internal security; the direct participation of Armed Forces resources in internal order tasks is regulated in Article 39 et seq. of our Political Constitution by virtue of certain states of constitutional exception

[831] Finally, in the area of disaster safety, defense policy supports the specific policies in which it is embodied

[832] The articulation or coordination of different public policies can give rise to a "security strategy" to address the various challenges facing the State; however, the security strategy should not be understood as an all-encompassing policy that frames all public policies from the perspective of national security

[833] NATIONAL DEFENSE POLICY Chapter 109 Book of National Defense of Chile 2010 VII

[834] Defense policy consists of the set of guidelines with which the State executes its defense function

[835] It defines the objectives of national defense, guides the actions of its Armed Forces through a military policy, and establishes the priorities and methods for investing financial resources, thus contributing to the achievement of the country's interests and objectives

[836] In general terms, the State of Chile has a defense policy aimed at protecting the country's population, protecting its national interests, and safeguarding its political independence, national sovereignty, and territorial integrity

[837] To be able to fulfill these objectives, it possesses and prepares the necessary military resources to be used in legitimate defense, which requires modernizing all defense institutions, including the Armed Forces

[838] It is also clear that, in a comprehensive and modern sense, among the factors that affect national security itself are international stability and security

[839] It follows that, to achieve these goals, defense policy seeks to contribute to global peace and security by collaborating with the community of nations in preventing international conflict and supporting the validity of international law. It also seeks to cooperate with the region on defense and security matters, as well as strengthen mutual trust-building measures with other States (Box

[840] It is under a conception such as the one described that the Armed Forces assume the missions assigned to them to satisfy these purposes

[841] Defense policy is a public policy and, therefore, must be subject to the general conditions for the formulation, implementation, and verification of other public policies, including the requirements of transparency and citizen participation

[842] However, certain aspects of defense policy require safeguards due to the nature of national defense, which differentiates it from other public policies and explains why, in general, it is socially accepted that specific activities or decisions related to the country's external security must be protected by special legal provisions

[843] This justifies the specificity of treatment in certain cases and the sensitivity of its management

[844] NATIONAL DEFENSE POLICY Chapter 111 Book of National Defense of Chile 2010 BOX 18: OBJECTIVES OF NATIONAL DEFENSE • Preserve the country's independence and sovereignty

[845] • Maintain the integrity of the national territory

[846] Contribute to the creation of fundamental external security conditions to achieve the common good of the nation

[847] • Support Chile’s international projection

[848] • Contribute to the maintenance and promotion of international peace and security, in accordance with the national interest

[849] • Contribute to national development and cooperate in achieving a balanced and harmonious achievement of the country's various capabilities

[850] • At the institutional level, contribute to the preservation of Chile's institutional framework as a democratic Republic and the rule of law

[851] • Likewise, by embodying national traditions and symbols, they contribute to safeguarding our historical and cultural identity, without hindering its renewal and enrichment

[852] Contribute to the activities that the State carries out with the purpose of strengthening citizen commitment to Defense

[853] The Principles of Defense Policy Chile's defense policy is framed within the following general principles of the State: • The State of Chile does not harbor aggressive intentions against any nation in the world, nor does it have territorial claims in the neighboring area

[854] • The State of Chile is committed to protecting its population, defending its national interests, safeguarding its political independence, national sovereignty, and territorial integrity

[855] • The State of Chile has the responsibility to maintain sufficient military capacity to facilitate the achievement of the country's interests and objectives, ensuring that the development of this military capacity is proportional to the development of the rest of the country's capabilities

[856] • The State of Chile has the right and responsibility to employ all national capabilities, and in particular its military potential, if necessary, to defend the country and safeguard national interests against external threats

[857] • The Chilean State must foster citizen commitment to national defense, considering, among other objectives, the necessary provisions for the provision of Compulsory Military Service, the satisfaction of national mobilization needs, and those arising from maintaining the capacities and skills of the reserves

[858] • For the State of Chile – as for all States in general – its relative geographical location remains an important reference in the formulation of defense policy

[859] • The State of Chile places its defense policy fully within the institutional legal framework in force in the country, and recognizes and respects the international treaties and agreements incorporated into said framework, such that its defense policy possesses the legitimacy inherent to any State policy and is representative of the political commitment that binds the State to its national citizens, on the one hand, and to the international community, on the other

[860] For the State of Chile, the close link and coordination between its defense policy and its foreign policy is of particular interest and convenience, complementing and strengthening each other, although the former acts in support of the latter

[861] • The State of Chile maintains its commitment to contribute to the defense and promotion of international peace, in accordance with the national interest

[862] NATIONAL DEFENSE POLICY Book of the National Defense of Chile 2010 VII

[863] The Defense System Due to the nature and repercussions of decisions related to national defense, its management requires a specific system and organization

[864] The "defense system" is the generic name that encompasses the organizations involved in both decision-making and the execution of actions that materialize defense

[865] This system is made up of decision-making authorities at different levels—primarily the political and strategic levels—advisory bodies, executive bodies, and entities that produce defense goods

[866] Specifically, these “actors” are the President of the Republic and the Minister of National Defense at the political decision-making level; the Commanders-in-Chief of the military institutions; and the military authority that commands the forces in the event of a conflict at the strategic, institutional, and joint decision-making levels, respectively

[867] These actors are joined by the President's advisory councils, such as the National Security Council (COSENA), and the Minister's advisory bodies, such as the Joint Chiefs of Staff

[868] There are also military organizations and forces that operate in conflict situations

[869] Finally, defense industries are considered

[870] In this system, the "actors" interact in a regulatory environment shaped by the Executive Branch and the National Congress, and they engage in the execution of various processes relevant to decisions and actions in this area

[871] There are several processes that take place in the defense system

[872] One of these is the assessment or analysis of all external variables that influence the country's security, the results of which allow us to recommend to the President of the Republic the measures that are deemed necessary to anticipate or implement

[873] Another very significant process is planning, which is generated by the resolution or guidelines issued by the President of the Republic

[874] A third highly significant process is the acquisition of weapons systems, which requires subsidiary processes as complex as the evaluation of investment projects

[875] Finally, there are also administrative and management processes, and even collaboration and advisory processes

[876] Some of these processes are replicated at different levels, such as planning, which reaches down to the tactical level, but all are essential for the defense system to fulfill its function as such (Figure

[877] NATIONAL DEFENSE POLICY Chilean National Defense Book 2010 GRAPHIC 7 NATIONAL DEFENSE SYSTEM PDTE MDN

[878] Military Chief J CC J COSENA NATIONAL CONGRESS ARMY FORCES NAVY FORCES

[879] Certainly, given the nature of national defense, the defense system permanently incorporates as inputs for its different processes the background, data and contributions that ministries, departments or agencies of other public sectors may provide

[880] NATIONAL DEFENSE POLICY Chilean National Defense Book 2010 The conduct of defense, materialized in the exercise of command, is the process by which decisions are made to direct the defense of the country

[881] Based on a specific organizational structure, this process flows through a regulated and systematized channel from the highest decision-making level of the State, which corresponds to the President of the Republic, to the strategic or military level

[882] Since defense is an eminently national matter, the responsibility for its general management, governance, and administration rests with the Head of State

[883] Given the importance of decisions regarding the country's progress in times of peace and conflict, the President receives political contributions, which come from state agencies, institutions, or political authorities, and military contributions, which come from the Armed Forces and are channeled through the Ministry of Defense

[884] CONDUCT OF THE DEFENSE

[885] Reference Framework The rules governing the conduct of defense in Chile are found in different instruments, which have different legal status and respond to different contexts, depending on the time in which they were promulgated

[886] Essentially, the concept of "strategic leadership of the Armed Forces" appears in Decree with Force of Law (DFL) No. 181 of 1960 and its complementary regulations, currently in force

[887] The concept refers to an action by which the National Security Council (COSENA) advises the President of the Republic and studies and proposes measures aimed at coordinating the work of the various ministries to ensure compliance with the policy established by the President regarding national security and territorial integrity

[888] The concept contained in this DFL dates back forty years and responds to the conditions and notions specific to that time

[889] However, over time, various changes have occurred that have impacted the field of defense: from a dynamic global landscape, with unprecedented political, social, and economic phenomena, to a more significant international legal framework and new technological developments

[890] The aforementioned changes have had a widespread impact on the country and have also been gradually incorporated into national defense

[891] Among other things, this has meant a greater emphasis on the availability of capabilities that allow for joint and combined operations

[892] As a result, defense systems around the world have tended to establish an operational line of command that is distinct from the institutional line of command

[893] Higher Defense Structure In Chile's presidential government regime, the main higher defense institution corresponds to the President of the Republic

[894] Other bodies, which perform various functions according to the current legal system of our country, are the National Congress, the National Security Council (COSENA), the Higher Council of National Security (CONSUSENA), and the Higher Council of National Defense (CONSUDENA

[895] Together, these institutions and State bodies, which act at the highest level of defense management, make up what is known as the higher defense structure

[896] NATIONAL CONGRESS PRESIDENT OF THE REPUBLIC NATIONAL SECURITY COUNCIL NATIONAL SECURITY SUPERIOR COUNCIL MINISTER OF NATIONAL DEFENSE JOINT OF COMMANDERS-IN-CHIEF NATIONAL DEFENSE SUPERIOR COUNCIL GRAPHIC 8 SENIOR STRUCTURE OF NATIONAL DEFENSE The President of the Republic Article 24 of the Political Constitution of Chile, when referring to the President of the Republic, states that he is responsible for the government and administration of the State and that his authority extends to everything that has the objective of preserving public order within the country and the external security of the Republic, in accordance with the Constitution and the laws

[897] In relation to the defense function, that is, the function oriented towards the external security of the country, the powers of the President of the Republic are: To arrange, organize and distribute the air, sea and land forces To assume, in case of war, the Supreme Command of the Armed Forces To declare war, with prior authorization by law, leaving a record of having heard the National Security Council To declare, with the concurrence of other organs of the State, the states of constitutional exception of assembly and siege The indicated functions summarize the main exclusive powers of the President of the Republic to resolve in the field of defense

[898] The National Congress The National Congress is composed of the Senate and the Chamber of Deputies

[899] Each legislative branch has a Defense Committee, which is responsible for analyzing and reporting to the respective plenary on all bills relating to national defense in general and the Armed Forces in particular

[900] Regarding the defense sector, Congress, in addition to analyzing and approving the budget law, which includes financial resources for defense, collaborates with the Executive Branch in the approval of permanent laws

[901] Likewise, Congress has the power to decide on the state of assembly in the event of a foreign war

[902] Furthermore, it is the Senate's responsibility to authorize or deny the entry of foreign troops into the territory of the Republic, as well as the departure of national troops abroad

[903] The National Security Council (COSENA) is an organization created by the Political Constitution (CPR) of the country in

[904] Its mission is to advise the President of the Republic on matters related to national security and to exercise other functions entrusted to it by the CPR

[905] Under the presidency of the Head of State, it is composed of the Presidents of the Senate, the Chamber of Deputies, and the Supreme Court of Justice, the Commanders-in-Chief of the Armed Forces, the Director General of the Carabineros, and the Comptroller General of the Republic

[906] According to the President of the Republic's determination, some Ministers may be included, including the Minister of National Defense

[907] The Council meets at the call of the President and requires a quorum of an absolute majority of its members

[908] In December 2009, the National Congress approved the bill that modernizes the Ministry of National Defense (MINDEF), this being its largest institutional reform since the beginning of the 1930s, when this State portfolio acquired the structural and functional design that it essentially maintained for seventy years

[909] During this long period, only two significant changes were made to the Ministry of National Defense: the creation of the National Defense General Staff in 1942, and the incorporation of the undersecretariats of the Carabineros and the Investigative Police in

[910] The creation of the Joint Chiefs of Staff as an advisory body to the Minister in 1960 was another important innovation in the national defense framework, but it did not constitute an organic or functional change to the Defense Ministry

[911] The Minister of National Defense The Minister of National Defense is the direct and immediate collaborator of the President of the Republic in the government and administration of the defense sector

[912] He leads the relevant Ministry in accordance with the policies and instructions received from the President

[913] In this role, he is responsible for the overall planning and direction of the Ministry's activities and for providing direct and immediate assistance to the President in the conduct of national defense, both in peacetime and during conflict. In carrying out his duties, the Minister is advised by the Joint Chiefs-in-Chief (JCCJ), his principal advisory body in these matters

[914] It also has the support and advice of the National Defense Staff (EMDN), an agency of the Ministry of Defense, and ad hoc bodies that deal with specific matters, such as the Committee of Auditors General, the Committee of Personnel Directors, and the Committee of Health Directors

[915] According to the bill approved by the National Congress, the organization of the Ministry will be modified as indicated

[916] According to the constitutional provisions, the institutions of the Armed Forces (Army, Navy and Air Force) are dependent on the Ministry

[917] In addition, organizations belonging to the defense sector such as the General Directorate of National Mobilization (DGMN), the National Academy of Political and Strategic Studies (ANEPE), the Joint Center for Peace Operations of Chile (CECOPAC), the Civil Defense of Chile and the state defense industries (FAMAE, ASMAR, ENAER

[918] In turn, in 2005, through a constitutional reform, it was determined that the Law Enforcement and Security Forces would be under the Ministry in charge of public security

[919] However, until the legal and institutional provisions for this purpose are implemented, the Law Enforcement and Security Forces remain administratively dependent on the Ministry of National Defense

[920] Consequently, the Undersecretariats of Carabineros and the Investigative Police are now part of the current organizational structure of this State Secretariat

[921] The New Ministry and the Management of Defense The reform of the Ministry is based on a model that considers, among others, the following features: a. Simplification of the superior structure of defense The elimination or modification of agencies that respond to a conception that confuses internal security and external security, among other drawbacks, aims to generate a simpler and more effective functioning superior structure

[922] It modifies or eliminates certain national security and defense institutions that make up the higher defense structure and that respond to a concept inherited from the Cold War

[923] Considering the constitutional reforms enacted in 2005, COSENA remains the President's advisory body on matters related to national security, transferring the functions of other advisory councils at this level to competent bodies within the Ministry of National Defense itself, and reassigning or reformulating tasks assigned under current regulations to various ministerial and ministerial agencies

[924] b. Strengthening the role of the Minister of Defense The model strengthens the role of the highest ministerial authority in the management of the sector's government and administration processes

[925] Fundamentally, the model expands the role of the Minister in three important ways: it designates him as Chairman of the Joint Chiefs of Staff at all times, makes him responsible to the President of the Republic for defense planning, and clearly positions him as a direct collaborator of the Head of State in the exercise of the latter's role when he assumes the position of Supreme Commander-in-Chief of the Armed Forces

[926] c. Reorganization of the sub-secretariat structure The sub-secretariat structure is being transformed from an "institutional" structure, where the various sub-secretariats serve the Army, Navy, and Air Force separately, to sub-secretariats that will work in areas of functional competence based on comprehensive processes that will cover the full cycle of each of the sector's policies

[927] In this sense, the model includes an undersecretariat dedicated to defense policy matters per se, including the evaluation of investment projects, and another in charge of matters related to the management of administrative processes that the Ministry and the Armed Forces require to fulfill their functions

[928] d. Consolidation of the Joint Chiefs of Staff Redefines the Joint Chiefs of Staff exclusively as a military advisory body to the Minister of Defense, with a consultative nature, on institutional, common, and joint aspects of the Armed Forces

[929] It is also strengthened by the permanent presidency of the Minister himself and the incorporation, as a full member, of the Chief of the Joint Chiefs of Staff

[930] Creation of the Joint Chiefs of Staff Although the model is consistent with the constitutional norm in understanding that the leadership of defense corresponds to the President of the Republic, and that the Ministry of National Defense is its highest body of collaboration in the functions of government and administration of the sector, it aims to strengthen the joint institutionality, both in the area of strategic advice and planning, as well as in that of operations

[931] For this purpose, the Joint Chiefs of Staff (EMC) is created to replace the National Defense Staff

[932] Likewise, the Head of the EMC is given military command of the forces assigned to the operations and, therefore, the exercise of the functions of strategic planning, joint training, and the corresponding doctrinal development

[933] Defense Doctrine The formulation of the doctrine for conducting national defense aims to integrate a set of principles that guide the efforts of all actors involved, from the political authority responsible for the defense sector to the forces that carry out campaigns and operations

[934] The premises on which this doctrine is based are the following: Defense constitutes a right, a responsibility and a task of all Chileans and is a proper and non-delegable function of the State

[935] Therefore, it is responsible to the citizens for its organization and effectiveness, and to do so, it must, among other things, require citizen commitment to defense in the terms and forms determined

[936] • The conduct of defense is a function of an eminently political nature, both in times of peace and in conflict situations, since, in any system of government, it is an exclusive responsibility of the person holding the position of head of state

[937] In our case, the Political Constitution of the Republic establishes that said authority assumes, in the event of war, the supreme command of the Armed Forces

[938] • The conduct of defense is not limited to the conduct of war, since the use of force is a tool that can be considered not only for use in an armed conflict, but also for a range of international situations that may affect the country's external security or for international cooperation, including peacekeeping operations

[939] • The conduct of defense is materialized, in relation to force, in two lines: the line of development of force and the line of employment of force

[940] • As a general rule

[941] The decisions that materialize the conduct of defense are grouped into three levels: Political, Strategic and Operational

[942] Our current defense doctrine, dating back to the 1960s, includes a political leadership level, a political-strategic leadership level, a strategic leadership level, an operational leadership level, and a tactical leadership level

[943] However, from a modern perspective, these levels are often differentiated in terms of the nature of the decisions taken at each one

[944] Therefore, the most appropriate classification should distinguish between levels where decisions are of an eminently political nature and levels where decisions are of a strictly military nature

[945] If the criterion for defining the levels of leadership are the decisions that are made, then the politicalstrategic leadership level does not exist, since there are no political-strategic decisions

[946] The Head of State always makes political decisions, although in conflict situations, he may certainly require elements of judgment generated from the strategic sphere

[947] For this reason, it is more appropriate to speak of political decisions that are conceived in a political-strategic "environment

[948] Given the existence of two higher levels of decision-making – political and strategic, respectively – the assessment process and the planning process must also consider two levels: primary planning and secondary or strategic planning

[949] Each planning process, at its appropriate level, must be assigned to the responsibility of the relevant stakeholders at each level

[950] • Chile's defense will continue to rely primarily on its own national power, without prejudice to its adherence to a security/defense alliance or coalition

[951] • In any circumstance in which Chile uses force, whether unilaterally or by integrating its means of defense into a multilateral action, it will respect the Charter of the United Nations and international humanitarian law, ensuring respect for the human rights of all people

[952] • It will also be the function of national defense to cooperate in the maintenance of international peace and stability, through its participation in multinational operations, in accordance with national interests

[953] These premises or principles should guide all the provisions the State makes to prepare national resources for the eventuality of a conflict situation

[954] They condition the development of the means, military and non-military, that make up their national power

[955] Likewise, they must shed light on political decision-making processes within the framework of international relations, both multilateral and bilateral

[956] The Defense Planning System Planning is a methodical and continuous process that aims to obtain the best solution to a given problem

[957] In the area of defense, this process allows for the adoption of resolutions related to the country's security and defense, including both the development and use of force

[958] This process is reflected in documents called "plans," which are conceived as provisions to organize, coordinate, and guide different actors or organizations to achieve a specific purpose or objective

[959] In the case of national defense, planning is organized into three areas, thus constituting a “planning process”: The process carried out in the first area is eminently political in nature

[960] This process analyzes the country's external security situation, considering all the factors that influence it, under the direction of the Ministry of Defense and with the collaboration of various sectors of the State administration

[961] Taking this analysis into account, solutions are proposed to address the potential risks and threats that have been established

[962] The presidential resolution to confront them triggers the actual defense planning process, aimed at preparing the country and its defense resources for such eventualities

[963] Based on the decisions made in the political sphere, the process carried out in the second planning area, which is eminently military and joint in nature, develops strategic plans and force development and preparation plans

[964] Based on the decisions taken in the strategic sphere, the third sphere corresponds specifically to the use of force and refers to operational planning

[965] GRAPHIC 10 PLANNED PLANNING SYSTEM

[966] As shown in Figure 10, in the defense planning system, the Ministry of National Defense (MINDEF) acts as a space for coordination between the political and strategic spheres

[967] DEFENSE AND SECURITY States are experiencing new and complex security scenarios and challenges at the international level

[968] The distinction between traditional and non-traditional threats, anchored in the ideas of the classic security paradigm, does not seem to be sufficient to account for concerns and challenges of a diverse nature, which encompass multiple dimensions of the life of States, impact global, regional and neighboring environments simultaneously, and involve not only the State, but also supranational entities and non-state actors

[969] In this context, it is important to have categories and concepts that allow us to understand and guide the State's actions in the situations described

[970] Among these concepts, the ideas of threat, risk, and crisis have been the subject of ongoing debate in the region and in our country, given their relevance to understanding security environments and determining and formulating defense policy

[971] Defense, Security and Development The relationship between defense, security and development is one of the most complex in the public sphere

[972] National security is a product of the set of activities that the State undertakes to advance toward the achievement of its objectives and safeguard national interests in relation to significant risks, threats, or interference

[973] In this sense, security is a condition that is desired to establish so that the purposes of the State and the nation can be achieved, particularly those of social and economic development

[974] Obviously, defense contributes to the country's security through its preparation for the legitimate use of force, its contribution to deterrence, and the participation of its resources in international cooperation missions

[975] However, between the global function of security—and external security, as part of it—and development, as the generic goal of the State, a relationship is established that constitutes a dynamic process, in which, however, security cannot be understood as an end in itself

[976] Indeed, in its role of providing security, the State is limited and conditioned by the requirement to seek the common good of the nation and to serve the human person

[977] Hence, it is not ethically legitimate to achieve the security of a nation by disregarding the permanent values of justice and human dignity

[978] The State has an unavoidable responsibility in achieving the objectives that the country sets for itself, for which it performs various functions

[979] Indeed, it performs social functions to achieve a satisfactory level of well-being for the population; legislative functions to provide an adequate regulatory framework for public and private activities, and to regulate social coexistence

[980] It carries out the function of administering justice to repair illicit breaches in that coexistence or punish illegal activities by some members of society

[981] To develop political, economic, and cultural relations between states and with international organizations, project their interests, assert their rights, and influence the international community, the state performs the diplomatic function

[982] Ultimately, the State materializes the financial function to allocate resources to support all its other functions, including security and defense

[983] The State performs these last two functions—security and defense—to create the conditions under which the desired objectives can be achieved without setbacks

[984] Although security and defense are closely related, defense is a more specific concept than security

[985] It does not by itself produce the desired security condition, but it is undoubtedly one of the essential factors for achieving it

[986] Security Problems From this perspective, those phenomena that, after the Cold War, have tended to be grouped under the term "threat" with the titles of "new threats", "emerging threats" or "unconventional threats" should be considered, prima facie, as "problems to the security of States" rather than as threats in the field of defense

[987] While threats in this area have been referred to as “traditional threats,” the concept of “threat” should be reserved for phenomena whose characteristics better reflect the characteristics of what constitutes a threat in its own right, including the existence of an international actor that demonstrates a willingness to harm national interests and has the capacity to do so

[988] The broader category of "security problems" includes phenomena such as terrorism, drug trafficking, the proliferation of weapons of mass destruction, poverty, pandemics, and others that do not constitute threats in themselves, although they may eventually become them

[989] Internally, each State, according to its unique circumstances, seeks to address these security problems with specific and particular security strategies, while, when dealing with threats to basic interests (sovereignty, territory, and population), States confront them with defensive means

[990] When "security problems" affect more than one State, cooperation with the international community, to which Chile subscribes, is essential to address them (Box

[991] Defense contributes to the security of the country through the legitimate use of force, deterrence, and international cooperation

[992] The latter materializes, among other activities, through the participation of Chilean military assets in multilateral cooperative schemes, peacekeeping operations, military exchanges, and combined multinational exercises, which are part of the wide range of mutual trust-building measures

[993] Each State has the sovereign right to identify its own national security priorities and define the strategies, plans and actions to address threats to its security, in accordance with its legal system and with full respect for international law and the norms and principles of the OAS Charter and the Charter of the United Nations

[994] • The threats, concerns, and other challenges to security in the Hemisphere are diverse in nature and multidimensional in scope, and traditional concepts and approaches must be expanded to encompass new and non-traditional threats, including political, economic, social, health, and environmental aspects

[995] • Traditional security threats and their mechanisms for addressing them remain important and may be different in nature from new threats, concerns and other security challenges and the cooperative mechanisms for addressing them

[996] • New threats, concerns, and other challenges to hemispheric security are cross-cutting problems that require multifaceted responses from diverse national organizations and, in some cases, partnerships between governments, the private sector, and civil society, all acting appropriately in accordance with the democratic norms and principles and constitutional norms of each State

[997] Many of the emerging threats, concerns, and other challenges to hemispheric security are transnational in nature and may require appropriate hemispheric cooperation

[998] • The states of the Hemisphere recognize different perspectives on threats and priorities to their security

[999] The security architecture in our Hemisphere must be flexible and take into account the specificities of each subregion and each state

[1000] • The security of the states of the Hemisphere is affected, in different ways, by traditional threats and by the following new threats, concerns, and other challenges of a diverse nature: -- terrorism, transnational organized crime, the global drug problem, corruption, money laundering, illicit arms trafficking, and the connections between them; -- extreme poverty and social exclusion of large sectors of the population, which also affect stability and democracy; -- natural and man-made disasters, HIV/AIDS and other diseases, other health risks, and environmental degradation; -- trafficking in persons; -- cybersecurity attacks; -- the possibility of harm arising from an accident or incident during the maritime transport of potentially hazardous materials, including oil, radioactive material, and toxic waste; -- the possibility of access, possession, and use of weapons of mass destruction and their means of delivery by terrorists

[1001] • It is the responsibility of the specialized forums of the OAS, inter-American and international bodies to develop cooperation to address these new threats, concerns and other challenges based on applicable instruments and mechanisms

[1002] In the debate on “security problems” the following aspects stand out: • The difficulty of establishing a single agenda These problems do not affect all States in the same way or with the same intensity

[1003] The impact of such phenomena varies depending on the characteristics of each state or country

[1004] This is why it is difficult to establish a single agenda for security issues for our continent

[1005] The order of priorities Just as it is difficult to establish a single agenda of security problems for the entire continent, it is also complex to set priorities among them

[1006] Even if a single agenda were established, it would be difficult to ignore the fact that these phenomena have different urgency or priority for each country

[1007] In this context, crisis and conflict prevention and international cooperation appear to be the best and most effective ways to address these types of problems on the continent and find solutions

[1008] • Diversified response Although no country is a priori exempt from suffering security problems, the impact of these on the countries of the American continent varies, whether based on constants (geographic location and size) or variables (strategic importance or range of resources, materials and human resources that each State has

[1009] For this reason, the way in which each country addresses them and assumes international commitments to prevent or combat them also varies

[1010] Chile recognizes the existence of various security problems for states on the continent, but the approach to solving them varies depending on the situation

[1011] The Charter of the United Nations enshrines the principle of cooperation in the actions of this organization to maintain international peace and security

[1012] Accordingly, every member of the international community—regardless of its size or power—has a share of responsibility for preserving these conditions among the actors of the international system

[1013] COOPERATION IN DEFENSE AND SECURITY X

[1014] Chile fully applies the principle of international cooperation and, therefore, develops intense multilateral diplomacy, actively participating in the UN system and global forums related to peace and security in the world, as well as with the corresponding bodies of the OAS and UNASUR, all of which contribute synergistically to the preservation of peace and international security

[1015] Likewise, Chile actively participates with its defense resources in international peacekeeping operations, in combined exercises with third-country forces to ensure it is able to act jointly in situations that could disrupt global security, and in other security initiatives and regimes, such as the action against antipersonnel mines established by the Ottawa Convention and the action against cluster munitions

[1016] Chile's defense policy is comprehensive, that is, based on the principle of cooperation within the framework of the international system, especially the United Nations, embodying the principles of our foreign policy (Box 20); it combines international responsibility with consideration of the national interest; and it assumes that national security and development interests are directly related to global peace and stability

[1017] Considering all these aspects, our country understands its commitment to world peace in an active and profound manner, both in relation to its diplomatic action and in regard to the use of means for its defense

[1018] BOX 20: PRINCIPLES OF CHILE'S FOREIGN POLICY The principles of our foreign policy are the fundamental guidelines that support and give coherence to the decisions that Chile adopts in matters of foreign relations

[1019] These are the highest-ranking guidelines that must be considered when establishing definitions in foreign policy

[1020] Respect for international law: Chile asserts its rights in the international arena by strictly respecting and adhering to the legal instruments that regulate international relations

[1021] International law allows for regulating and limiting the use of force in the management of relations between States

[1022] Consequently, respect for them is an indispensable condition for the preservation of peace and security in international society

[1023] • Within the various fundamental norms that define and structure the international legal order, promoting peaceful relations between States, Chile assigns special importance to the following: -- Validity and respect for treaties -- Peaceful settlement of disputes -- Independence and respect for sovereignty -- Territorial integrity • Promotion of democracy and respect for human rights: Democracy is the political system that constitutes the appropriate framework for full respect for the essential rights of every human being

[1024] Chile values that the rights of individuals, as inalienable attributes of every human being, are observed in all circumstances, times, and places

[1025] Responsibility to cooperate: A cooperative approach, with a broad perspective, is essential when addressing global dangers or “security problems.”

[1026] No member of the international community wishing to promote peace, security and the common good can evade this responsibility

[1027] “Principles and Priorities of Chile’s Foreign Policy,” Santiago: Ministry of Foreign Affairs

[1028] Elaborating on the use of its defense resources, and after signing and ratifying the Charter of San Francisco, which gave rise to the United Nations, it should be emphasized that Chile joined the peacekeeping operations decided by the Security Council since 1949, although it limited its participation to sending officers from its Armed Forces as "military observers

[1029] This type of collaboration with world peace has been maintained since 1949, when the United Nations Military Observer Group in India and Pakistan (UNMOGIP) was established, until today (Figure
