[1033] In this way, Chile moved from the restricted role of "observer" to the active role of "actor" in cooperation with world peace

[1034] In August 1991, our country traveled to Kuwait with 50 Air Force specialists and a group of six helicopters to join the United Nations Iraq-Kuwait Observation Mission (UNIKOM) and support the United Nations' surveillance and monitoring efforts in Iraq following the conflict known as "Gulf War I

[1035] With the subsequent enactment of Law No. 19,067 in 1991, permanent regulations were established for the withdrawal of Chilean troops

[1036] This law represented a significant step forward in the process of consolidating a broader and deeper concept of cooperation between the Chilean State and international peace and stability

[1037] In 1992, a contingent of marines and military observers were sent to Southeast Asia to join the United Nations Transitional Authority in Cambodia (UNTAC) and assist in riverine and coastal patrols as part of the peace and reconstruction effort following the fall of the Khmer Rouge government

[1038] Four years later, in August 1996, a new Air Force group, composed of 42 personnel and five of its helicopters, joined the United Nations Special Commission (UNSCOM) to collaborate in the location and elimination of weapons of mass destruction that allegedly remained in Iraq's hands after the so-called "Gulf War II

[1039] This latter experience prompted our country to formulate, in November 1996, an explicit policy of participation in peacekeeping operations, although limited only to missions conceived under Chapter VI of the Charter

[1040] Three years later, however, Chile expanded its collaboration with the United Nations by signing an agreement with the UN Secretary-General to become part of the UN Standby Force Arrangements System (UNSAS) and also to join peace enforcement missions, i.e., those conceived according to Chapter VII of the Charter

[1041] The first peacekeeping operation in which Chilean troops took part under this new legal framework was East Timor, where a force of 163 troops and a helicopter platoon from the Army Aviation Brigade were sent in early 2000 to join the United Nations Transitional Administration Mission in East Timor (UNTAET

[1042] JOINT CENTER FOR PEACEKEEPING OPERATIONS OF CHILE (CECOPAC) In light of the National Policy for Participation in Peacekeeping Operations, the Joint Center for Peacekeeping Operations of Chile (CECOPAC) emerges as a response to Chile's efforts to maintain international peace and strengthen international cooperation

[1043] In particular, it contributes to the development and improvement of national capacities for national participation, using military, police, or civilian resources, in one of the most complex activities currently carried out in the world to prevent or mitigate violence or restore and preserve peace

[1044] CECOPAC is a joint center that integrates the country's civil, military, and police forces

[1045] It reports to the Ministry of National Defense, through the National Defense General Staff, and its mission is, on the one hand, to prepare and train personnel of the Armed Forces and Public Order and Security, as well as civilians, who must participate in Peace Operations; on the other hand, to provide advice to the Minister of National Defense on all matters related to Peace Operations and to conduct studies and research related to such operations. CECOPAC is also a tool for cooperation with other nations and trains foreign personnel, through external funding, for United Nations peacekeeping missions

[1046] Two years later, in July 2002, Chile created the Joint Center for Peacekeeping Operations of Chile (CECOPAC

[1047] In February 2004, our country gave further proof of its commitment to world peace by responding to a Security Council resolution to promptly respond to Haiti to help prevent a humanitarian crisis and stabilize the country

[1048] Pursuant to Security Council Resolution No. 1,529 of February 29, 2004, which authorized the deployment of the Interim Multinational Force (MIFH), Chile sent a contingent of 331 troops to Haiti within 72 hours to complete the international force, along with the United States, France, and Canada

[1049] Having met the MIFH's objective of reducing violence and granting the United Nations sufficient time to deploy a mission of "blue helmets," the Security Council approved resolution 1,542 in April 2004 to create the United Nations Stabilization Mission in Haiti (MINUSTAH), in which Chile has continued to participate to date with a broader participation, both in personnel and resources

[1050] In order to concentrate the operational command of the peacekeeping force deployed in MINUSTAH, it was decided in May 2004 to invest the Chief of the National Defense Staff with the status of National Military Authority (ANAMIL

[1051] It is worth remembering that, at the regional level, Chile's experience in cooperation for international peace and security was enriched early on by its incorporation, in 1935, into the Neutral Military Commission that oversaw the disengagement of Bolivian and Paraguayan forces at the end of the Chaco War

[1052] Subsequently, the participation of Chilean military observers in the multinational force formed to oversee the agreements between Ecuador and Peru following the conflict that confronted them in 1995 is highlighted, within the context of the actions of the guarantor countries (MOMEP) established by virtue of the mandate of the Itamaraty Peace Declaration (Brazil

[1053] Chart 12 illustrates Chile's presence in peacekeeping operations until December

[1054] Finally, Chile consolidated its commitment to collaboration with the international community organized within the United Nations through the enactment, in 2008, of Law No. 20,297, which modifies Law No. 19,067 and expressly regulates the participation of Chilean troops in peacekeeping operations

[1055] Specifically, it establishes the procedures to be followed by the President and Congress regarding the request, authorization, disposition, and timeframe for Chilean forces to participate, alone or as part of a coalition, in peacekeeping operations, whether peacekeeping or peacekeeping

[1056] It also empowers the President to decide whether to send troops abroad in other cases, as specified in the law

[1057] GRAPHIC 12 CHILE'S PRESENCE IN OPAZ X

[1058] Defense agreements signed by Chile with other nations and with some international organizations, such as the UN and the European Union, have received various names, such as "Memorandum of Understanding" (MOU), "Memorandum of Cooperation", "Declaration on Defense Matters" or "Declaration of Cooperation

[1059] These titles identify instruments with very diverse characteristics, objectives and validity

[1060] The scope of these agreements is diverse and covers general topics such as the establishment of permanent mechanisms for political consultations on defense issues, and more specific topics such as cooperation in ministerial management; the protection of information; the acquisition or exchange of supplies; defense research and development; military service; military health issues, etc

[1061] Chile has registered commitments since 1945, although the bulk of these agreements have been signed in the last two decades, especially since

[1062] In total, Chile maintains 73 instruments of this nature with 28 countries and 2 international organizations: the European Union and the United Nations (Chart

[1063] • It has facilitated political dialogue between Latin American countries that contribute military and civilian resources to the international effort, strengthening coordination with ad hoc mechanisms for political consultation between said countries at the level of ministries of defense and foreign affairs

[1064] • It has generated strategic and political conditions linked to the commitment to international peace and security, giving Chile the opportunity to play a relevant role in the regional arena

[1065] • It has encouraged the improvement of national regulations on the use of defense resources in international operations

[1066] • It has constituted a concrete experience of coordination and support between foreign and defense policies in international matters

[1067] • Both by incorporating civilians into the deployment and by showing the Armed Forces in a new light, it has contributed positively to the civil-military relationship

[1068] • It has allowed Chilean citizens to understand and appreciate the State's actions with defense resources deployed abroad to participate in peacekeeping missions

[1069] • It has offered an opportunity for our Armed Forces to perform professionally under the conditions of a real peace enforcement operation and to acquire new skills: -- Optimize aspects of joint and combined planning, both operationally and in logistical and administrative matters

[1070] Gain experience conducting joint and combined operations. -- Develop capabilities to interoperate with NATO-standard armed forces

[1071] Encourage interaction with personnel from Latin American forces, especially at the MINUSTAH Headquarters level

[1072] Put into practice the strategic capacity for force deployment

[1073] Standardize operating procedures among military units from different institutions of our Armed Forces

[1074] The country with which the most defense agreements have been signed is Argentina (13 in total), followed by the United States (11 agreements

[1075] Meanwhile, the region with which the most cooperation instruments have been agreed upon is South America, with 27 agreements

[1076] Regarding agreements with international organizations, these primarily refer to or relate to peacekeeping operations, such as the UN Standby Forces Agreement (1999), the Stabilization Mission in Haiti, or Operation ALTHEA (a mission in Bosnia-Herzegovina), a protocol signed with the European Union

[1077] The Neighborly Relationship in Defense and Security With Argentina One of the elements of transformation of the relations between the South American States, with bilateral and subregional scope, has been the modification of the character of the relations that existed between Chile and Argentina during a good part of the 20th century, with serious border claims and strategic imbalances, and its evolution towards a model of association and integration that was configured since 1984, with the Treaty of Peace and Friendship

[1078] The structuring characteristic of the bilateral relationship has been one of the pillars, along with others, of the capabilities that both countries can now deploy in their mutual relationship

[1079] This has translated into integrative advances across a wide variety of public and private sectors, reflected in multiple instances of coordination and execution of joint projects involving regional, provincial, civil society, and armed forces stakeholders

[1080] A very significant initiative was the signing, in October 2009, by Chilean President Michelle Bachelet Jeria and Argentine President Cristina Fernández de Kirchner of the "Maipú Treaty of Integration and Cooperation between the Republic of Chile and the Argentine Republic," which expands and complements the 1984 Treaty of Peace and Friendship (Box

[1081] This bilateral instrument includes, among others, matters related to or relevant to defense and sets out the various institutional mechanisms through which the relationship between the two countries is developed

[1082] BOX 23: MAIPÚ TREATY OF INTEGRATION AND COOPERATION BETWEEN CHILE AND ARGENTINA (Matters related to defense) Chapter I, “Objectives,” sets forth the following: Article 1: • Deepen the bilateral strategic relationship; • Work jointly to consolidate a culture of peace and integration, based on common cultural and social visions, that promotes a shared legacy for future generations, through joint efforts regarding educational systems, cultural, sports, and social institutions, and the media; • Continue promoting cooperation and integration in the area of defense, intensifying bilateral dialogue on matters of international security, disarmament, and non-proliferation; In Chapter II, and among its "Special Provisions", the following are included: Article 22: The Parties agree that the Bilateral Working Group of the Combined Joint Peace Force "Cruz del Sur", created by the Memorandum of Understanding signed on December 4, 2006 by the Ministers of Defense of both States, will continue to play its coordinating role, and move toward the formalization of a binational mechanism for the political direction of this military cooperation for peace undertaking

[1083] The Parties consider “Cruz del Sur” to be one of the most representative examples of the depth of integration that has been achieved between the two States

[1084] The Parties agree that the Argentine-Chilean Permanent Security Committee (COMPERSEG), created by the "Memorandum of Understanding between the Argentine Republic and the Republic of Chile for the Strengthening of Cooperation in Security Matters of Mutual Interest", signed on November 8, 1995, and the Consultation Mechanism of the Ministers of Foreign Affairs and Defense of both States, will continue to operate in their respective spheres of action, strengthening to this end the channels of communication between their competent institutions

[1085] In Chapter VII on “Cooperation in defense production”, the following is considered: Article 35: The Parties shall develop, within a period of three years, a Bilateral Cooperation Program in defense production, based on joint projects that include land, naval and aeronautical areas

[1086] Article 36: The cooperation set forth in the preceding chapters shall be developed in accordance with the actions determined by the other Commissions, Subcommissions, Integration Committees and other mixed binational groups, with specific competence in culture, education, science and technology, environment and sustainable development that contribute to the binational integration process

[1087] The Parties may establish other commissions or working groups for the identification, collaboration, dialogue, and development of projects in areas of mutual interest, such as agriculture, fisheries, aquaculture, forestry, agroindustry, agro-foods, biofuels, the peaceful uses of nuclear energy, and space cooperation

[1088] The favorable development of the overall bilateral relationship has had substantial effects on the fields of security and defense

[1089] As the logic of the relationship shifted toward partnership and integration, the focus of the strategic relationship has also shifted toward cooperation to identify shared interests and carry out common tasks

[1090] The progress of this bilateral process in the area of defense has been based on two main pillars: building trust and developing cooperative initiatives based on coordinated policies, which support the process of physical integration and political collaboration in other areas

[1091] Not unrelated to these advances has been the fact that Chile and Argentina, following the presidential agreement reached in 1991, have sought to resolve outstanding territorial issues on the long border they share on the American continent

[1092] The mutual recognition of their respective territories and maritime spaces, and finally the 1998 agreement defining the boundary from Mount Fitz-Roy to Cerro Daudet, have provided a solid foundation for deepening mutual trust and expanding Chilean-Argentine cooperation in numerous areas

[1093] In this sense, the following can be highlighted in the political sphere: Building trust

[1094] Initially, efforts in the bilateral confidence-building process focused on initiatives aimed at stabilizing the military aspect of ties between the two countries. Subsequently, progress was made in consolidating ties in other relevant areas of defense and in institutionalizing cooperative relations, which have yielded significant initiatives such as the formation of the Combined Peacekeeping Force "Cruz del Sur

[1095] Common policies at the global level

[1096] Chile and Argentina have continued to advance their convergence in defense matters: for example, the importance they attach to the task of maintaining and building international peace under the mandate of the United Nations

[1097] This has enabled the development of military exercises aimed at achieving greater interoperability between its forces, the joint participation of Chilean and Argentine forces in UN peacekeeping missions (UNFICYP, Cyprus, 2003) and the creation of joint efforts such as the one that has materialized in the aforementioned "Cruz del Sur" Force

[1098] Chile pays particular attention to strengthening ties with Bolivia

[1099] Fundamental to this was the joint definition of the "Thirteen-Point Agenda," agreed upon in La Paz in July 2006. In terms of defense, it served as the impetus for the agreement between the two Defense Ministries, materialized in a bilateral "Work Plan" signed in Santiago in November 2006, and the "Memorandum of Understanding" in

[1100] Since the Thirteen-Point Agenda, bilateral relations have developed through broad dialogue, where the willingness of both countries and an understanding of each other's realities serve as the basis for cooperative work

[1101] By 2009, this Agenda had already achieved successes, such as the implementation of the Free Transit Regime in the Chilean port of Iquique and the development of the Interoceanic Corridor linking Arica/Iquique with Oruro-Santa Cruz-Corumbá-Cuiabá and São Paulo

[1102] This dynamic and constructive process is an expression of a shared desire to build mutual trust and expand the areas of cooperation

[1103] In addition to the presidential and foreign ministerial dialogue, various bodies actively contribute to the political and technical work carried out by both countries, such as the Political Consultation Mechanism at the Vice Foreign Ministerial level, the Working Group on Bilateral Affairs, the Border Committee, and the Free Transit Group, among others

[1104] Chile and Bolivia currently enjoy a fluid and open dialogue and must persevere in seeking convergence, strengthening cooperation mechanisms and acting jointly on issues of common interest. This will ensure that they continue to provide the bilateral relationship with greater substance and depth, as well as a clearer focus on future issues that transcend current circumstances

[1105] In short, the relationship between Chile and Bolivia is open to consensus, through broad, constructive, and unconditional bilateral dialogue, as their respective governments have committed to

[1106] A cooperative relationship with Peru is a highly relevant objective for Chile, given the geographical proximity, mutual interests, and economic development opportunities that integration offers both countries

[1107] Chile and Peru have shared historical trajectories and events, and they face the challenge of not backtracking on the ties forged through treaties and their shared proximity

[1108] Efforts have been made to foster a relationship at higher political levels, enriched by active cross-border relations, which requires clarity and understanding of the factors affecting the bilateral landscape

[1109] Indeed, today, mutually beneficial economic elements are being incorporated into the bilateral relationship, with active participation from the private sector, and cultural factors and the contributions of immigrants are being valued

[1110] In trade alone, bilateral exchange reached more than $3 billion in

[1111] Chile has become Peru's third-largest trading partner, surpassed only by two major powers (the United States and China), and Chile is Peru's main export destination in Latin America

[1112] See Chapter III “The population”

[1113] A new topic that has been prominently featured on the Chilean-Peruvian bilateral agenda is migration

[1114] Just over 100,000 Peruvian citizens live in Chile, participating in the workforce, as well as in social and cultural circles

[1115] Within the framework of its migration policy, Chile has promoted a regularization of foreigners that has greatly benefited Peruvian citizens residing in our territory

[1116] At the same time, there is a dimension of interaction between the border areas of both countries, particularly through the Border Committee, which has channeled the active participation of state agencies and the respective organized national communities

[1117] The development of a joint strategy for the Asia-Pacific is also proposed, as Chile has strongly advocated for Peru's incorporation into APEC. It also emphasizes the need to consider Peru as a player in bioceanic corridors, with elements of competition between the southern ports of Peru and the northern ports of Chile

[1118] The search for convergences and partnerships is a matter of interest to our country

[1119] Finally, it is important to highlight the many contacts between the Armed Forces of both countries, the ongoing coordination between border authorities, and, of course, the coordination between the respective parliamentary bodies of Chile and Peru

[1120] Chile believes that all the areas of relations described above must continue to advance with a view to achieving mutual benefits for development

[1121] Peru's filing of its lawsuit against Chile before the International Court of Justice in The Hague on January 16, 2008, was a deeply regrettable event for our country. It culminates a Peruvian effort to revise existing treaties, particularly with the adoption of a law on maritime domain baselines and a map showing Peru's maritime claim

[1122] This Peruvian claim ignores the common maritime boundary between the two countries, and there are no merits or grounds for challenging this issue, since Chile's borders with Peru were definitively established more than half a century ago through fully valid and fully respected international treaties, such as the 1952 and 1954 treaties, based on the respective 200-nautical-mile proclamations of

[1123] Chile has responded to this new situation by officially stating that it will assert objectively supported grounds before the International Court of Justice

[1124] Bilateral Cooperation Mechanisms with Argentina Since 1994, a series of initiatives have been developed as part of the promotion of mutual trust, a framework in which political and military relations have obtained a qualitative benefit for both countries: a. Binational Meetings of Ministers Established in the "Maipú Treaty of Integration and Cooperation between the Republic of Chile and the Republic of Argentina", it is an institutional mechanism of great importance

[1125] This is a multi-stakeholder meeting of ministers from different public sectors of both governments, which may include the respective defense ministers

[1126] The sessions are held annually and assess the progress of the bilateral integration process and adopt the necessary measures to achieve the objectives stipulated in the aforementioned treaty and in compliance with the guidelines of the meetings held by both Heads of State

[1127] b. 2+2 Meetings. In 1997, 2+2 meetings were established, comprising the Ministers of Foreign Affairs and Defense of both countries

[1128] They were created to reflect each country's desire to consolidate the advancement of bilateral relations in the fields of security and defense

[1129] In turn, in 1995, and through a Memorandum of Understanding, the Permanent Committee on Security (COMPERSEG) was created with the purpose of deepening bilateral cooperation in security matters

[1130] Its function is to implement decisions made by the Presidents and Ministers of Foreign Affairs and Defense of both countries

[1131] Among the topics on its agenda are the following: • Strengthening communication channels in the field of defense, • Providing timely information on military maneuvers that the parties agree to notify each other, and • Promoting academic activities that encourage cooperation on matters of mutual interest

[1132] In 1994, and within the framework of the 1984 Treaty of Peace and Friendship, institutional and defense staff meetings were established as a mechanism for consultation and coordination between armed forces

[1133] e. ECLAC Methodology In 2001, the Common Standardized Methodology for Measuring Defense Expenditures was agreed upon, requested by both countries to ECLAC, with the aim of establishing a comparison of expenditure as a measure of promoting mutual confidence and stimulating transparency in defense expenditures between both countries

[1134] f. Information exchange mechanism on logistics matters

[1135] In 2005, the Joint Declaration on technical, scientific, and logistical cooperation in defense matters was signed. This established a mechanism for the exchange of periodic reports on the progress of the bilateral agenda in this area and considered the planning of joint actions in the technological and industrial fields between agencies of both Ministries of Defense

[1136] g. Combined Peacekeeping Force Project “Cruz del Sur” The bilateral agreement between the Ministries of Defense of the Republics of Chile and Argentina that gave rise to the Combined Peacekeeping Force “Cruz del Sur” (CPC) project dates back to 2005 and is an expression of the will of both States to adopt a common position regarding requests from the United Nations and the international community, expressed in regional agreements or organizations, regarding peace operations

[1137] General objective The main objective of the FPC is to provide the United Nations with a specialized force to contribute to the preservation of peace in the world

[1138] Furthermore, its purpose is to increase integration and cooperation between Chile and Argentina, and to optimize the human and material resources of each country's Armed Forces

[1139] UNSAS15 System This binational peacekeeping force will operate under the United Nations Standby Forces Arrangements System, established in 1994, by which UN member states make forces and resources available to it for use in peacekeeping missions

[1140] Under UNSAS conditions, the Southern Cross Force considers a deployment period of 30 to 90 days following the corresponding Security Council resolution and the respective approval of the governments of Chile and Argentina

[1141] An initial deployment period of 6 months and, eventually, up to 1 year is also contemplated

[1142] Its transport to the mission area will be established by agreement between the UN and both countries, and it is estimated that the Force will be able to operate in the mission area with a self-sustainment level of 90 days

[1143] Organization of the FPC At the highest level, the FPC is organized by a Binational Working Group, constituted as a political direction mechanism and composed of civilian representatives from the Ministries of Foreign Affairs and Defense of both countries; the National Defense Staff of Chile; and the Joint Chiefs of Staff of the Armed Forces of Argentina

[1144] UNSAS, for the acronym in English of United Nations Stand-by Agreement System

[1145] The Peace Force itself is made up of a Joint-Combined Staff and a force that integrates resources or components from the three branches of the Armed Forces of each country (Chart

[1146] FPC Components In this military force project, the land, naval and air components will be made up of military units with similar characteristics from both countries

[1147] • Possible specific tasks In the staff work carried out to date, a series of possible tasks to be carried out by the “Cruz del Sur” Force have been planned

[1148] These include: providing headquarters staff, liaison officers, and military observers; providing a UN presence in crisis areas; preventing an escalation of violence; cooperating, monitoring, assisting, and facilitating a ceasefire or truce agreement; providing a general security environment; and providing base security for lines of communication, airports, and so on

[1149] create "Safety Areas" for the civilian population or vulnerable groups; provide security during humanitarian operations; serve as a "bridge" between a regional operation and the launch of a UN operation; and strengthen a developing OPAZ

[1150] In principle, the Southern Cross Force is to be submitted to the United Nations Department of Peacekeeping Operations by the end of

[1151] The date of its deployment will depend on any request made by the Security Council and on whether the mission requesting the Force is in the national interest of both countries

[1152] With Bolivia In June 2008, Chile and Bolivia signed a Memorandum of Understanding on defense cooperation

[1153] This Memorandum refined the bilateral "Work Plan" (Box 24) reached in November 2006 and covers the following aspects: • Establishment of communication channels on defense matters; • Professional exchanges; • Development of academic activities in different areas of defense, for both military and civilian personnel of the parties; • Exchange of knowledge, experiences, and training in the area of peace operations; • Promotion of cooperation in areas of mutual interest between the defense industries of both countries; • Development of cultural and sports activities in the field of defense; • Cooperation in other areas of defense that may be of common interest

[1154] The areas considered were the following: • Liaison mechanism • Humanitarian demining • Natural disasters • Mission in Haiti (MINUSTAH) • Academic exchange • Police Attaché • Standardized Methodology for Calculating Defense Spending, and military cooperation

[1155] Within the framework of the agreements described, Chile and Bolivia held in October 2009, at the Computer-Tactical Operational Training Center (CEOTAC) of the Chilean Army War Academy, the first “Military Collaboration Exercise in Case of Disasters 'Hermandad 2009'”, organized by the National Defense Staff and the Joint Chiefs of Staff of each country respectively. With Brazil In December 2007, Chile and Brazil established an agreement on defense cooperation, addressing matters such as the following: • Development of meetings between the defense institutions of the parties

[1156] • Exchange of instructors, professors and students from military institutions and academic establishments of the defense of the parties

[1157] • Participation in theoretical and practical courses, internships, seminars, conferences, debates, and symposia at military and civilian entities of interest to the parties' defense. • Implementation and development of defense technology application programs and projects, with the possibility of participation by military and civilian entities of strategic interest to the parties

[1158] This bilateral agreement between governments expanded the framework of the 2000 agreement between the Ministries of Defense of both countries, which created the Bilateral Defense Working Group (BDWG

[1159] Since its creation, the GBTW has become a consultation mechanism aimed at developing bilateral relations in the areas of defense, scientific and technological cooperation, and institutional cooperation between the Armed Forces of both countries

[1160] With Ecuador, a Cooperation Protocol between the Ministries of National Defense of Chile and Ecuador was signed in 1999, and subsequently, in 2002, a Memorandum of Understanding was signed that considered and expanded the matters contemplated in the Protocol

[1161] Among others, the following were considered: Establishment of a common standardized methodology for measuring defense expenditures

[1162] • Establishment of a consultation procedure between the National Defense Staff (Chile) and the Joint Staff of the Armed Forces (Ecuador

[1163] Participation of the Armed Forces of both countries in combined exercises. Military-industrial cooperation, as well as in the field of research and scientific work

[1164] Likewise, in 2006, a declaration of intent was issued between the Ministers of National Defense of both countries that addressed the following new matters: Exchange in areas of training and capacity building at the operational level of the Armed Forces

[1165] • Participation in peacekeeping operations in which Chile and Argentina participate jointly

[1166] • Feasibility of combined exercises between the Chilean and Ecuadorian armed forces in the context of natural disasters

[1167] In addition to the above, Chile and Ecuador maintain a company of military engineers in MINUSTAH (Haiti

[1168] With the United States of America The Memorandum of Cooperation on defense and security matters between the Ministry of National Defense of Chile and the Department of Defense of the United States of America was added, in 1996, to the two previous agreements signed in 1992 and 1993, relating to the protection of classified military information or material, the first, and the exchange of points of view on issues of mutual interest between the EMDN and the Joint Staff, the second

[1169] Within the framework of the 1996 Memorandum of Cooperation, a Defense Consultative Committee (DCC) was created, conceived as a body to debate the adoption of policies and technical aspects of the defense and security agendas, with jurisdiction over matters relating to the following two areas: • Exchange information and contributions to be used in the political-military consultation mechanisms between Chile and the United States

[1170] Analyze issues of policy and strategy, global security, regional security, and bilateral defense and security cooperation

[1171] The above instruments were supplemented by bilateral agreements signed in the following years: 2000: on the acquisition and/or exchange of reciprocal supplies and services; 2006: on the supply of defense articles, defense services and related training; 2007: on the exchange of engineers and scientists for the design, development and manufacture of military systems; and 2008: on the exchange of information on research and development

[1172] With Peru, the creation of Chilean-Peruvian cooperation mechanisms dates back to 2001, following the signing of a Memorandum of Understanding to strengthen cooperation in security and defense matters

[1173] This instrument created the Security and Defense Committee (COSEDE) and also considered matters such as the following: Agreeing on a common standard system for measuring defense spending; Promoting transparency and full public disclosure of defense spending; and Contributing to the gradual limitation of defense spending in the region

[1174] In 2002, the previous instrument was complemented with a Memorandum of Understanding on cooperation in disaster relief, and in 2006, with the signing of a new Memorandum of Understanding between the Ministries of Defense to cover matters such as the following: • Bilateral activities and exercises between Armed Forces

[1175] Creation of a working group for possible joint participation in a UN-mandated peacekeeping operation and development of a plan for a combined joint peacekeeping force. Exchange of mine action plans and establishment of a political dialogue on deadlines for compliance with the Ottawa Convention

[1176] These other consultation instances can also be mentioned: General Staff Meetings

[1177] Annual exchange and coordination mechanism between institutional and defense staffs. 2+2 meetings

[1178] A body for political consultation and coordination initiated in 2001 and comprised of the Ministers of Foreign Affairs and Defense of both countries

[1179] With Uruguay, In 2007, the Ministries of National Defense of Chile and Uruguay signed an agreement on defense cooperation that addresses the following aspects: • Establishment of communication channels; • Professional exchanges; • Development of academic activities for civilians and military personnel; • Exchange of experiences in peacekeeping operations; • Exchange of experiences in the scientific, technological, and logistical fields; and • Promotion of cooperation between defense industries

[1180] Confidence and Security Building Measures (CBMs) Confidence and Security Building Measures (CBMs), previously known by the generic title of Mutual Confidence Building Measures (MCMs), emerged as agreements or commitments between two or more States in the field of defense to establish actions that tended to mitigate perceptions of mutual threat and avoid surprise situations in their military relations

[1181] Its purpose was, and remains primarily, to make mutual intentions more predictable in order to avoid misunderstandings in this area

[1182] These measures do not constitute disarmament or arms control measures, although they may be part of decisions that aim in those directions

[1183] Today, the old mutual trust measures, which have been deepened and expanded over time, have become part of the broader concept of MFCYS, which extends its application to the field of security, that is, without restricting it solely to the field of defense

[1184] In this sense, MFCyS contribute to the integration and strengthening of relations between countries

[1185] In Chile's case, these measures have resulted in a series of activities that have significantly contributed to achieving greater transparency and understanding among the Armed Forces of Chile, Argentina, and Peru

[1186] With regard to Argentina, the level of relations achieved, particularly within the framework of the meetings of the Permanent Committee on Security (COMPERSEG), has allowed for the consideration of "military cooperation and integration measures" (exchange of officers in operational units; cooperation on Antarctic matters; control of maritime traffic and air and sea search, rescue, and salvage in the southern region; combined land, air, and amphibious exercises; cooperation in the field of logistics; exchange of instructors in training centers for peace operations; and integration of Chilean officers into the Argentine battalion deployed in Cyprus

[1187] MUTUAL TRUST-BUILDING MEASURES • Regular meetings of the General Staffs of the Chilean Armed Forces with their foreign counterparts for the purposes of strengthening cooperation and integration, the exchange of knowledge and experiences, and the planning of activities. • Exercises with units of the Armed Forces of other countries

[1188] • Conducting simulation exercises, seminars, and other bilateral academic activities with the participation of diplomats and military officers

[1189] • Strengthening information and cooperation mechanisms in search and rescue operations

[1190] • Exchange of information and experience on doctrinal aspects, peace operations, and other specific topics of mutual interest

[1191] • Establishment of procedures on matters of common interest among Armed Forces units deployed in border areas

[1192] • Exchange of specialized military personnel in the areas of personnel, intelligence, operations, logistics, civil affairs, legal affairs, data processing, and other areas of interest. • Regular communications between border force commanders to coordinate the activities undertaken by their units in these areas. • Exchange visits of civilian, ministerial, and military authorities, as well as units of the respective Armed Forces

[1193] Regarding Bolivia, the bilateral "Work Plan" agreed upon in 2006, the first defense-related document signed by both countries since the 19th century, includes activities that contribute to building trust between the two countries in the areas of humanitarian demining, military training, academic activities, and disaster mitigation, among others

[1194] Chile has agreements with Peru for personnel exchanges, training exercises, search and rescue exercises in border areas, oceanographic activities, academic activities, and support for Peruvian activities in Antarctica

[1195] Regional Forums on Defense and Security Commission on Hemispheric Security It is one of the six commissions of the Permanent Council of the Organization of American States (OAS) and deals with both public security matters and matters related to defense in the region

[1196] Inter-American Defense Board International forum composed of military representatives appointed by the member states of the OAS

[1197] Collaborates in identifying solutions to common defense and security challenges that arise in the Americas

[1198] The IADB provides advice and technical services to the OAS

[1199] Conferences of Defense Ministers of the Americas The Conference of Defense Ministers of the Americas was established in

[1200] It consists of a multilateral political forum that brings together the Ministers of Defense of the American continent and its founding meeting was held in Williamsburg, Virginia, United States

[1201] The Conference of Ministers is the main meeting place for the defense sector in the Americas. Its central objective is to debate, analyze, and exchange experiences on the “Agenda Items,” which arise from the proposal made by the host country and the consensus reached among its members at the preparatory meeting

[1202] To date, eight Conferences have been held: Williamsburg, Bariloche, Cartagena, Manaus, Santiago, Quito, Managua and Banff

[1203] BOX 26: WILLIAMSBURG PRINCIPLES • Reaffirm the commitment of the Santiago Agreement that the preservation of democracy is the basis for ensuring our mutual security. • Recognize that military and security forces play a critical role in supporting and defending the legitimate interests of sovereign democratic states. • Affirm the commitments made by our countries in Miami and Managua that our armed forces should be subordinate to the control of democratic authority, act within the limits of national constitutions, and respect human rights in training and practice. • Increase transparency in defense matters through the exchange of information, reporting on defense spending, and increased civil-military dialogue. • Establish as a goal for our hemisphere the resolution of major disputes through negotiated agreements and the broad adoption of confidence-building measures, all within a timeframe consistent with the course of hemispheric economic integration and in the recognition that the development of our economic security profoundly affects our security, and vice versa

[1204] • Promote greater defense cooperation in support of voluntary participation in UN-sanctioned peacekeeping operations and cooperate by providing support in the fight against narcoterrorism

[1205] South American Defense Council The South American Defense Council (CDS) is a political forum made up of the twelve Defense Ministers of South America and emerged as an initiative of Brazil within the context of the Union of South American Nations (UNASUR

The general objectives of the CDS are the following: a. To consolidate South America as a zone of peace, a basis for democratic stability and the integral development of our peoples, and as a contribution to world peace

[1208] b. Build a South American identity in defense matters that takes into account subregional and national characteristics and contributes to strengthening the unity of Latin America and the Caribbean; and c. Generate consensus to strengthen regional cooperation in defense matters

[1209] In November 2009, at its meeting in Quito, Ecuador, the CDS agreed on a series of multilateral confidence-building measures of interest to the entire region

[1210] Conferences of Senior Commands of the American Armed Forces These are multilateral forums that bring together the Commanders-in-Chief of the respective military institutions of the American continent to discuss various matters of a professional nature and common interest

[1211] a. Army: Conference of American Armies. This Conference was created in 1960 as a forum for the exchange of ideas and experiences between armies of the continent relating to matters of common interest in the field of defense

[1212] Recently, the CEA has focused on the areas of combined education and training, technology development, and environmental protection

[1213] Special attention has been paid to the regulation, preparation, and training of combined military exercises, supported by simulation systems, for the development of peacekeeping operations and disaster relief operations

[1214] Inter-American Naval Conference It originated in 1959, when the navies of the continent were invited to participate in the semi-annual conference of Chiefs of Mission of the United States Navy

[1215] It consists of a biannual multilateral forum whose purpose is to study, debate, and propose initiatives for common naval problems in order to achieve greater overall efficiency and stimulate professional contacts among American Navies, fostering cooperative measures between them

[1216] Chile has hosted the event three times: 1962, 1990, and

[1217] c. Air Force: System of Cooperation of the American Air Forces SICOFAA is an organization created in 1961 and its purpose is to promote and strengthen the bonds of friendship, cooperation and mutual support among its participating institutions

[1218] Its objectives are achieved through the exchange of experiences, resources, training and instruction of personnel, and everything that facilitates the development of procedures for joint action, in compliance with the provisions of the respective governments

[1224] PERSONNEL POLICIES Military policy is a public policy understood as the set of guidelines that the Ministry of Defense directs to the Armed Forces to materialize the military norms of defense policy

[1225] It guides the work of the Armed Forces as a harmonious and coordinated whole, hence its joint nature

[1226] In short, military policy lays the foundation for specific institutional policies

[1227] Among other things, it can establish criteria or standards for materials and equipment commonly used by the three institutions, logistics and resource management procedures, standard operating procedures, joint training, and other matters that optimize the fulfillment of the institutions' common and joint tasks

[1228] The general areas or spheres in which military policy is expressed are the conduct, employment and development of force

[1229] Likewise, as personnel policies, military policy includes the professional training processes of military institutions, the creation of professional troop corps, the modernization of Compulsory Military Service, and the increasing incorporation of women in the various institutional areas of defense

[1230] The Strategic Vision of Defense Corresponds to the general condition that the State wishes its national defense to achieve in order to better serve the function of safeguarding the sovereignty of the State, preserving the territory and protecting its population

[1231] The above means, among other things, making efforts to achieve the following objectives: • Having sufficient forces, organized and trained as institutional and joint operating systems from peace

[1232] • Establish a deterrence strategy that achieves the desired effects. • Consolidate Chile's effective participation in international cooperation for global peace and stability

[1233] Military Policy Military policy derives from defense policy and corresponds more to a government policy

[1234] Therefore, the first is more dynamic than the second and must be planned within shorter timeframes

[1235] Its purpose is to materialize the military norms contained in the defense policy

[1236] This corresponds to the set of guidelines that the Ministry of Defense issues to the Armed Forces, in accordance with the instructions issued by the President of the Republic, to respond to the risks and threats to the country's defense established at the state level

[1237] Ultimately, military policy aims to respond, at the strategic level, to the requirements of defense policy and, at the same time, to lay the foundations for specific institutional policies

[1238] It also guides the work of the Armed Forces as a harmonious and coordinated whole, hence its three-dimensional nature

[1239] Military policy “translates” defense policy into guidelines for the Armed Forces and, among other things, establishes criteria or standards for materials and equipment commonly used by the three institutions, defines logistics and resource management matters, and addresses issues that optimize the fulfillment of the institutions' common and joint tasks, taking into account the resources allocated by the State

[1240] That is, military policy refers to the dimension of the country's military force, its structure and its technical and professional capacity

[1241] It contributes to the level of readiness and equipment of the Armed Forces, seeking uniformity of equipment and the development of human and material reserves

[1242] It therefore addresses the capabilities of the Armed Forces and, consequently, the budget allocation criteria and development niches for the defense industry

[1243] At this level, the core of the problem to be solved is the selection of the necessary combat force systems to fill the gap between existing capabilities and the capabilities needed to implement the country's security strategy

[1244] Finally, military policy has to do with the formulation of the doctrine for the use of force

[1245] Challenges of Military Policy Adaptability and flexibility: Promote the increase of three-dimensional and joint activities and introduce the necessary reforms to the military career

[1246] Quality: Materialized in education and training programs for professional development

[1247] Modernization: Referring to the updating or improvement of its warfighting capabilities, the rationalization of weapons system acquisitions and corresponding logistical support; they also refer to the management, maintenance, and protection of institutional assets

[1248] GRAPHIC 15 FORMULATION OF MILITARY POLICY Defense Policy Military Policy Institutional Policies EMDN State Security Requirements New roles, reinforcing their action and oriented towards joint advisory and functions. (Joint Chiefs of Staff) The formulation of a military policy (Chart 15), which incorporates the proposals of the institutional Commanders-in-Chief, must be as objective as possible, so that it is a useful element that supports and guides the conduction of the Armed Forces and their management

[1249] The processes involved require a three-dimensional approach that allows for the greatest possible rationalization and management, which, together with a common doctrine, facilitates the operational actions of institutional forces, both individually and jointly

[1250] Force management encompasses all activities related to planning, preparation, and use of military assets

[1251] CONDUCTING FORCE

[1252] Strategic Leadership In general terms, strategic leadership of the force is that which is exercised over the total of the forces assigned to the operations and considers the set of planning, preparation and execution activities necessary for that function

[1253] Basically, and without ignoring the peculiarities in which each country embodies them, there are two models of strategic leadership: model 1 in figure 16 illustrates a conception in which the joint use of forces is transitory or circumstantial (when necessary) and partial (where necessary), depending on the objective to be achieved

[1254] Model 2 of the same graph presents an idea where the joint use of forces responds to a permanent and global conception of the whole, without canceling out the existence of individual operations that propose partial objectives

[1255] In the 2002 National Defense Book, there are already antecedents of an orientation of national defense towards an impulse to the joint in the title “Considerations on the joint issue” and the following sections: “Some concepts”, “Joint conduction”, “Joint operations” and “Conclusions on the joint”

[1256] Since the appearance of LDN 2002, the drive for joint action has continued to develop in the relevant bodies with the aim of designing a strategic management concept similar to that expressed in Model

[1257] Figure 17 shows a model of strategic force management acting jointly

[1258] GRAPHIC 17 STRATEGIC FORCE MANAGEMENT MODEL PROPOSED MANAGEMENT President of the Republic Minister of National Defense (“through the”) STRATEGIC MANAGER Joint Chiefs of Staff Advisory Bodies CJE CJA CJFA JEMC Joint Staff Joint Maneuver Bodies Political Level Strategic Level Operational Level Non-Joint Maneuver Bodies Ops

[1259] Joint In turn, the model contemplates a collegiate military body – in the case of Chile, the Joint Commanders-in-Chief – as an advisory body to the Minister of National Defense on common matters of the Armed Forces related to the development and use of military means

[1260] Joint Doctrine As a result of the experiences gained from the war conflicts of the last century, national defense policy has adopted joint leadership as the strategic method of conducting military forces

[1261] This means that the concept of the employment of military assets is joint at the strategic level, although at the operational and tactical levels it is possible to execute maneuvers or operations that employ only land, naval, or air assets. To implement this decision, a set of doctrinal documents covering the different areas of action necessary to guide the concept of the joint employment of assets is currently being developed

[1262] This includes rules relating to planning, command and control, operations, intelligence, logistics, personnel, organization and training

[1263] Considered as a whole, these documents form what has been called the “joint doctrine”

[1264] As a doctrine, it also requires consideration of the principles that guide the use of the resources available to the Chilean Armed Forces, which aim to form, foster, and maintain unity of thought and action to achieve the intended objectives

[1265] The application of the joint doctrine will prevail over institutional doctrines on the use of resources

[1266] This means that subsidiary doctrines must be adapted to ensure consistency with the joint doctrinal publications adopted

[1267] However, the prevalence of joint doctrine does not encroach on the institutions' authority to define the foundations on which the use of their own means is based

[1268] Joint action ultimately consists of combining land, naval, and air forces under a single command for the execution of military operations. This presupposes, of course, some requirement for interoperability that allows forces of different natures to act as a harmonious whole

[1269] In this sense, the search for strategic, operational, and tactical solutions involves integrating different capabilities and equipment, according to the established plan, in order to achieve results that are both effective in terms of impact and timeliness, and efficient in terms of the use of resources

[1270] As stated, a fundamental element for joint leadership is interoperability, which allows the leadership of forces from different institutions and their operational complementarity (Box

[1271] BOX 28: INTEROPERABILITY Interoperability should be understood as the ability of systems, units or forces to deliver or receive services from other systems, units or forces, and to use these shared services efficiently

[1272] It strengthens the force's ability to operate with forces from other defense institutions or with forces from other countries in order to achieve synergies that enable the most efficient and effective achievement of a specific objective

[1273] Interoperability is a basic requirement for the execution of joint military operations or combined forces, and is primarily manifested in technological, doctrinal, and procedural compatibility

[1274] The Battlespace The “battlespace” refers to the environment, factors, and expected conditions that must be considered to successfully apply the combat power of the military assets employed to achieve an objective

[1275] The concept goes beyond the strictly terrestrial, maritime, or air geographical perspectives on which military doctrine was traditionally based

[1276] Concepts such as land battlefield, maritime domain, and airspace have evolved in recent years to integrate into a single battlespace that, in addition to the aforementioned environments, also encompasses the electromagnetic spectrum and the modern notion of cyberspace

[1277] These characteristics, combined with the resources of different institutions and capabilities, make its administration and management a fundamental priority. This requires a command and control system and extensive use of the concept of interoperability by all forces present, the only way to achieve control of the different areas that comprise it and the protection of the forces operating within it

[1278] It is, therefore, a concept that represents the environment where military operations will be carried out in a conflict situation under the three-dimensional modality or joint use of forces (chart

[1279] GRAPHIC 18 BATTLESPACE AIR POWER LAND POWER NAVAL POWER JOINT CAPABILITIES XXX Need for Joint Capabilities This concept of battlespace assumes the existence of joint capabilities in the armed institutions, without this hindering the availability of capabilities specific to each institution to respond to specific demands

[1280] The development of joint capabilities must be functional to strategic leadership, considering that the joint employment of force is permanent and comprehensive

[1281] In developing capabilities, military institutions take into account the nature of their function, the characteristics of their resources, and the physical setting in which they are to be used

[1282] All of this is expressed, as shown in Figure 19, in a trilogy of factors composed of the setting, the control of that physical setting, and the power or force required to exercise that control

[1283] GRAPHIC 19 ELEMENTS CONCURRENT IN THE BATTLESPACE LAND SEA AIR FORCE FORCE FORCE CONTROL CONTROL CONTROL SCENARIO POWER CONTROL BATTLEFIELD SEA CONTROL AIRPOWER BATTLESPACE The concept that the Army is implementing to account for the above is that of the “battlefield.”

[1284] In the case of the Navy, it corresponds to “sea control” and for the Air Force it is “air power.”

[1285] These concepts have the difficulty that they are not completely interchangeable, since they are not homologous

[1286] In the Army's concept of the "battlefield," the emphasis is on a physical, dynamic, and multidimensional space, where its resources must operate to operationally control it

[1287] The concepts of the Navy ("sea control") and the Air Force ("air power") also consider the operational use of their assets, but with an emphasis on other elements of the scenarioforce-control trilogy

[1288] In the case of the Navy, "sea control" emphasizes the control function, but gives this function a broader scope, as it includes both naval (operational) control and maritime control (the functions of monitoring all activities carried out in maritime areas under national responsibility

[1289] In the case of the Air Force, "air power" emphasizes the element of force, but it is a concept that participates in the more general notion of national “aerospace power,” and therefore the control function also assumes a broader scope than operational control

[1290] This means that in the scenario-force-control trilogy that materializes the tasks and missions of the armed institutions according to the nature of each one's specific weapon, the force factor, which is common to all three military institutions, is the element from which joint capabilities must be developed that, in a conflict situation, will allow their means to operate among themselves under a single command

[1291] The "control" factor of the physical space where own or joint military means operate is also similar in the three institutional perspectives, although the scope of control is broader in some cases than in others

[1292] But this factor consists of a function that is executed and that requires force to materialize

[1293] Consequently, the "force" element is what combines and integrates the three institutional approaches to the challenges of the battlespace and allows them to be developed as joint capabilities (Figure

[1294] On the control function, see also Chapters XVIII, XIX and XX

[1295] Joint capabilities are developed through harmoniously conceived and developed force systems through the integration, in the necessary proportion, of land, naval, and air forces, concentrating efforts on what is essential, pursuing effectiveness and complementarity, and avoiding redundancies. This facilitates mutual cooperation, since in joint employment, each of them offers synergies to the other forces thanks to the availability of technical means capable of closely coordinating their actions, something that was not always the case in the past

[1296] This complementarity and synergies offer the best results when each component has its appropriate weight within the whole, depending on the physical setting in which they will operate and the objective being pursued

[1297] Ground Theater Ground Control Air Control Airspace Maritime Theater Sea Control Force Force Force JOINT CAPABILITIES BATTLESPACE MILITARY POLICY Battlespace Approaches a. Battlefield The battlefield is a concept that allows the Army to establish where and how the combat power of a ground force is effectively applied in the battlespace

[1298] It includes all tangible and intangible dimensions materialized in the front, depth, height, cyberspace and electromagnetic spectrum

[1299] The concept of "battlefield" is currently used by the Army as a forward-looking tool

[1300] Consider the construction of a virtual scenario that visualizes the development, results, and future consequences of the execution of land operations as a whole

[1301] The results of the “battlefield” design constitute the conceptual framework that supports a subsequent activity, called “battlefield dynamics study.”

[1302] Despite being a virtual tool, it is necessary that the concept of "battlefield" be captured in a physical setting at some point in the operations planning process

[1303] In this way, the concept of the "battlefield" helps support the development of the force's operational doctrine, provides guidance regarding training, and governs development planning

[1304] Ultimately, the exercise of visualizing the battlefield, understanding the different variables that interact, the capabilities that confront each other, the effects that occur, and the actions that can be taken, is of vital importance for the military leader

[1305] b. Control of the sea The particular characteristics of the sea, such as its large size, varied use and different jurisdictional powers exercised over it, make its total and complete control difficult to achieve

[1306] Therefore, the concept of "sea control" refers to obtaining a certain degree of control over the activities carried out in a maritime space of interest, for a specific period of time

[1307] Control of the sea in a conflict situation will be oriented towards enabling friendly forces to use the sea and coastal space free of threat, denying its use by the adversary and projecting the State's military power from the sea based on the established battle space

[1308] Depending on the purpose to be achieved by exercising control of the sea, the Navy will act jointly with other branches of the Armed Forces, with foreign navies, or with other State organizations

[1309] Depending on this purpose, the presence of naval forces, both those that project power and those that protect the territory, will vary in the quantity and type of resources assigned, as well as their mode of employment

[1310] Air power Air power is the force element of aerospace power that is employed in the battlespace to use it to one's advantage and deny it to the adversary

[1311] It is represented by the sum of the weapons systems used by the Air Force to combat the enemy and includes all the support activities necessary for their use and the personnel trained to operate them

[1312] Air power has the ability to act deep within the battlespace

[1313] That is to say, due to its characteristics, air power can carry out individual operations, in the sense that only air assets are considered, but in support of the ultimate joint strategic objective

[1314] Among the characteristics of air power are the ability to graduate its application, its rapid deployment to distant theaters, and the ability to leverage the strengths of one's own forces and exploit the weaknesses of the adversary

[1315] Now, air power is made up of elements that allow the results of its use to be enhanced

[1316] Some of these include: a logistics system capable of supporting the force in a sustained, timely manner, and in the required quantity and quality; accurate and timely intelligence for defining the air strategy and correctly selecting targets; and finally, a command and control system that allows for single command and centralized control for the decentralized execution of air operations

[1317] MILITARY POLICY The natural projection of airspace is extraterrestrial space

[1318] The acquisition of capabilities to operate in this area has a multiplying effect on air power

[1319] Conclusion Battlespace dominance is central to joint doctrine, primarily due to the nature of today's operations, the range and precision of weaponry, and the need to concentrate all available potential with maximum effectiveness

[1320] Joint action is enhanced by technological advancement, current means of information and control, and the importance of a single command

[1321] Finally, in order to facilitate the command, coordination, support, and conduct of forces, the concept of battlespace is physically expressed in the theater of war, which may be composed of two or more theaters of operations

[1322] MILITARY POLICY Chile is a country that prioritizes law over force and, consequently, respects the international legal system that governs the use of force

[1323] For this reason, Chile has decided to have a military power capable of satisfactorily fulfilling the definitions made regarding its use in national defense policy: legitimate defense, deterrence, and international cooperation

[1324] EMPLOYMENT OF FORCE XIII

[1325] Legitimate Defense The Chilean State recognizes as situations of legitimate use of military force, both individually and collectively, those established within the framework of the Charter of the United Nations

[1326] In Article 51, the Charter recognizes every State's inherent right to self-defense, individually or collectively, in the event of an armed attack against it

[1327] BOX 29: BASIS OF SELF-DEFENSE (United Nations Charter, Article 51) "Nothing in this Charter shall impair the inherent right of individual or collective self-defense in the event of an armed attack against a Member of the United Nations, until the Security Council has taken the necessary measures to maintain international peace and security

[1328] Measures taken by Members in the exercise of the right of self-defense shall be immediately communicated to the Security Council and shall not in any way affect the authority and responsibility of the Council under the present Charter to take at any time such action as it deems necessary to maintain or restore international peace and security

[1329] MILITARY POLICY By virtue of the Charter of the United Nations and other treaties signed and ratified by our country, Chile reaffirms its commitment to peace and its determination to abide by said international regulations and apply the principle of peaceful resolution of disputes

[1330] In exercising the right to self-defense, Chile will always act in accordance with international humanitarian law and the general rules of national law applicable in these circumstances

[1331] In the case of the right to self-defense, the use of military force could be materialized in two ways: The potential (coercive) use of military force: this is exercised against a potential adversary through the capabilities of the means of defense, although without resorting to the use of violence, with the purpose of avoiding an aggression or possible effective use of the opponent's force

[1332] • The effective (coercive) use of military force is the violence exercised, in any of its degrees, with the aim of imposing one's will in the face of an attack or danger of attack

[1333] This framework constitutes the context in which national defense policy and the strategic attitude in the use of force are oriented

[1334] Deterrence The concept of deterrence encompasses both an effect and the action that causes it

[1335] As an effect, deterrence corresponds to a psychological and subjective dimension that occurs in a potential adversary

[1336] That is, deterrence does not aim to paralyze all actions contrary to the national interest, but rather to generate in the potential adversary the conviction that the cost of coercively interfering against one's own vital interests will be higher than the benefits to be obtained

[1337] Deterrence could therefore be defined as a State's strategy to inhibit a potential adversary from acting coercively against its own vital interests and to influence it to adopt non-coercive courses of action

[1338] MILITARY POLICY As an action, deterrence is likely to be materialized through a State's strategy to achieve the effect described above

[1339] This allows you to conceive a goal or purpose and how to materialize it

[1340] For this reason, a strategy of this nature must focus on the development of national capabilities, particularly those of a military nature

[1341] This makes deterrence a credible action

[1342] However, in the strategy it defines, the State assigns functions primarily, though not exclusively, to the defense sector and the foreign relations sector

[1343] In other words, it cannot be deterred without the existence of military force, but it is ultimately deterred by virtue of the political-strategic stature that the country has achieved

[1344] Deterrence helps stabilize international relations by persuading parties not to resort to force to impose solutions in the event of a conflict

[1345] It should be noted that the concept of deterrence referred to here refers to conventional deterrence, since Chile maintains its international commitments regarding the non-proliferation of weapons of mass destruction

[1346] Since the ratification of the United Nations Charter, Chile has participated with military means in international cooperation activities for the maintenance of peace and security in the world

[1347] During the last decade of the 20th century, the Chilean State increased its contribution quantitatively and qualitatively

[1348] This is because the Chilean State views this collaboration with the international community as a form of force

[1349] Although Chile decides to incorporate its defense resources into international peacekeeping missions based on the principles of international responsibility and solidarity, also considering the case-by-case study of the merits of such operations, already in the 2002 National Defense Book, international defense cooperation acquired a relevance equivalent to that of the concept of "deterrence

[1350] MILITARY POLICY In a globalized world, Chile understands that, although its security depends essentially on its most immediate strategic environment, the importance of its security relations with other international actors and the influence of an international agenda less tied to an exclusively territorial definition of security have increased

[1351] Hence, Chile does not limit its defense policy to the protection of its territory, which is otherwise fully in force

[1352] It has been enriched by the openness of its economy, the process of reforming international political institutions, where the country participates in the dynamics of globalization, and the growing importance of international crises originating in intrastate conflicts or non-state threats

[1353] Cooperation does not mean minimizing or changing the functions and missions that the Armed Forces have developed and fulfilled throughout their history, but rather directing part of their efforts toward collaborating with the global community's goal of creating secure international environments and sustaining the drive toward integration

[1354] Similar to the effects of deterrence, international cooperation contributes to stabilizing international relations, not only by mitigating conflicts but also by preventing them (Box

[1355] BOX 30: DETERRENCE AND COOPERATION

[1356] Although the concepts of deterrence and cooperation are part of the universally adopted language of defense, there has been controversy as to whether, when embodied in policies, they are compatible

[1357] However, deterrence and cooperation are not only compatible, they coexist

[1358] Deterrence favors non-forceful solutions in the event of conflict, and it is precisely the stabilization of relations between States that enables the establishment of cooperative relationships

[1359] On the other hand, when two or more countries participate in cooperative action, each country gains insight into the other's capabilities, which, if positive, has a deterrent effect

[1360] GRAPHIC 21** **LINK BETWEEN DETERRENCE AND COOPERATION** PEACE OPERATIONS INDIVIDUAL DEFENSE COLLECTIVE DEFENSE (NATO

[1361] Figure 21 expresses the possibility of deterrence and cooperation coexisting as modalities of use of force by the same country

[1362] The Cold War provided clear examples where deterrence between defensive blocs (the Atlantic Alliance and the Warsaw Pact) coincided with cooperative policies between partners from both blocs that were as sensitive to security as energy supplies

[1363] Furthermore, examining such examples, it is possible to observe that the articulation of forces (cooperation) within a defensive bloc coexisted with significant doses of internal deterrence or even developments of nuclear force (deterrence) over which a State reserved the sovereign decision of its coercive (deterrent) use against anyone (including its partners

[1364] MILITARY POLICY** Consequently, Chile’s defense policy considers the following modalities of use of force in a context of international cooperation: International Cooperation Operations a. Peace Operations The term “Peace Operations” (OPAZ) is not mentioned in the Charter of the United Nations

[1365] However, the practice of these operations, since 1945, has created a number of universally accepted standards, forming the regulatory basis for their implementation

[1366] The very employment of operational capabilities in OPAZ requires imposing a set of guidelines, issued by the competent superior authority, that outline the circumstances and limitations under which the force will initiate or continue an encounter with other forces, which together are referred to as rules of engagement (ROE
