[1370] LAW 20.297 ON ENTRY AND EXIT OF TROOPS

[1371] On December 13, 2008, Law No. 20,297 was published in the Official Gazette. This law introduces amendments to Law No. 19,067, which establishes permanent regulations on the entry of foreign troops into the territory of the Republic and the exit of national troops from the same and which constitutes the concretization of national efforts to regulate national participation in peace operations, by including a new paragraph 2 to Title II, specifically intended to regulate the "Exit of troops to participate in Peace Operations

[1372] In the context of the above, it is necessary to highlight the following elements: A flexible and periodic mechanism for reporting to the National Congress is specifically established, not only regarding the missions in which our country participates, but also regarding all operations in progress, in order to account for the international panorama in this matter

[1373] • The creation of the Interministerial Commission on Peace Operations is hereby established to permanently advise the Ministers of Foreign Affairs and National Defense on the matter and serve as a consultative and working body

[1374] The procedure by which the President of the Republic requests a ruling from the Senate, either to initiate national participation in a specific operation or to request its extension, is regulated. This procedure is rigorously detailed in the supporting documentation that must accompany it, so that the Upper House can issue a ruling with as much information as possible and available

[1375] It expressly regulates the use of combined forces in peace operations, that is, forces composed of contingents from Chile and one or more countries acting jointly in the same theater of operations

[1376] In this case, the withdrawal of our troops will be subject to the condition precedent that the third States with whose troops we participate jointly have authorized the withdrawal of their own contingents

[1377] This, and to safeguard the principle of sovereignty, without prejudice to Chile's decision to independently participate in the peacekeeping operation for which the combined force was intended. • It provides for a mechanism for rapid authorization for the withdrawal of troops in three exceptional cases

[1378] In the face of these exceptional situations, which cannot be subject to the normal procedures of Title II, the Senate must rule, in accordance with the provisions of paragraph 10 of Article 53 of the Political Constitution, within a period established by the bill: 48 hours from the date the President of the Republic submits his request

[1379] Establishes the presidential power to revoke authorization for the entry or exit of troops at any time, by supreme decree issued through the Ministry of National Defense and signed by the Minister of Foreign Affairs

[1380] It is also stipulated that the return of national troops must be carried out as soon as possible if necessary, and in any case within one year

[1381] It provides a legal definition of the concept of troops, meaning "all military personnel, armed or unarmed, regardless of their number, organization, or modality, who enter or leave the national territory to perform acts of service

[1382] Chapters VI and VII of the United Nations Charter establish the legal basis for carrying out Peace Operations, and two general lines can be identified: peacekeeping operations (associated with Chapter VI) and peace enforcement operations (associated with Chapter VII

[1383] • Peacekeeping Operations (PKO) PKOs (Peacekeeping Operations), or OPAs, can include missions such as military observers, peacekeeping forces, or a combination of both

[1384] In general, these are missions whose main objective is to stop or defuse a conflict between two or more states, keeping the belligerents separated and stabilizing the situation

[1385] Military observer missions are staffed by unarmed officers, and peacekeeping forces carry weapons, but only for self-defense

[1386] The coercive use of force by deployed troops is authorized only in this case: self-defense

[1387] Essentially, these operations are carried out to verify truce agreements reached between belligerent parties, requiring the deployment of peacekeepers to support diplomatic efforts to establish a political settlement

[1388] Among the tasks that military peacekeeping forces must perform are deterring those who wish to oppose the truce agreements, ensuring compliance with demobilization and disarmament, cooperating in the rehabilitation and reintegration of combatants, as well as other functions related to the reconstruction of the State and its institutional capacities

[1389] The strategic concept of United Nations peacekeeping operations has evolved as the nature of conflicts has changed over the years

[1390] Indeed, peacekeeping operations have increasingly had to deal with intra-state conflicts and civil wars

[1391] Peace enforcement These are operations whose purpose is to maintain or restore peace, authorizing the coercive use of military force in situations where the existence of a threat, violation or breach of peace or act of aggression has been determined

[1392] Peace enforcement operations require the rapid deployment of forces in order to establish a significant and visible military presence

[1393] To this end, the military forces have an established organization that defines protected zones, allows for the planning, coordination, and control of military operations in countries or territories in conflict, and provides protection and safeguarding of forces against a possible attack by belligerent forces

[1394] In these cases, the international military force operates in accordance with Chapter VII of the Charter of the United Nations

[1395] The imperative nature of the operation refers to the requirement to cease hostilities even if the parties involved do not wish to do so

[1396] For this reason, this type of peacekeeping operation involves accepting potential combat situations for participating troops, including the likelihood of their own casualties

[1397] Multidimensional peace operations are operations conceived with integrated participation between civilian and military components, and aim to resolve not only the security problem, but also to stimulate development, promoting the rule of law

[1398] To this end, reliable justice mechanisms are established and the special needs of the civilian population are addressed

[1399] At the same time, the disarmament of former combatants, their demobilization, and their social reintegration are addressed

[1400] They are also known as third generation operations and involve the participation, in addition to the military component, of civil organizations, both public (police, representatives of international organizations, electoral observers, etc.) and private, all of which must coordinate their tasks based on the success of the assigned mission and the achievement of common objectives

[1401] At the military level, this requires a specialized unit within the deployed force to establish communication channels between all participating organizations

[1402] These types of operations have been conceived as a result of conflicts where the affected nation may be left in a state of failure, that is, where the State is unable to deliver public goods to the population due to the destruction of institutions and governance

[1403] Footnote 17: First-generation OPAZs involve the observation and monitoring of truces; while second-generation OPAZs involve the use of force to intervene between rival forces

[1404] • Mitigation of humanitarian crises In its most recent development, the Chilean State's policy on participation in OPAZ, in addition to recognizing the general types already described, includes the following special case: the use of its military means to prevent serious harm to the civilian population in an armed conflict zone, as determined by the UN Security Council (humanitarian crisis

[1405] National Protection Operations The manner and modalities in which the State of Chile may participate in international intervention operations carried out by Chilean forces for the purpose of protecting nationals, civilians or military personnel, in conditions of imminent risk to their lives, are regulated by Law No. 19,067, amended by Law No. 20,297, which explicitly establishes standards for the participation of Chilean troops in peace operations and includes this modality of use of force in a context of international cooperation

[1406] Given the instability and variability of the scenarios in which these operations could be carried out, there will always be a risk that the situation could evolve into violent situations, which would require the development of certain military operations to contain the situation

[1407] The Chilean troop operations contemplated under this modality are the following: Operations for the protection or evacuation of national troops

[1408] These are carried out in the event of imminent danger to the lives of Chilean troops abroad, deployed on a peacekeeping mission

[1409] They involve an increase, for a period not exceeding 30 days, in the authorized contingent in order to protect them or facilitate their evacuation

[1410] b. Protection, rescue and evacuation operations These are carried out to protect, rescue or evacuate non-combatant or non-armed persons of Chilean nationality who are in a conflict zone with imminent danger to their lives

[1411] National Humanitarian Aid Operations Chile will employ military force in a non-coercive or coercive manner to collaborate with third-party States in situations where massive aid needs are estimated as a result of emergencies not arising from an armed conflict

[1412] The concepts of humanitarian assistance and natural disaster mitigation are directly linked to this type of operation

[1413] Among the operations in which the use of Chilean troops under this modality is contemplated are the following: a. Humanitarian assistance operations

[1414] They are operations aimed at alleviating human suffering from the effects of natural or man-made disasters

[1415] These operations are typically carried out by specialized civilian organizations, and military action focuses on providing advice for organization, leadership, and control, providing security, and providing support to facilitate mobility and maintenance

[1416] b. Disaster or catastrophe mitigation operations These operations arise from the consequences of natural or man-made disasters and their purpose is to restore, as quickly as possible, the basic services necessary to sustain the lives of people in the affected areas

[1417] Chart 23 shows Chile's historical participation in United Nations peacekeeping operations

[1418] GRAPHIC 23** **CHILE'S HISTORICAL PARTICIPATION IN UN OPAZ** **MILITARY POLICY** **FORCE DEVELOPMENT

[1419] Force development is the process of defining and generating the means necessary to achieve certain military capabilities

[1420] In this context, "force" refers to the organized and trained resources, in accordance with a specific employment doctrine, that provide the necessary capabilities to achieve assigned objectives

[1421] Similarly, capability is the ability to successfully complete assigned tasks or missions, as well as to support and leverage effects, within different environments

[1422] Joint capability will exist when there is the ability to successfully complete assigned tasks or missions using forces from different military institutions under a single command (Figure

[1423] CHART 24** **DEFINITION OF CAPABILITY** CONFLICT DEFENSE WHAT IS CAPABILITY? PARTICIPATION IN A COALITION PARTICIPATION IN COMBINED ACTION JOINT CAPABILITIES INTEROPERATION CAPABILITIES INFRASTRUCTURE PERSONNEL EQUIPMENT ORGANIZATION INFORMATION LOGISTICS TRAINING DOCTRINE FORCE ELEMENTS OPERATIONAL ENVIRONMENTS

[1424] Force development planning is a central element of military policy and requires a comprehensive view of force employment to guide institutional force development plans

[1425] Development of Joint Capabilities There is consensus that contemporary armed conflicts can only be addressed through a single command that, regardless of who exercises it, adequately manages the concept of "joint action" and directs forces from all institutions as an organic whole that is structured and dimensioned according to the objective to be achieved

[1426] This is where the importance of developing joint capabilities lies

[1427] These joint capabilities refer to the existence of joint command bodies, inter-institutional education and training processes, and administrative and logistical systems capable of operating in an integrated manner and with a common classification

[1428] A step toward fully achieving these capabilities is the existence of a joint doctrine and command and control systems that allow for exercising control over the forces participating in a joint exercise

[1429] This development requires support, in general, on the concept of interoperability

[1430] In the national case, the ongoing modernization processes in the defense sector require, among other developments, the implementation of a planning cycle that contains significant elements aimed at strengthening current initiatives and those that need to be implemented to achieve higher levels of joint capability

[1431] In addition to the efforts made up to the publication of the Defense Book in 2002, we must add the initiatives promoted based on the experiences Chile has gained in the field of joint exercises and international peacekeeping operations since then

[1432] Particularly in this last area, the participation of Chilean military forces in the United Nations Stabilization Mission in Haiti (MINUSTAH) has provided valuable lessons in joint matters

[1433] Among the most recent advances are the following: Creation of the Northern Joint Training Center

[1434] • Development of joint command and control systems

[1435] Centralization of planning and execution of joint exercises within the National Defense General Staff

[1436] • Development of joint doctrine

[1437] • Establishment of the Joint Cataloging Office • Improvement in education, adding new training opportunities to the joint periods at the War Academies

[1438] • Joint conception and execution of international peace operations

[1439] • Technological development projects in the area of joint military application

[1440] • Creation of the Joint Chiefs of Staff (EMC

[1441] Development of the land force The Army is undergoing a transformation process to modify its structure, reorganize its forces, adapt its training, and reformulate institutional management

[1442] From 2002 onwards, the process gained renewed momentum and the first major objectives in the reorganization of the force were achieved (Figure

[1443] In this context, it became imperative to conceive and develop a strategic plan that would reflect an updated design for developing a ground force appropriate to the new realities of doctrine, structure, and technology

[1444] The doctrine was reformulated to provide intellectual support and guide the organization in new military procedures and competencies

[1445] The force structure was designed to support new capabilities and potential

[1446] Technology was incorporated to make the use of ground forces more efficient and effective

[1459] Naval Force Development The Navy defined its strategic objectives for 2010 through its institutional planning process

[1460] The main one has been the development of capabilities to fulfill their roles and functions

[1461] In this context, surface units, aircraft, submarines, and maritime patrol vessels were renewed through various programs and projects, supported by initiatives aimed at improving the efficiency and effectiveness of allocated resources

[1462] This modernization prioritizes defense capabilities to act jointly with other institutions of the Armed Forces and in conjunction with other foreign navies. These efforts also consider developing capabilities to exercise institutional responsibilities in the areas of safeguarding and monitoring national maritime interests, ensuring the safety of life at sea, and conducting scientific research in both hydrographic and oceanographic matters

[1463] The Navy recognizes that the nature of threats affecting the maritime arena is dynamic and, therefore, will impact the design of forces that will be required to operate in the future

[1464] According to this reality, capacity development is a continuous process that determines the major objectives of institutional strategic planning, which is structured in the following three management levels (Chart 27): The “Ocean” Directive, which defines the permanent mission of the Navy, its institutional vision and the strategies necessary to achieve the identified strategic objectives

[1465] It covers a period of twelve years and the Directive is currently in force until

[1466] The “Admiral” Plan, which guides the pursuit of the objectives established in the “Ocean” Directive by defining partial objectives and policies for each area

[1467] This Plan is projected over four years

[1468] GRAPHIC 27** **NAVY DEVELOPMENT PLANS** Admiral Directive Plan Activities Mission Vision Objectives 12 years 4 years Partial Objectives Policies 1 year Tasks Resources Ocean Directive • The Annual Activities Plan, which establishes the specific tasks and assigns resources to the “administrative commands”, within the framework of each calendar year, with the aim of moving towards achieving the objectives defined in the aforementioned higher planning

[1469] Strategic Management Support System The Navy has made a significant effort to optimize efficiency, effectiveness, and transparency in the use of available resources, evolving toward a Strategic Management Support System

[1470] It is a system based on a management methodology for non-profit organizations that has allowed the design of a structure designed to increase the degree of alignment between strategic planning and its corresponding execution

[1471] This is achieved by periodically comparing what was planned with what was actually done, and the results are used to feed back into the planning process

[1472] Development of the Air Force The modernization process of the Chilean Air Force is aimed at developing a modern and powerful force

[1473] To maintain institutional potential, it is necessary to combine three fundamental factors: human capital development, technological development, and financial resources

[1474] In terms of technological innovation, it is necessary to modify and ultimately replace aircraft equipment from time to time, and to train its personnel accordingly

[1475] Therefore, the replacement of combat aircraft must consider, on the one hand, the completion of their life cycles, staggering them over the years; and, on the other hand, the advantage that a modern platform offers by providing a high degree of availability derived from its technology

[1476] Having more efficient equipment allows for lower operating costs and increases the time between failures

[1477] To meet the stated objectives, the modernization process is supported by the institutional development plan

[1478] Considering both the current situation and the fulfillment of the mission and strategic vision of the Air Force, this plan establishes, among others, the following objectives: Structure a force that meets the demands of modern warfare and efficiently supports national defense policy

[1479] • Develop and consolidate capabilities to achieve better integration into multinational forces dedicated to peace enforcement and maintenance

[1480] • Structure a comprehensive, continuing education system that provides the institution with the necessary personnel in a timely manner, in quantity and quality, and that is compatible with the cutting-edge operating systems. • Modernize and adapt the administration, logistics, personnel, and financial systems to facilitate management with the goal of achieving better workforce training

[1481] The Air Force's strategic development objectives, outlined in its respective plans for 2020, are designed to enable the creation of a force that incorporates advanced technological systems, such as aircraft, radar surveillance, electronic and computer systems, short- and medium-range anti-aircraft missiles, and information and management technologies, not to mention research in the areas of optical sensors and satellite imagery

[1482] The complementarity in the development of each of these areas will allow us to achieve a technological level in strength, survivability, and information that ensures predominance in the application of air power when required

[1483] From the Air Force's development plans for 2020, two major objectives emerge in the area of technological projection (Chart 28): Incorporate cutting-edge technology and generate a critical mass that allows obtaining the maximum benefit from it, and Actively participate in the development of technologies, together with universities and companies

[1486] Bicentennial Objectives Plan MILITARY FORCE AND INTENTIONALITY: ITS INDICATORS The force development plans, in the three institutional dimensions described in the previous chapter, have allowed the evolution of military means in the categories of personnel, material and financial resources (expenditure

[1487] The indicators that show the evolution of these media categories and the size of the military force help to objectify the "intentions" of the Chilean State and are, consequently, an expression of the will that drives our country in matters of defense

[1488] State Intentions and Arms Circulation Relations between states have much to do with the calculation of intentions

[1489] International treaties, whether bilateral or multilateral, aim to frame intentions within commitments that demand respect

[1490] The intangibility of treaties, recognized and supported by Chile's foreign policy, can be understood as a principle that aims, precisely, to prevent state behavior determined by unilateral intentions rather than by bilateral or multilateral commitments

[1491] Unless there is an agreement between the signatories of a treaty to revise or replace it, its intangibility is a reasonable guarantee of predictability in the international conduct of the States involved

[1492] It is important for States to publicly declare their objectives and aspirations, particularly in the area of defense

[1493] Since 1997, the year in which Chile's first National Defense Book was published, our country has stated that the orientation of its defense policy is defensive and deterrent and has explicitly established, as one of the principles of its defense policy, that it does not harbor aggressive intentions against any nation in the world, nor does it have claims on the territorial scope of its neighbors (Box

[1494] PRINCIPLES OF CHILE'S DEFENSE POLICY Chile has declared the objectives of its defense and set forth the principles by which the Chilean State regulates its action in this area in all its Defense Books (LDN), since

[1495] Among such principles, the following stands out: LDN 1997 “Both because its national objectives exclude territorial claims in the neighboring sphere, and because it has a clear and manifest peaceful vocation, Chile is a country that should not be perceived as a risk or threat by other States.”

[1496] • LDN 2002 “The State of Chile does not harbor aggressive intentions against any nation in the world, nor does it have territorial claims in the neighboring area.”

[1497] Notwithstanding the importance of declarations of national objectives and aspirations, formalized in defense policy and set forth in defense books or other public documents, and despite the development and proliferation of instruments and institutions that determine how a state can act in the international arena, the international community also considers the information it provides when assessing a state's integrity

[1498] This is because the "intention" of States remains a relatively uncertain area, that is, difficult to determine and predict with absolute certainty

[1499] Hence, it is important to display "indicators of intent" that support these statements, helping to make the alleged intentions more transparent and predictable

[1500] Consistency between stated objectives and indicators of intent is one way in which the national and international community can assess the integrity and trustworthiness of a given state

[1501] The figures presented in this chapter of the Book of National Defense are limited to data that indicate the amount of human, material, and financial resources available to or used by the Chilean State for its defense

[1502] These indicators undoubtedly have limitations, as they should not be considered a basis for measuring the country's strategic capabilities

[1503] These capabilities respond to a more complex concept related to the potential use not only of the country's military, material, and human resources, but also of other capabilities in a given scenario

[1504] Only in the area of military resources do factors such as training, the ability to mobilize reserves, logistical support, troop morale, and even the quality of leadership influence the outcome

[1505] BOX 33: ARMS RACE Although the term “arms race” or “arms race” is often linked to the military competition between the United States and the Soviet Union during the Cold War, there is no generic, canonical definition of the concept

[1506] A look at the issue should consider that every State determines the amount of material resources it requires based on the set of conflict hypotheses it faces

[1507] Consequently, an "arms race" can be said to exist when the acquisition of war material shows a tendency to exceed the quantity of resources adequate to satisfy the strategic needs arising from such a set of hypotheses, causing strategic imbalance and competition with potential rivals that results in a cycle composed of phases of searching for a new strategic parity and searching for new strategic advantages

[1508] One of the antecedents that is usually used to assume the intentions of States in military matters is to identify the presence of an "arms race" (box

[1509] In the 2002 National Defense Book, this concept was explained as the effect of an erroneous assessment of a threat that can lead to increased demand for defense and, therefore, increased spending to meet it

[1510] If this increase is, in turn, perceived by other states as a threat, what is known as an “arms race” can occur, in which each country responds with spending increases to the threats it perceives in its potential rivals, with the limiting effect, in any case, of excessive spending on economic growth

[1511] The difficulty of defining the concept can be reduced by using indicators that complement those of military expenditure, particularly in the categories of military personnel and military equipment

[1512] Thus, the existence of disproportionate increases in these three categories could be an indication of "armament" and the true "intention" of a State

[1513] Evolution of Military Personnel in the Armed Forces Chart 29 shows how the personnel of the Armed Forces has evolved between 1990 and

[1514] Considering 1990 as the base year, it can be seen that by 2000, military personnel had shrunk by 9.4 percent, and by 2009, it had shrunk by 21.8 percent

[1515] In conclusion, the Chilean Armed Forces have reduced their military personnel by just over a fifth in the last 19 years

[1516] Furthermore, the figures show that the contraction was greater between 2000 and 2009 (13.8 percent) than in the previous decade (9.4 percent

[1517] This decrease is explained both by the personnel policy implemented and by the modernization plans of military institutions, which consider, as a relevant factor, the incorporation of technology that requires less personnel for its operation

[1543] When the weapons inventory is hidden or distorted, one way to verify the "armament boom" is to measure military spending

[1544] In this regard, the latest measurement conducted by ECLAC, using the Chilean-Argentine Common Standardized Methodology, shows that the increase in the international price of copper since 2004 has not generated spending increases that could reflect an excessive tendency to increase Chile's military capacity beyond its strategic needs

[1545] On the contrary, it is interesting to note that, through efficient management of its financial resources, Chile has managed to gradually replace its obsolete equipment with weapons systems that are undoubtedly of higher technological quality, while maintaining its inventories and, in some cases, even reducing them

[1546] This reality is perfectly consistent with the explicit guidelines incorporated into our country's defense policy since at least 1995, which are concerned with the adequate sizing of our Armed Forces, but also with equipping them with better technology

[1547] Chart 34 shows the evolution of military spending in Chile between 1996 and

[1548] The concept of military expenditure corresponds to the aggregate of G1 expenditure (General or Basic) established by the Chilean-Argentine Common Standardized Methodology developed by the United Nations Economic Commission for Latin America (ECLAC

[1549] The figures calculated by ECLAC itself indicate that, in terms of GDP, G1 remained at a relatively even level

[1550] For absolute figures expressed in thousand US dollars, the situation is somewhat more uneven between 1996 and 2004, with a difference of US$54 million between 1998, the initial phase of replacing obsolete material in the Chilean Armed Forces, and

[1551] The difference rises to US$343 million, but between 2003 and 2004, the lowest and highest, respectively, of the entire period measured by ECLAC; curiously, two successive years

[1552] These figures indicate that Chile's defense spending "intention" has a reasonably consistent or stable pattern between 1996 and 2004 if military spending is calculated as a percentage of GDP

[1553] It should be noted that countries with lower military spending than Chile's, as a percentage of GDP, can nevertheless spend more than Chile in absolute terms (millions of dollars: MUS$) simply because their GDP is larger than ours

[1554] An explanation of the ECLAC methodology can be found in Chapter XXI “Financial Resources”

[1555] Several factors influenced spending behavior during the period calculated by ECLAC: among them, the international price of copper, which remained relatively low and stable; and the introduction of the structural surplus rule in fiscal spending, introduced in 2000, which limits spending financed with funds from the Reserved Copper Law by preventing it from exceeding the law's long-term capacity to generate resources

[1556] Now, unilaterally applying the same ECLAC methodology by the Chilean Ministry of National Defense for the years 2005 to 2008, as shown in Chart 35, military expenditure (G1) as a percentage of GDP in 2005 remained within the range of the 1996-2004 period, but clearly fell below the range from 2006 to 1.01 percent in 2008, that is, one third less than military expenditure, as a percentage of GDP, in

[1557] The situation is very different when measuring military spending in absolute terms (millions of dollars: US

[1558] Indeed, for the period 2005 to 2008, G1 increases significantly until 2007, although it decreases by US$ 270 million in

[1559] What is interesting in this part of the series calculated using ECLAC methodology is that the Chilean State restricted military spending despite GDP growth and greater availability of resources due to the increase in the international price of copper starting in

[1560] This decision to restrict or limit spending is what indicates the decline in G1 as a percentage of GDP precisely between 2005 and 2008, and is what confirms

[1561] Despite the increase in the international price of copper since 2004, the restriction or limitation of spending can be explained by the continued impact of the structural surplus rule on fiscal spending, but also by the elimination of the rule for distributing resources generated by the Reserved Copper Law to three thirds of the Armed Forces and its replacement, starting in 2003, the year in which military spending was lowest relative and absolute in the period measured by ECLAC, by a very different distribution rule

[1562] This new rule reflects the state's decision to channel only the legal minimum of resources generated by the Copper Law to the Armed Forces annually and to allow additional annual spending above this "floor" only with a joint agreement between the Ministries of Defense and Finance

[1563] Thus, a considerable proportion of the resources generated by the Copper Law each year are not actually spent, but rather are deposited into a special account for financial management

[1574] Transparency on Conventional Arms: UN Register Although all countries in Latin America and the Caribbean have provided information to the United Nations Register of Conventional Arms at least once since its implementation in 1992, Chile is one of only three Latin American countries that has reported annually on its conventional arms acquisitions

[1575] When examining the consistency of participation in the Register by the world's various states, it can be seen that only 43 countries have reported every year since

[1576] The other two countries are Brazil and Mexico. To fulfill this commitment, Chile annually submits to the UN data on military equipment in those arms categories where it has acquired items, with the latest report having been submitted in August

[1577] The seven categories considered in the Register are: battle tanks, armored combat vehicles, large-caliber artillery systems, combat aircraft, attack helicopters, warships, and missiles and missile launchers

[1578] In addition, Chile reports the acquisition of small arms and light weapons

[1579] See the UN Register of Conventional Arms database: www.disarmaments.un.org/cab/register.html, accessed 9 October

[1580] Chile also submits information on its conventional weapons acquisitions to the registry maintained by the Organization of American States, in accordance with the "Inter-American Convention on Transparency in Conventional Weapons Acquisitions," in force since January

[1584] In turn, the Register's Group of Experts issues its own report on the global trade in conventional arms

[1585] Progress of Humanitarian Demining in Chile In 2002, the year in which the Ottawa Convention came into force in Chile, there were a total of 123,421 antipersonnel mines laid in 198 minefields and danger areas in our country

[1586] Added to this number were the 231,076 mines that were stored in the Armed Forces depots, which were destroyed in 2003, except for a small amount that was kept for training and technological development purposes in accordance with Article 3 of the Convention

[1587] In the case of planted mines, a total of 22,631 devices have been removed and destroyed in 36 fields or danger areas

[1588] At the time of going to press, a total of 100,790 anti-personnel mines laid in 162 fields or danger areas remained to be cleared and destroyed

[1589] All minefields or danger areas in Chile are identified, delimited and marked in accordance with applicable international standards (Chart

[1590] For example, in the Register category for "large-caliber artillery systems," Chile reports each piece of artillery acquired, while the "artillery group" category used in this Book covers a set of pieces, and its composition can vary. Prior to the entry into force of the Convention, Chile eliminated a total of 93,208 additional anti-personnel mines through clearance and token destruction

[1592] As of December 31, 2008, the number of mines retained for training was 4,083. Source: CNAD

XVI

[1597] Conclusion In conclusion, the tables presented in the categories of personnel, materiel, and expenditure show a country that, in twenty years, has reduced the number of its military personnel by one-fifth, that has reduced its inventories of military equipment to limited magnitudes; a country that has restricted its military spending levels—maintaining its relative military spending (as a percentage of GDP) within a reasonably stable range since 1996 and limiting its absolute military spending (millions of dollars)—despite greater availability of financial resources; a country that spends more on the retired personnel of its Armed Forces than on its active personnel; and, finally, a country that has known how to plan the development of its force, that transparently reports its acquisitions of military material to the international community, and that honors the commitments it has made to the community of nations by becoming a party to instruments such as the Ottawa Convention on anti-personnel mines, despite the fact that, in this case, it involves a type of weaponry considered defensive

[1598] That is to say, in terms of the intentionality that could be attributed to the State of Chile, the paintings are evidence of the circumspection of our defense policy, of the peaceful will that animates, and has animated, the Chilean State, and of the invariability of its purpose to use the military means it possesses only for its legitimate defense, deterrence, and cooperation with the international community

[1599] All of the above confirms that our national defense policy does not aim to create a strategic imbalance in the region, but rather follows an eminently defensive and deterrent orientation

[1600] Although personnel issues have been a source of concern over the last decade, as evidenced by DFL No. 1 (G) of 1997, the Armed Forces Personnel Statute, and DFL No. 1 (G) of 1998, the Armed Forces Plant Law, or the legislation on Compulsory Military Service (SMO) and Professional Troops, to name the most notable initiatives, an approach that emphasizes personnel matters is needed in light of the reforms being promoted in the sector and to which government policies should be a priority

[1601] Among the topics receiving attention from this approach are career reform, including the provision of the Armed Forces, the incentive structure, the professionalization of soldiers, and the future of the Military Service and the Reserve, issues that are typically viewed from the perspective of their operational impact. In addition, other topics include the full incorporation of women into the armed forces, the participation of civilians in defense, the standardization of sectoral regulations regarding fundamental rights, the military education and military health systems, religious assistance, and the reform of military justice and the current disciplinary model, among others

[1602] This entire set of issues suggests the need to consider developing a new Personnel Management model that addresses these challenges from a systemic perspective and is fully aligned with the sector's modernization process

[1603] The development of this trend in the defense agenda has materialized in initiatives such as the approval of the bill on professional troops; other matters, such as military careers and compensation and incentives to encourage the development and retention of highly specialized personnel, are still in the early stages of development

[1604] There is also a strong need to study the institutional and general effects of greater "professionalization" of personnel, particularly in the Army, in line with technological advances in weapons systems and the new demands they place on the military profession. This will require a rethinking of career-ending processes, as well as the economic benefits that graduation entails and its impact on the treasury

[1605] Book of National Defense of Chile 2010 Chapter MILITARY POLICY XVI

[1606] Vocational Training Due to the characteristics of the Armed Forces, the profession of arms is eminently vocational

[1607] This gives unique characteristics to the education and training provided by military institutions to those who join them, since it is necessary to form corps of officers and non-commissioned officers who are homogeneous and possess the virtues and character necessary for military life

[1608] The varied training curriculum includes subjects and practical training, both in the military and scientific-technical and humanistic fields, to address the challenges faced by all members of the Armed Forces

[1609] Based on these training needs, institutional schools and academies, within the framework of the national higher education policy and in accordance with current legal regulations, have the legal authority to award specialized degrees, higher and professional technical degrees, and academic degrees

[1610] Currently, in order to optimize the use of each institution's resources and capabilities, the specialization courses common to the Armed Forces are offered jointly, according to the area of greatest specialization

[1611] Institutional Perspectives In the Army, the responsibility for training, teaching, and improving its personnel is carried out through the parent function "Prepare" and with this the institution assumes the obligation of having a competency-based educational system, in accordance with the demands of the transformation processes, whose coverage reaches all its members

[1612] This new concept of "Prepare" is embodied in the current doctrine of the Chilean Army and is essentially implemented by the Education Division and the Schools Division

[1613] In the Navy, the training process for seamen is the responsibility of the General Directorate of Naval Personnel, through the Education Directorate, and is guided by a competency-based management model

[1614] This process encompasses everything from the selection of Navy applicants to the delivery into naval service of professionals trained according to the aforementioned model. In turn, in the Air Force, the training process is directed by the Personnel Command through the Education Division

[1615] It is based on the concepts of continuing education in all specialties, through distance learning modules and in-person phases, complemented by various agreements with universities and higher technical institutes, both in the country and abroad

[1616] This type of study is used for both officers and non-commissioned officers

[1617] Stages of Professional Training In general, the training process in the three institutions of the Armed Forces is carried out in three stages, which are developed sequentially throughout the professional career: Initial, basic or matrix training stage Corresponds to the period carried out in the respective institutional Matrix Schools during which the knowledge, skills and attitudes necessary to perform as members of each armed institution are delivered

[1618] This stage is intended to provide the intellectual, physical, and value foundation to meet institutional demands

[1619] b. Technical-professional or specialization stage This corresponds to the period in which the knowledge, skills and attitudes necessary for professional performance in a specific area of the respective institution are delivered

[1620] This stage is intended to deliver the specific skills to operate and maintain the different weapons systems

[1621] c. Higher education or post-specialization stage This corresponds to the period in which higher and specific knowledge is delivered to that acquired in the specialization stage

[1622] It is intended to increase the knowledge obtained in the same area of specialization or in another area that complements the original

[1623] To achieve the training objective outlined, institutions implement a competency-based curriculum

[1624] In addition, they have a teaching and training management system with a structure that provides members of each institution, through academic agreements, the opportunity to update and improve their knowledge in higher education institutions nationally and internationally

[1625] At the same time, efforts are being made to ensure that the maximum number of officers and permanent staff are trained in foreign languages, preferably English, to meet professional development needs and fulfill the commitments to exchange, support, and cooperation made by the defense and its institutions with international organizations

[1626] All of this allows for a better-trained and ready force, whose use guarantees successful performance in our defense scenarios

[1627] Compulsory Military Service The current recruitment system for the Armed Forces was implemented in 2006, following the publication of Law No. 20,045 on September 10, 2005, which amended Decree Law No. 2,306, which referred to regulations on recruitment and mobilization of the Armed Forces, of August

[1628] Compared to other countries, in 2006 Chile adopted a unique military service model that maintains the constitutional obligation of this public duty, although applying recruitment under the formula "voluntary in principle, mandatory in subsidy

[1629] Chart 40 shows the evolution of military spending in Chile between 1996 and

[1630] The concept of military expenditure GRAPHIC 40 MAP OF PROFESSIONAL ARMED FORCES AND CONSCRIPTION Conscription is explained by different reasons, among which are certain social realities and obligations of citizens towards the State, in addition to the need to have sufficient soldiers

[1631] In general, the trend in more developed countries is toward professional armed forces, while conscription is currently characteristic of developing or underdeveloped countries

[1632] Among the so-called “BRIC” countries (Brazil, Russia, India and China), only India has professional armed forces

[1633] The system implemented in Chile is mixed, with peculiar conscription features that distinguish it from other countries with conscription-based armed forces

[1634] Book of National Defense of Chile 2010 Chapter MILITARY POLICY GRAPHIC 40 MAP OF PROFESSIONAL ARMED FORCES AND CONSCRIPTION BLUE Countries with Armed Forces

[1635] Professionals and voluntary SM ORANGE Countries with plans to eliminate conscription RED Countries with conscription GREEN Countries or territories without armed forces

[1636] or military obligation for its population GREY No information Characteristics of the Current Recruitment System a

[1637] This is done through the list of all people who turn 18 within the calendar year, which the Civil Registry and Identification Service sends to the General Directorate of National Mobilization

[1638] Voluntary in principle

[1639] Individuals who have expressly expressed their decision to voluntarily submit to military service and who meet the legal and health requirements are preferably selected

[1640] c. Raffle as a subsidiary modality

[1641] In the event that there are not enough male volunteers to meet the number of personnel required annually by the Armed Forces, the Law provides for a public and random lottery, called the General Lottery, to complete the missing contingent

[1642] d. Alternative modalities to SMO

[1643] For young men who are in their last year of secondary school, studying for a higher level professional or technical degree, or doing their professional practice and who are called by virtue of the General Lottery, the law provides the right to choose, for one time only, between the following alternative modalities: • Military Service immediately or upon completion of their studies

[1644] • Provision of services for 180 days related to their studies in those professions of interest to the Armed Forces

[1645] • Participation for up to one hundred and fifty days in Special Military Training Courses

[1646] e. Elimination of special advanced courses for students

[1647] The law eliminates the possibility of postponing military service for those in their final year of secondary school or pursuing technical or higher education studies; it also eliminates courses for young people under 18 years of age who previously performed military service through instruction on weekends and during vacation periods

[1648] Chapter MILITARY POLICY f. Change of jurisdiction from military justice to ordinary justice

[1649] In times of peace, the jurisdiction of ordinary justice applies to offenders and defaulters, while military justice is only considered applicable in times of war

[1650] g. Office of Assistance to Conscript Soldiers

[1651] There is an office dedicated to receiving, addressing, and processing written or verbal requests from parents or guardians of conscript soldiers regarding the activities involved in military service

[1652] h. National Recruitment Commission

[1653] It is the government agency responsible for supervising and controlling the entire selection and recruitment process in the Armed Forces

[1654] i. Special Accreditation Commissions

[1655] These are provincial or communal bodies, dependent on the National Recruitment Commission, responsible for hearing and resolving requests for exclusion from military service submitted by non-volunteers who have been selected by lot

[1656] Achievements of the Current Recruitment System The modifications to the Compulsory Military Service introduced by Law No. 20,045 constitute a set of advances and innovations for the contingent recruitment and selection system

[1657] Indeed, the proposed amendments reconcile the legitimate contingent needs of national defense with a selection system that, while promoting voluntary participation, is based on criteria of equality, civic quality, flexibility, and objectivity in the selection processes, in order to make them more egalitarian, objective, and transparent

[1658] Among the achievements, the following stand out: Since the year in which the new system began to be applied (January 2006), it has allowed the total contingent needs of the Armed Forces to be covered with 100% of young people who voluntarily present themselves for ordinary military service (chart

[1659] In view of the above, it has not been necessary to apply the complementary modalities of fulfilling military service

[1660] The full coverage of the annual contingent quotas with young volunteers has made it unnecessary to resort to young people selected by the lottery provided for by law

[1666] Professional Troops The Chilean defense system depended during the last century on a mixed system of permanent professional personnel and conscript soldiers/sailors (temporary recruits

[1667] In line with the global trend toward the professionalization of all military personnel, Law No. 20,303 was enacted in Chile in 2008, creating the professional troop plant in each of the armed institutions

[1668] EVOLUTION OF SLC AND SLP IN THE ARMY (2004-2010) In general terms, the incorporation of professional soldiers into the contingent presents the following advantages: • Contribute to replacing conscript soldiers with professional troops and free up a certain amount of permanent staff from certain positions

[1669] This will facilitate the performance of functions that until recently had to be performed by lower-ranking personnel, allowing them to focus on tasks specific to their rank

[1670] It increases the flexibility of armed forces to participate in international cooperation operations by allowing them to have greater availability of professional personnel

[1671] CATEGORY YEAR SOLDIERS CONSCRIPTS PROFESSIONAL SOLDIERS TOTAL 2004 18,098 500 18,598 2005 15,465 1,000 16,465 2006 13,603 2,000 15,603 2007 12,528 2,800 15,328 2008 11,711 3,465 15,176 2009 12,408 4,025 16,433 2010 (Approximate Figures

[1672] Although this law is generally applicable to all three institutions of the Armed Forces, its impact is primarily felt in the Army

[1673] From this perspective, the incorporation of this contingent of professional soldiers has the following advantages: -- It guarantees the permanent operational capacity of certain ground units, avoiding the problems that arise in this regard due to annual conscription discharges or the lack of permanent staff

[1674] It increases the combat efficiency of ground forces, allowing them to have highly trained and operationally capable units that could be present or rapidly deployed to foreseeable deployment scenarios. -- It significantly improves the possibilities of operating with other Armed Forces institutions on a permanent basis

[1675] The incorporation of professional soldiers into the Army began in 2004 through a specific institutional program, and the Professional Troops Law aims to generate a cadre of this personnel in the institution of approximately 5,000 male and 350 female professional soldiers by

[1676] Due to the nature of the role these soldiers and sailors perform, the law provides for a five-year stay in the armed forces

[1677] However, after their third year, these personnel may apply to the Non-Commissioned Officer or Specialty Schools of their respective institutions

[1678] Women in the Armed Forces The participation of women in military institutions has changed qualitatively since 2005, the year in which the Ministry of Defense issued the document "Participation of Women in the Armed Forces," which includes a set of guiding principles common to all institutions

[1679] These principles are: equal opportunities for men and women; career integration; professional competence; fair treatment; leadership responsibility toward female subordinates; and work-life balance in institutional planning

[1680] The implementation of these principles has enabled the development of a career path for female personnel in the Armed Forces without significant restrictions

[1681] Currently, women in the Army can access combat support services (Engineering, Telecommunications, and Artillery), as well as quartermaster and war materiel services, while infantry and armored vehicles remain open to male personnel only

[1682] Primary and secondary specialties are open to women under the same conditions as their male counterparts, depending on career paths and institutional needs

[1683] In the Navy, women are allowed to enter the ranks of Naval Executives and Engineers, Coastal and Supply, while the Marine Infantry and the diving and lighthouse specialties remain open exclusively to male personnel

[1684] Recently, women were allowed to enter the regular courses at the Naval School, the School of Cabin Boys and the School of Specialties

[1685] In the Air Force, all ranks and specialties are open to men and women without restriction

[1686] This covers the ranks of Air Officers, Engineers, Air Defense, Telecommunications and Information Technology, and Administration, as well as their equivalents in the permanent staff, with the sole exception of the radar specialty (due to the health risk in case of pregnancy

[1687] Within this process, there has been a growing interest in women joining the permanent staff of the Armed Forces

[1688] Based on an inclusive selection process, the Parent Schools accept applications from women under the same conditions as those for men in all specialties open to them

[1689] The same is true of female conscription, which has increased to 1,000 vacancies in the Army since

[1690] The interest of young Chilean women in volunteering for the annual selection process has consistently provided a solid pool of candidates

[1691] Likewise, the participation of women as professional soldiers in the Army has begun, with a gradual increase in available vacancies

[1692] Another area of female integration in the Armed Forces that has been opened is that of peace operations, where their participation has gradually increased in recent years

[1693] Before 2004, only five women had participated in peacekeeping operations, while since 2004, the number of women taking part in such missions has reached 20 in

[1694] It is expected that the implementation in Chile of United Nations Security Council Resolution No. 1325 on "Women, Security and Peace" will stimulate the incorporation of female soldiers into the military forces with which our country contributes to maintaining world peace (Box

[1695] BOX 34: UN SECURITY COUNCIL RESOLUTION 1325 • United Nations Security Council Resolution 1325 “Women, Security and Peace” addresses the protection of women and girls in conflict situations where they face specific needs related to sexual violence, insecurity, and discrimination; and refers to the necessary participation of women – military, police, and civilians – in peace operations, as well as local women in peacebuilding and negotiation processes, and national women in decision-making on peace and security matters at the level of relevant state, regional, and international organizations

[1696] • Chile has developed a National Action Plan for the implementation of Resolution 1,325, which was signed by the President of the Republic on August

[1697] • This initiative, coordinated by the Ministry of Defense in conjunction with the Ministry of Foreign Affairs and the National Women's Service, has embodied intersectoral work that includes military, police, and civil society dimensions

[1698] This Plan has a comprehensive vision that incorporates a gender, rights, and participation approach

[1699] This not only involves increasing women's participation in peacekeeping operations, but also developing greater gender competencies among all Defense personnel involved in this activity
