[1703] CHILEAN AIR FORCE Part The means of national defense correspond to the set of material and human resources available to a country to achieve a condition of external security that allows it to achieve its interests and objectives and, mainly, ensure its sovereignty and territorial integrity without external interference

[1704] Taken as a whole, the military institutions constitute the Chilean Armed Forces, and they are fundamentally responsible for fulfilling the missions set by the State to protect the country from external threats

[1705] This section outlines the main characteristics of each of the armed forces—the Army, Navy, and Air Force—as well as their specific functions and tasks

[1706] For this purpose, they must be organized, equipped and trained

[1707] This requires them to remain permanently prepared in peacetime to realize the deterrent nature of our defense and, at the same time, fulfill international cooperation tasks in the areas of defense and security

[1708] THE MEANS OF NATIONAL DEFENSE In order to make defense policy effective and thus materialize the defense function, understood as a collective or joint effort, the State conceives the means of defense as a modern and balanced military power, capable of responding to the demands of the various operational environments in which it is decided to use it

[1709] THE ARMED FORCES

[1710] The Armed Forces, dependent on the Ministry in charge of National Defense, are composed only of the Army, Navy, and Air Force of Chile

[1711] These armed forces exist for the defense of the homeland and are essential for national security

[1712] For these purposes, they are organized on the basis of senior commands, advisory and management bodies, and operational and support forces

[1713] As armed forces, they are essentially obedient, non-deliberative, professional, hierarchical, and disciplined

[1714] On a more specific level, the general mission of the Armed Forces is to assist in safeguarding sovereignty and maintaining territorial integrity, as well as protecting the country's population, institutions, and vital resources from any external threat or pressure

[1715] In addition, they cooperate with other countries' military forces on bilateral or multilateral initiatives, always in accordance with national interests

[1716] Toward these ends, the work of the Armed Forces is ongoing and rests fundamentally on the oath of service to the homeland and defense of its essential values that each of its members swears upon joining the ranks, as well as on the proper training of personnel and equipment for each of the military institutions

[1717] For these reasons, and due to the nature of the missions assigned, the profession of arms is considered eminently vocational

[1718] This is reflected in the corresponding legal framework, which establishes, among other things, that "the incorporation of officers and permanent staff into the plants and staffing systems may only be done through their own parent schools

[1719] THE MEANS OF NATIONAL DEFENSE Chilean National Defense Book 2010 The personnel of the Armed Forces is made up of permanent personnel (officers, permanent staff or seamen, professional troops and civilian employees), contracted personnel and reserve personnel called to active service

[1720] In addition, and although they are not part of the permanent staff, sublieutenants, cadets, cabin boys, and students of institutional schools, as well as conscript soldiers, are also subject to the hierarchy and discipline of the Armed Forces and the corresponding laws

[1721] The Armed Forces need to be prepared and trained to fulfill their missions with a sufficient degree of flexibility and dynamism

[1722] In general terms, they must be prepared both for combat and to strengthen the peace Chile enjoys and participate in the State's efforts to maintain world peace

[1723] This is why the Armed Forces cannot be conceived as institutions dedicated solely and exclusively to the problems of war, but also to the problems of peacebuilding

[1724] Their deployment and peacekeeping organization must give them the capacity to become the military body the State needs to confront potential conflicts or to take charge of the tasks the State entrusts them with in the area of international cooperation

[1725] General Functions In order to fulfill their tasks, the institutions that make up the Armed Forces perform specific, common and joint functions: Specific or Institutional Functions These are activities that, in their own and exclusive manner, correspond to each of the branches of the Armed Forces

[1726] Common Functions These are activities that, although they pursue a similar purpose, are carried out individually by the Army, the Navy and the Air Force, without prejudice to the corresponding coordination

[1727] Joint Functions These are activities that require, to achieve a specific objective, the active and integrated participation, under a single command, of resources and personnel from the Army, Navy and Air Force, or at least two of these institutions

[1728] Areas of Action a. National: integrate the national defense system

[1729] b. International: support the State's cooperation efforts with the international community to maintain peace and security in the world

[1730] c. Development cooperation: contributing institutional capacities to the country's social, economic, and environmental sustainability, as well as addressing disasters or catastrophes

[1731] THE MEANS OF NATIONAL DEFENSE The Chilean Army was born with and for the homeland

[1732] His story epitomizes not only his dedication to defending his country, but also recalls his undefeated record in the wars the country has faced in the past

[1733] In addition to the above, his contribution to international peace, territorial consolidation, and, in general, his response to the security and development challenges the country faces are noteworthy

[1734] CHILEAN ARMY

[1735] Army Vision: An Army for combat: effective and efficient in deterrence, security, international cooperation, and conflict; versatile, interoperable, up-to-date, and sustainable, with adequate management capacity, functional performance, and valued by the society it serves

[1736] Mission In order to achieve the objectives of national defense, the Army defines its mission as follows: “The Army's reason for being is to contribute fundamentally to preserving peace

[1737] Its primary mission is to guarantee national sovereignty, maintain territorial integrity, and protect the country's population, institutions, and vital resources from any external threat or aggression, as well as serving as an important tool in Chile's foreign policy

[1738] Lines of Action To fulfill its mission, the Army articulates its operational competencies and capabilities into three lines of action that give meaning to its military function and guide the Institution's ongoing work

[1739] These lines of action are as follows: Defense, Security and International Cooperation, and Institutional Social Responsibility

[1740] Defense The Army requires, as a first priority, to develop, prepare and sustain a ground force with diverse capabilities to carry out combat operations effectively and efficiently in any required scenario

[1741] The Army's combat power is centered on its members as its primary and fundamental element; on its combat training, based on an operational doctrine tailored to national reality; on the available weapons systems; on the technology incorporated; and on its capacity to sustain and support the force

[1742] a. Imperatives • Contribute to generating a high level of deterrence against potential adversaries

[1743] • Successfully confront a crisis or minor conflict, with a short readiness period and practically no mobilization

[1744] • Win a major, high-intensity conflict with mobilization

[1745] b. Capabilities • Have sufficient forces, organized and trained as operating systems

[1746] • Have some short-term availability units with great combat power

[1747] • Have short-term availability and high tactical mobility forces, capable of grouping together in a short time to create centers of gravity and threaten important objectives

[1748] THE MEANS OF NATIONAL DEFENSE • Have immediately available forces of high specialization, combat capacity, multipurpose, and great strategic mobility

[1749] • Ability to reinforce critical fronts with short- and medium-term availability forces

[1750] • Have essential air resources that provide mobility, transportation, logistical support, and armed reconnaissance

[1751] • Provide command and control, communications, electronic warfare, and information systems appropriate to the force structure. • Interoperate with Navy and Air Force assets at the appropriate levels. • Mann, prepare, and sustain the force structure derived from the aforementioned capabilities. Security and International Cooperation Based on national interest and in compliance with international treaties, the Army develops capabilities—in conjunction with other defense institutions and state agencies—to engage in security and international cooperation activities

[1752] These are new combat capabilities, as well as joint and combined versatility and interoperability of the ground force

[1753] Activities carried out in this area include operations aimed at preserving peace, stabilizing conflict zones, and providing humanitarian aid in areas affected by natural disasters

[1754] Regional and neighborhood integration actions are also carried out, as well as measures to promote mutual trust, particularly through multinational exercises

[1755] Imperatives • Promote measures of mutual trust

[1756] THE MEANS OF NATIONAL DEFENSE Chilean National Defense Book 2010 • Strengthen cooperation and exchange with friendly countries

[1757] b. Capabilities • Have essential forces, with different capabilities, located in the required geographic areas, to promote neighborhood security and integration

[1758] • Project forces of different characteristics, with short- and medium-term availability, outside the national territory to operate in international operations. • Ensure the support of the projected forces with national and foreign resources

[1759] • Interoperate with Navy and Air Force forces, as well as with forces from friendly countries at the appropriate levels

[1760] • Participate in combined exercises, international organizations, and bilateral and multilateral conferences

[1761] • Man, prepare and sustain the force structure derived from the above capabilities

[1762] Institutional Social Responsibility Development cooperation tasks are understood as the ongoing exploration and utilization of the institution's existing capabilities in terms of infrastructure, technology, and diverse resources, to carry out tasks that enable it to meet social demands within the framework of national public policies

[1763] These tasks are primarily focused on providing support in response to natural disasters; promoting traditions and protecting historical and cultural heritage; maintaining a presence in Antarctica; providing healthcare to citizens; and promoting sovereignty and social cohesion in remote areas of the country

[1764] The Army assumes responsibilities in this area because it is inherent to its institutional dimension, its capabilities, its deployment in the national territory, and its belonging to the society it serves and protects

[1765] For this reason, the Army believes that social responsibility is an ethical commitment

[1766] Imperatives • Contribute to social cohesion, development and national unity, without distorting the military function

[1767] • Act transparently, responsibly, and with respect for best practices, in line with society's demands. • Respond to society's expectations for efficient, credible, approachable, and valued professionals. • Minimize the negative externalities resulting from the Army's actions

[1768] • Support authorities and society in the field of development cooperation and contributing to national unity and social cohesion

[1769] • Have forces that have high levels of competence, are committed to the common good, and are governed by codes of conduct accepted and shared by society

[1770] • Contribute with concrete contributions to national development, within the scope of its powers, human, material and available infrastructure capabilities

[1771] • Promote a work environment that enables the effective fulfillment of its missions, providing its members with the necessary spaces for their comprehensive development

[1772] • Manage institutional resources in an efficient, fair and transparent manner

[1773] • Incorporate positive social, communication, and environmental practices. • Contribute to national unity throughout the country

[1774] Peacetime Tasks • Organize, equip, and prepare the ground force. • Contribute to deterring potential adversaries. • Participate in multinational operations mandated by the government. • Participate in humanitarian aid, international cooperation, and mutual trustbuilding measures, in accordance with international agreements and treaties signed by Chile. • Support civilian authorities and society, cooperating for development, national unity, and social cohesion, within the legal framework and without distorting the military's function

[1775] In a Crisis Situation • Contribute militarily to the resolution of the crisis. • Provide strategic cover, including its use in island territories, when required. • Deploy resources to occupy isolated and/or unpopulated areas of the territory that require protection. • Deploy resources to occupy areas that generate advantages or security for the force for subsequent actions. • Reinforce resources deployed anywhere in the territory

[1776] • Carry out protection and surveillance of vital economic centers and strategic facilities, and project forces into remote territories

[1777] Confront and neutralize any level of use of adversarial force against national resources

[1778] • Carry out a strategic deployment that will help quickly and without interference move to war preparations if the crisis escalates to the point of no return

[1779] • Project forces abroad, within the scope of international operations, in accordance with the requirements of the political leader

[1780] Contribute to the protection of the country's territorial integrity, population and vital resources, rejecting any aggression against national sovereignty

[1781] • Generate the necessary forces to sustain the operational effort and support the forces initially committed. • Defeat the opposing forces

[1782] To fulfill its missions and develop its activities, the Army structures its permanent professional work into four core functions: • Planning This is the core function responsible for the analysis, conception, planning, programming, prioritization, coordination and control of the most important processes that are developed in the Army

[1783] It is implemented through strategic, management-level planning that guides and directs overall development and action

[1784] It includes the ongoing analysis of situations that could affect the institution, the evaluation and detection of critical factors that influence processes and activities, the definition of the battlefield and likely scenarios for the ground force, enabling the required anticipation and foresight

[1785] The “Plan” function, as an institutional management level, is also the one that manages and coordinates the exercise of the matrix functions “Prepare”, “Act” and “Support”

[1786] Prepare: It is the core function responsible for keeping the Army's operational doctrine and instruction and training up to date, programming educational processes, developing professional teaching and training, defining and verifying preparation standards, designing combat organizations, and establishing equipment requirements

[1787] It is relevant in peacetime, since it provides the operational capability required by ground forces to combat

[1788] It has a cross-cutting nature, as it is present at all levels of the organization, and its results have implications for the application of the professional skills of personnel and units to execute and implement the Army's activities

[1789] The purpose of the “Prepare” function is to be found in the commanders, combatants, and ground force units

[1790] Action It is the main function in charge of instructing, training and employing the ground force

[1791] It is responsible for preparing to achieve the highest level of equipment, operational capacity, and availability, leading and controlling its actions based on the established objectives

[1792] This function is the result of the “Plan”, “Prepare” and “Support” functions of the ground force

[1793] It is where all the Institution's processes and efforts are oriented and aligned

[1794] • Support It is the matrix function responsible for making the other matrix functions viable

[1795] Thus, it is defined as a set of related and interrelated activities that transform personnel and material needs into solutions that contribute to achieving the operational capacity required by the Army in a timely manner and with efficient use of resources

[1796] It is realized through a system that integrates the following subsystems, among others: personnel and resources, infrastructure support, information management system, quality control systems, and budget system

[1797] This systemic approach is divided into two support subsystems: the first is support for the Institution's staff, providing well being and health support for staff and other beneficiaries; the second is support for the force, encompassing logistical support, administrative support, technical support, and general support

[1798] In this context, the Army has, within its structure, the following bodies (chart 45): CHART 45 ARMY ORGANIZATION CJE SGE AUGE COTRAE CESIM EMGE CEDOC COT CAF CAAE COSALE CGGERM I DE II DE III DE IV DE V DE VI DE BOE BAVE Commander in Chief The Commander in Chief (CJE) is the one who exercises the highest command of the Army in accordance with current legislation, responding for its effectiveness and efficiency, ensuring the morale, discipline and general well-being of the institution's personnel

[1799] As the highest authority, he updates the institutional vision, determines its concept of leadership, ensures compliance with strategic objectives, and assigns goals to be achieved, developing efforts on all fronts, with the aim of creating and ensuring the most favorable conditions—internal and external—for achieving the institution's objectives

[1800] To fulfill its mission, it relies on several working bodies, such as the General Secretariat of the Army (SGE), the General Audit Office of the Army (AUGE), the Army Comptroller's Office (COTRAE), and the Center for Military Studies and Research (CESIM), which together comprise the Command-in-Chief of the Institution

[1801] Army General Staff (EMGE) It is the main advisory body to the CJE, responsible for providing it with the necessary elements of judgment to adopt and substantiate its resolutions, translate them into plans and orders, and ensure their compliance

[1802] It is the main governing, coordinating and controlling body at the institutional level, in its capacity as responsible for the parent function “Planning”

[1803] It comprehensively develops all the processes and subfunctions that materialize it. It is composed of a leadership team and various departments that coordinate the processes of the various primary functions of command, development and technology, international affairs, as well as other advisory bodies that enable it to fulfill its professional mission

[1804] Education and Doctrine Command (CEDOC) It is responsible for the main function “Prepare”, although this function has a transversal character to the entire Institution

[1805] It is responsible for developing, evaluating, and maintaining operational, instruction, and training doctrine; conducting combat research and experimentation; and designing unit organization and equipment; defining and verifying training standards; and providing feedback on doctrine generation processes through the Army's Lessons Learned System (SILAE

[1806] Likewise, it is responsible for programming educational processes, developing teaching and professional training processes for Army personnel, and constitutes the final instance of verification and feedback on the standards to be achieved by commanders, combatants, and units

[1807] Land Operations Command (COT) It is the organization responsible for the parent function "Action", so it comprehensively develops all the processes and subfunctions that materialize it

[1808] It exercises functional command of the Combined Arms Units and institutional operating systems for the development of the "Actuate" matrix function, in matters related to instruction, training, exercises, maneuvers, operational readiness, and mobilization

[1809] Likewise, it is responsible for exercising command and control over the use of resources in emergency situations, catastrophes, natural disasters, and public calamities; exercising control over personnel and institutional units deployed in international operations; exercising “operational command” of the combined arms units so designated; and, occasionally, exercising the inspection function within the Institution when expressly ordered by the Chief Executive Officer

[1810] It is composed of a Command and a General Staff that allow it to fulfill the assigned tasks

[1811] Force Support Command It is the organization, at the Army level, responsible for a part of the main function "Support", in terms of material and equipment, for which it comprehensively develops all the processes and subfunctions that materialize it

[1812] Regarding the needs of the ground force, it is responsible for processing and formulating the demands for its equipment and maintaining support and material in working condition

[1813] Generally speaking, it is the body responsible for regulating, directing, integrating, coordinating, and controlling logistical support, administrative support during campaigns, and technical support within the institution, enabling the viability of the other core institutional functions

[1814] It is composed of a Command and a General Staff that allow it to lead its subordinate units

[1815] Regarding meeting the well-being needs of personnel and other beneficiaries, the responsible agency is the Army Administrative Support Command (CAAE

[1816] In addition, the Army Health Command (COSALE) provides effective access to health care

[1817] Combined Arms Units (UAC) are organizations that group forces from different maneuver and combat support units under a single command

[1818] In addition, they can rely on combat support units in administrative and logistical matters, which train them to carry out a specific military operation

[1819] The UAC Command is comprised of the commander and a headquarters, which is organized around a general staff, an administrative and logistical support group, and a security and protection group

[1820] Other Organizations Likewise, organizations such as the General Command of the Army Garrison of the Metropolitan Region (CGGERM) are part of the Army's higher structure

[1821] This Command, along with the CAAE and COSALE, are also bodies responsible for activities that carry out the Institution's own functions in peacetime

[1822] The Land Force The land force constitutes the main element that the Army has to materialize and fulfill its primary mission

[1823] It is permanent and comprises all units organized on the basis of operational systems. With their human resources, organization, equipment, operational doctrine, and organizations, including support resources, belonging to the Army, both present during peacetime and mobilized, they can carry out joint or combined military operations, serving as an effective combat instrument across the full scope of the conflict

[1824] The ground force is an element that has two connotations: • A physical connotation, which considers an integrated set of commanders, combatants, units, weapons systems, equipment, supports, command and control systems, and operational doctrine, whose purpose is to fight to achieve the imposed objective

[1825] Its development, preparation, readiness, maintenance, support and leadership constitute the purpose of the Army, since such activities guide its functioning in times of peace, crisis and war

[1826] A value connotation that refers to the moral and psychological strength necessary to achieve a level of effectiveness in accordance with the objective to be achieved

[1827] It also includes the proper treatment of opposing combatants and protected persons, which translates into compliance with the rules of the International Law of Armed Conflict (IC, also known as International Humanitarian Law). Whatever the contingency, the force's ability to adapt in combat must allow it to carry out all activities arising from military operations and requiring the application of combat power

[1828] Consequently, ground forces make possible the primary purpose of military function when employed on the battlefield

[1829] However, military operations involving ground forces range from conventional warfare to the complex range of tasks involved in military operations other than war

[1830] Therefore, from an organizational perspective, it is the responsibility of all units and agencies belonging to the Army, both those present in peacetime and mobilized, to carry out military operations, including the support resources that sustain such operations

[1831] However, the ground force does not involve a specific level of military leadership nor does it have a single command, but rather corresponds to the total of the means that are in operating condition and those that will be assigned to the different commands that are formed (chart

Robinson Crusoe 68º44' CHILEAN ANTARCTIC TERRITORY SOUTH POLE Diego Ramírez Islands “1998 Agreement” IQUIQUE ANTOFAGASTA VALPARAÍSO SANTIAGO RANCAGUA TALCAHUANO VALDIVIA PUERTO MONTT COYHAIQUE PUNTA ARENAS Operating Systems An operating system is the capacity of a given military organization, normally the Combined Arms Unit (UAC), given that it has all the combat elements to carry out offensive and defensive operations autonomously, with the appropriate administrative and logistical support

[1835] To achieve this level of autonomy, support, and protection of the subordinate units that comprise it, the operating system harmoniously develops subsystems or combat functions that define the organization of its command, intelligence, combat, and service troops

[1836] An operating system, rather than being associated with a specific unit, is linked to the concept of comprehensive operational capabilities required in the battlespace

[1837] Therefore, it can also be defined as the integration of different capabilities, knowledge and technologies that provide potential in intelligence, maneuver, logistical support and command and control activities in a Combined Arms Unit, in a balanced manner, under an operational intention, which serves indistinctly for the operational or tactical level, although it reaches its greatest expression in the latter

[1838] CHILEAN NAVY The contribution that the Navy has made to Chile, in almost two centuries of existence, is characterized by its intervention in the conflicts the country has faced, its role in territorial consolidation, and its participation in national cultural, scientific, and technological development

[1839] Its media are instruments that must be able to contribute to the promotion and support of national interests wherever they materialize

[1840] Vision of the Navy To be a Navy with a high standard of readiness and operational excellence, which effectively contributes to the security and development of the country

[1841] Mission In order to achieve the objectives of national defense, the Navy defines its mission as follows: “To contribute to safeguarding sovereignty and territorial integrity, maintaining national security, promoting national development, and supporting national interests abroad.”

[1842] Action Vectors or Roles To fulfill its mission, the Navy applies the national maritime strategy in an environment defined by three action vectors or roles that give meaning to its military function and guide the permanent work of the Institution

[1843] These vectors or roles are the following: Defense Vector or Military Role, Maritime Vector or Role of support for the socioeconomic development of the country and International Vector or Role of support for the foreign policy of the State

[1844] Depending on the state's needs, the institution articulates its actions with varying intensity in one vector or another

[1845] From each of them, and depending on the situation in the country, the functions to be executed are derived

[1846] These determine the required capabilities, which may already exist or, if not, must be developed

[1847] Defense Vector or Military Role In this role, the institution is part of a defense system, in which, together with the Army and Air Force, it acts to deter or repel potential aggressors

[1848] The Navy promotes development and joint action to achieve the most effective and efficient military strategy

[1849] Maritime Vector or Role of Support to the Socioeconomic Development of the Country In this role, the Navy, with the support of other State agencies, contributes to national development, creating the conditions for the use of jurisdictional maritime spaces safely and with respect for the environment

[1850] To achieve these goals, the Institution requires a permanent and comprehensive presence

[1851] Furthermore, in accordance with commitments made under international conventions related to the safeguarding of life at sea and maritime traffic control, the Navy must operate throughout the entire national maritime area of responsibility, which covers an area of approximately 26,000,000 km

[1852] International Vector Role of Support to the State's Foreign Policy In this role, the Navy participates in the promotion and protection of a priority and fundamental national interest: contributing to preserving peace, security and stability of the international order

[1853] To achieve this, we will necessarily act in conjunction with other armies, navies, air forces, national or foreign agencies, and international organizations

[1854] Functions and Tasks In each of the vectors or roles, the Navy performs various functions and tasks both in peacetime and in conflict situations

[1855] In times of peace

[1856] Defense vector or military role: Develop and maintain, in terms of its structure and use, effective and efficient naval power for both national and international operations. Contribute to joint readiness with other branches of defense, enabling effective deterrence

[1857] Confront non-military situations at or from the sea, such as piracy, drug trafficking, and illegal immigration

[1858] Maritime vector or role in supporting the country's socioeconomic development: Ensure the necessary degree of control of the sea, in jurisdictional and interest areas, to allow, without interference or restrictions, the normal development of national maritime activity

[1859] • Monitor and control maritime, river and lake spaces, enforcing current legislation

[1860] Carry out boarding, inspection, and search of vessels to verify compliance with national legislation and International Maritime Law

[1861] • Safeguard human life in maritime, river, and lake areas under national jurisdiction and in areas of responsibility determined by international treaties. • Provide safe navigation in waters under national jurisdiction through the preparation and publication of nautical charts, installation and maintenance of maritime signage, meteorological support, and information systems of interest to mariners. • Protect the marine environment and its resources by promoting measures that aid their conservation. • Promote maritime awareness and contribute to the development of national maritime interests. • Support the development of isolated areas and national territories remote from the continent. • Provide humanitarian aid and contribute to the mitigation of national disasters. • Contribute to the strengthening and development of the naval industry in the country. • Contribute to national sovereignty in Antarctica and support activities carried out on this continent and adjacent maritime areas. • Contribute through institutional means, in coordination with other national and international organizations, to the advancement of scientific knowledge and the best use of the seas. • Contribute through institutional means to the actions of other State agencies in the maritime sphere. • Contribute, from a maritime perspective, to protecting and disseminating national values and traditions, while also preserving our historical and cultural heritage

[1862] International vector or role of support to the State's foreign policy • Maintain naval presence, displaying the flag in the seas where national interest requires it

[1863] Participate in international operations in pursuit of objectives of common interest between States or that tend to promote global peace and security, in accordance with the requirements of the State's foreign policy

[1864] • Participate through institutional means in international exercises, visits, seminars, and academic activities, helping to foster mutual trust between partner navies. • Protect or evacuate Chilean civilians or military personnel in danger, in conflict areas, or affected by a disaster

[1865] • At the request of the State, provide humanitarian aid and contribute to disaster mitigation abroad

[1866] • Participate in international forums, meetings, assemblies or seminars in the maritime field

[1867] b. In a crisis situation b

[1868] Defense or military role: • Constitute an instrument available to the political level, considering the special characteristics of naval forces, mainly the flexibility of their use, the gradual nature of their employment, and the ability to be ready to operate upon arrival

[1869] • Deploy, in advance, adequate resources in quantity and quality that are capable of remaining and operating in a given ocean space, as required by strategic management

[1870] • Coercively employ the forces of the Navy to persuade an adversary

[1871] • In maritime areas of interest, ensure a degree of sea control that allows our national power to carry out the necessary military and economic activities

[1872] Role of supporting the State's foreign policy: • Integrate forces with other countries, in accordance with the directives of the State's top leadership

[1873] In a situation of war c

[1874] Defense vector or military role: Contribute to achieving the ultimate strategic objective of the Armed Forces

[1875] In maritime areas of interest, ensure a degree of sea control that allows our national power to carry out the military and economic activities necessary for the war effort, and deny it to the adversary

[1876] • Perform projection operations

[1877] Protect important objectives on the coast

[1878] Carry out strategic maritime transport

[1879] In a crisis or war situation, the Navy will continue to carry out peacetime tasks wherever necessary, to the extent permitted by the main effort

[1880] In accordance with its mission, and in the context of a continuous search for better management, the Navy has a functional organization, which is composed of the following types of bodies (chart 47): Commander in Chief The command of the Chilean Navy is exercised by its Commander in Chief, who holds the rank of Admiral and is the highest authority of the Institution

[1881] The Commander-in-Chief is advised by a set of direct advisory bodies, made up of officers who exercise high institutional command

[1882] These include the Strategic Planning Council (for institutional management matters), the Economic Council (for budgetary and financial control matters), and the Naval Council (for the management of institutional management matters

[1883] The Commander-in-Chief is also supported and advised by the General Staff, the General Secretariat—to which the Communications Directorate reports—the Intelligence Directorate, the Comptroller's Office, the Audit Office, and a range of management support systems

[1885] Advisory and Governing Bodies

[1886] General Staff of the Navy It is the advisory and working body of the Commander-in-Chief in all aspects relating to the senior management and direction of the Navy, performing the functions of superior planning, management control and coordination, both institutional and extra-institutional and international

[1887] Likewise, through him, the Commander-in-Chief exercises leadership and supervision of the naval zones

[1888] b. General Directorate of Navy Personnel It constitutes the highest institutional level in terms of personnel logistics, corresponding to the functions of selection, incorporation, instruction, preservation, care, promotion and retirement

[1889] To this end, it has a set of specialized technical agencies that address the areas of human resources, education, medical care, social welfare, and religious assistance

[1890] General Directorate of Navy Services It is the body responsible for the superior administration of the material, technological and infrastructure resources of the Navy

[1891] As such, it constitutes the highest institutional level in terms of material logistics, responsible for the development and maintenance of the means used and the standards that must be applied to ensure its efficient and safe use, both for the material and the people who use it, as well as for the environment

[1892] To fulfill its mission, it oversees a variety of specialized technical and logistical organizations covering regulatory, research and development, procurement, project management, and material maintenance management

[1893] THE MEANS OF NATIONAL DEFENSE 270 Book of National Defense of Chile 2010 d. General Directorate of Finance of the Navy It is the body in charge of the superior administration of the financial resources of the Institution

[1894] As such, it is responsible for proposing and implementing the Navy's financial policy, conducting comprehensive financial and accounting management, and establishing general standards and procedures for budgeting, accounting, calculations, cost control, and financial management

[1895] To carry out its functions, this department is responsible for technical bodies specializing in budgeting and accounting

[1896] e. General Directorate of Maritime Territory and Merchant Marine This is the body through which the State ensures compliance with laws and international agreements to protect human life at sea, the aquatic environment and marine natural resources, and regulates the activities carried out in these areas

[1897] The General Directorate directs, regulates, and coordinates its technical work through the "Maritime Safety and Operations" and "Maritime Interests and Aquatic Environment" directorates. It is also represented in important international maritime organizations, such as the IMO (International Maritime Organization) and IALA (International Association of Maritime Signallers and Aids to Navigation

[1898] Operational Forces

[1899] Naval Operations Command It is the body that leads, at a higher level, the process of naval operations with the purpose of achieving the objectives assigned to it

[1900] It is responsible for training naval forces to carry out the operations planned and conduct the operations

[1901] The Naval Operations Command is responsible for the Fleet, the Submarine Force, the Naval Aviation, the Marine Infantry, the Naval Transport Command, and the Special Forces

[1915] Means Understood as platforms, equipment and systems associated with the required capacity, the Navy articulates them into two categories: type organization, which serves the readiness needs of the means, and task organization, which satisfies the operational needs according to the mission to be accomplished

[1916] Furthermore, in peacetime, these forces cooperate in activities that benefit national development and the protection of human life at sea, under the circumstances and in the manner provided for by the legal system and international agreements

[1917] Additionally, the General Directorate of Services and the General Directorate of Personnel have dependent Technical Directorates, whose tasks are focused primarily on the repair and maintenance of naval assets, research and development, supply, welfare, and education

[1918] CHILEAN AIR FORCE Since its origins, at the beginning of the 20th century, Chilean aeronautics has been in constant evolution

[1919] This process has led to the Chilean Air Force (FACH) currently having a significant strategic dimension

[1920] Thanks to decades of effort, the FACH has served as the basis for the development of the various components of a true Aerospace System

[1921] Vision of the Air Force To be a modern, efficient and effective Air Force, with the purpose of contributing to the achievement of the objectives set by the defense policy of the Chilean government, for which it must continue developing the competencies that allow it to exercise, together with the other defense institutions, strategic deterrence, international cooperation and, if the supreme government so decides, the coercive use of force, contributing to achieving success in war. XX

[1922] Mission In order to achieve the objectives of national defense, the Air Force defines its mission as follows: “To defend the Republic of Chile through the control and exploitation of airspace, participation in the surface battle, and support for its own and friendly forces.”

[1923] THE MEANS OF NATIONAL DEFENSE 275 Book of National Defense of Chile 2010 XX

[1924] Areas of Action To fulfill its mission, the Air Force groups its operational competencies and capabilities into three areas of action that give meaning to its military function and guide the institution's ongoing work

[1925] These areas of action are the following: Defense Area, Development Area, and International Area

[1926] Defense Scope The Air Force aims to maintain a modern, technologically advanced force structure, capable of cooperating with other defense institutions to generate credible deterrence and, should that deterrence fail, to use coercive force to win the air war in pursuit of the strategic objectives established in the planning

[1927] This implies that the Air Force must continue developing its capabilities to control national airspace and use it to its advantage, so that if attacked, it can defeat the air adversary, participate in the surface battle, and support its own and friendly forces

[1928] Consequently, the Air Force will need to be permanently equipped with modern and effective combat, transport, and combat support equipment to fulfill the functions imposed on it by its core mission

[1929] However, a modern and technologically advanced Air Force is of little value if it lacks personnel properly trained and educated in the institutional values and with the skills to maintain and operate its weapons systems

[1930] In this regard, the quality of instruction and training will be continuously increased, aimed at ensuring a level of readiness that will allow them to fulfill their mission when the political level so dictates

[1931] THE MEANS OF NATIONAL DEFENSE 276 Book of National Defense of Chile 2010 Development Area A permanent effort of the Air Force is to support national development, through air operations to assist citizens and the integration and cohesion of the national territory, as well as industrial, technological and academic development

[1932] The incorporation of modern aircraft and spacecraft into the Institution, and its specialized personnel, strengthens its capabilities to fulfill its social responsibility

[1933] The fact that the FACH continues to provide aerospace resources and devices allows it, in addition to its basic function, to continue offering the country new geospatial technology capabilities to obtain and process timely, valid, and up-to-date information on the various variables that enhance and determine sustainable development

[1934] Since defense is linked to the country's development, actions that facilitate educational, recreational, charitable, and other activities that promote social inclusion and national growth will continue to be promoted, fostering the efficient use of energy and natural resources

[1935] International Scope Continue developing air capabilities to cooperate in achieving the foreign policy objectives set by the Chilean government at the local, regional, and global levels

[1936] The range, speed, and altitude characteristics of its vehicles give the Air Force the capacity to be used as an effective instrument to support the international interests of the Chilean State

[1937] Consequently, the institution will enhance its capacity to participate in local, regional, and global cooperation, participating in multinational forces in peacekeeping operations and humanitarian activities, and integrating aeronautical technical and cooperation schemes between air forces and other related entities

[1938] Likewise, the Air Force will continue to develop measures of mutual trust, in accordance with the defense policy, THE MEANS OF NATIONAL DEFENSE 277 Book of National Defense of Chile 2010 XX

[1939] Functions and Tasks organizing or participating in multinational exercises, as well as promoting bilateral or multilateral meetings between foreign air forces

[1940] In each of its areas of action, the Air Force performs various functions and tasks, both in peacetime and in conflict situations

[1941] a. In peacetime • Training to fulfill the institutional mission: all activities carried out by the institution must be aimed at obtaining the skills to fulfill the mission, at the time so determined by the political authority

[1942] • Protect the country from aerial threats: As part of its mission, the Air Force must exercise control and exploitation of its own airspace, through surveillance and positive control of national airspace

[1943] • Develop national air power: it must study and propose the development of its resources, with the purpose of structuring a force that allows it to efficiently fulfill its mission

[1944] • Cooperate in deterrence: An Air Force prepared to efficiently fulfill its mission is the best cooperation that can be provided in deterrence, thereby inhibiting the use of force by any enemy

[1945] • Contribute to Chile's international projection: Activities aimed at international cooperation, participation in peacekeeping missions, combined exercises, and others contribute to achieving our country's foreign policy objectives

[1946] • Cooperate with national development: by cooperating with national security, the Air Force is helping to create the conditions that the country requires for its development. It also complements its contribution when, among other actions, it provides direct support to citizens, facilitates the union of the national territory, supports the maintenance of sovereignty, and participates in the industrial and technological development of the country

[1947] • Contribute to national cohesion: The harmonious integration of all sectors of the nation is a goal of the Chilean State, which contributes to by preserving and disseminating the values of nationality, enabling territorial integration, cooperating in social solidarity, maintaining a fluid relationship with Chilean society, and contributing to the country's historical and cultural identity

[1948] • Promote Chile's aerospace interests: harmoniously promote all aerospace interests, with the goal of fostering a positive and proactive attitude among Chilean society in favor of the development of national air and space activities. • Direct and coordinate air defense

[1949] b. In a crisis situation • Be an efficient instrument at the political level: the Air Force is a key instrument for crisis management, given the characteristics of air power, such as mobility, flexibility, rapid response capacity and concentration, among others

[1950] • Protect the country from aerial threats: As part of its mission, the Air Force must exercise control and exploitation of its own airspace, through surveillance and positive control of national airspace

[1951] • Contribute to crisis management: contribute to the resolution of the crisis at the national and international level, in accordance with the provisions of the legitimately established authority and current regulations

[1952] • Direct and coordinate air defense

[1953] THE MEANS OF NATIONAL DEFENSE Book of National Defense of Chile 2010 Chapter C. In a situation of war • Destroy or neutralize adversary air, land or naval forces: the Air Force, through its capabilities, contributes to the achievement of strategic objectives, destroying or neutralizing the adversary's air, land or naval forces

[1954] • Direct and coordinate air defense

[1955] • Achieve the degree of air control required for the development of military operations

[1956] Achieve the degradation of the adversary's air offensive power: as a result of the application of air power, increase freedom of action for the development of own land, naval and air operations

[1957] Furthermore, by increasing the degree of airspace control, it reduces the freedom of adversary action, thus optimizing the integrated and synchronized application of all national military capabilities

[1958] Participate in the surface situation: due to its characteristics, the Air Force has the capacity to operate in three dimensions: land, sea, and air

[1959] The objective of participating in a surface situation is to cooperate with friendly forces to prevent the enemy from using its military power

[1960] • Strategically weaken the adversary: Use air power in strategic precision operations to destroy or damage enemy capabilities and thus sustain the war effort. • Conduct strategic aerial reconnaissance: Seeks to gather information about the adversary necessary for the formulation of military policies and plans at the national and theater levels. • Conduct strategic airlift: Provide strategic airlift of cargo and military personnel or civilians between the interior and theaters of operations. • Protect the country from aerial threats: As part of its mission, the Air Force must exercise control and exploitation of its own airspace through surveillance and positive control of national airspace

[1961] THE MEANS OF NATIONAL DEFENSE Book of National Defense of Chile 2010 Chapter ORGANIZATION OF THE AIR FORCE

[1962] Organization To fulfill its mission, the Air Force has a structure made up of organizations of the following type: Commander in Chief The highest institutional command is exercised by an officer with the rank of Air General with the position of Commander in Chief of the Chilean Air Force, who is responsible for the highest leadership of the Institution and its administrative management

[1963] To accomplish this, it relies on the General Staff of the Air Force, which advises it on the planning, senior management, and control of the institution, to meet the demands of air activities in peacetime, in crisis situations, and in wartime

[1964] Advisory and Governing Bodies a

[1965] General Staff of the Air Force It is the working body of the Commander-in-Chief, whose mission is to advise him in the planning, superior direction and control of the Institution, in order to satisfy the demands of air activities in peacetime and employment in wartime

[1966] Operational Units or Forces

[1967] Major Operational Unit The Combat Command is the Major Operational Unit and reports directly to the Commander-in-Chief of the Air Force

[1968] Its mission is to prepare, train, and lead the Institution's force structure with the goal of achieving the highest level of readiness and meeting the objectives set by national war planning

[1969] b. Operational units They are represented by five air brigades, whose jurisdictions cover the entire national territory, both continental and insular and Antarctic

[1970] Air brigades are operational units that exercise command within a geographic area

[1971] To this end, they have the necessary air and ground resources in their respective jurisdictional areas, with the goal of achieving and maintaining a level of operational readiness consistent with institutional planning

[1972] Each of them is commanded by a General Officer who reports directly to the Commander of the Combat Command

[1973] Each air brigade has aviation groups, anti-aircraft defense groups, and telecommunications and computer groups

[1974] That is to say, these units embody the Institution's air power, deploying its capabilities and means to carry out the assigned tasks

[1975] THE MEANS OF NATIONAL DEFENSE Book of National Defense of Chile 2010 Chapter GRAPHIC 50 DEPLOYMENT OF AIR BRIGADES Support Organs a

[1976] It is an executive body that reports directly to the Commander-in-Chief of the Air Force, whose mission is to obtain, provide, maintain and develop the material, technological and logistical information resources to satisfy the needs derived from war and development planning, and those generated by institutional activities

[1977] b. Personnel Command It is an executive body that reports directly to the Commander-in-Chief of the Air Force, whose mission is the superior administration of personnel in the areas of human resources, education, health, social welfare and air reserve, with the purpose of comprehensively developing the Institution's personnel in support of the mission of the Chilean Air Force

[1978] c. Aerofotogrammetric Service (SAF) It is a technical executive unit dependent on the Combat Command of the Chilean Air Force

[1979] Its mission is to meet institutional needs in the field of remote sensing, aerophotogrammetry, and related techniques, as well as to regulate and develop official State aeronautical cartography and complementary plans

[1980] Additionally, satisfy national needs in its area of expertise, carrying out work specific to its specialty, at the request of individuals and all entities covered by Law No. 18,575, the Constitutional Organic Law on the General Foundations of State Administration

[1981] All of the above, with the purpose of effectively contributing to aeronautical safety, defense, and national development, through geospatial imagery and information

[1982] THE MEANS OF NATIONAL DEFENSE Book of the National Defense of Chile 2010 Other Organizations a

[1983] Directorate General of Civil Aeronautics (DGAC) is an agency dependent on the Commander-in-Chief of the Chilean Air Force, through which the State regulates and supervises air activity, both national and international, that takes place within the airspace controlled by our country

[1984] It also controls the air activities carried out by national airlines abroad

[1985] The strategic products provided by the Directorate General of Civil Aeronautics are "Airport Services", "Air Navigation Services", "Operational Safety Services" and "Meteorological Services

[1986] The DGAC fulfills its mission by providing these strategic products, which are distributed through the extensive national airport network, comprised of 330 airports and airfields spread from Arica to Antarctica, including those located on our island territory

[1987] Aeronautical activity is carried out within the security framework provided by the General Directorate of Civil Aeronautics, providing the necessary means and services to generate connectivity within the territory

[1988] This is a fundamental condition, considering the complex geography of our country

[1989] Hence, it should be considered a social good, as it allows for immediate assistance and relief to those in need, providing closeness and protection to the national community

[1990] The work carried out by the DGAC transcends the national sphere

[1991] The fast, efficient, and safe air transport allows the Chilean State to connect with the globalized world and be present in international economic markets with its products and services, which constitutes an important contribution to the country's growth

[1992] This contribution by the Directorate to the world of aviation has also contributed to the growth of tourism in the country, facilitating access to our natural attractions, which in many cases are only possible by air

[1993] THE MEANS OF NATIONAL DEFENSE Book of National Defense of Chile 2010 Since 1930, the DGA has provided assistance and protection to air navigation, through the gradual and constant application of cutting-edge technology

[1994] This has enabled it to create the conditions for the proper development of aeronautical activity, within a framework of speed, safety, and efficiency

[1995] Media The rise of air power brought a completely different perspective to the concept of the battlefield

[1996] The two-dimensional paradigm, which had existed for centuries and was in force until then, changed with the use of aircraft in military operations

[1997] The ability to operate in the third dimension (air), with its ability to expose all enemy centers of gravity, brought another element to the battlefield equation

[1998] Today, new concepts of warfare and technological advances have given way to the concept of “battlespace,” understanding that battles are not only fought on land, sea, and air, but also require support from space technologies and (in the case of powers with use and control of space platforms) along information highways and information fronts

[1999] For an air commander operating in the battlespace, it is crucial to consider air, maritime, space, information, and ground operations aspects in the planning and execution of military operations

[2000] These battlespace dimensions can change over time as the mission expands or contracts, depending on operational objectives and force composition

[2001] In this regard, and considering the means assigned to his command and the coordination of the air and air defense means of other components, the three main functions in the mission of an air commander are: Plan and coordinate all air operations

[2002] THE MEANS OF NATIONAL DEFENSE Book of National Defense of Chile 2010 • Constitute itself as the airspace coordination authority, being responsible for the entire airspace control system

[2003] Become the air defense commander, being responsible for air surveillance and defense

[2004] For the efficiency of the Air Force, considering the quality and quantity of resources is essential

[2005] A balance must be reached between the two, because the main measure of final comparison between forces is their potential

[2006] Increasing operational capabilities in all weather conditions, providing precision weaponry, and improving survivability are among the tasks that contribute to developing potential advantages

[2007] The Air Force's air and ground assets must be capable of surviving and operating in a hostile electromagnetic environment

[2008] Technological progress requires the continuous development of institutional capacities

[2009] When designing future weapons systems, the evolution of air power must be taken into account so that they do not become obsolete once their implementation is complete

[2010] This requires ensuring that weapons systems and associated equipment do not degrade over time, that they receive appropriate maintenance, and that they are modernized in accordance with available and accessible scientific and technological advances

[2012] FINANCIAL RESOURCES Chapter XXII

[2013] RESOURCES APPLIED IN SCIENCE, TECHNOLOGY AND INDUSTRY Chapter XXIII

[2014] RESOURCES APPLIED TO SOCIAL AND ENVIRONMENTAL AFFAIRS Defense is a public good that requires funding from the State: first, because it fulfills a basic social function for the existence of society and, second, because it is a good whose provision cannot be provided by the market

[2015] Defense constitutes a pure social good, and it is the State that decides how and how much of this socially demanded public good to produce and, accordingly, allocate resources through the mechanisms it has available for this purpose

[2016] The financial resources provided by the State are allocated to activities specific to defense and its institutions, but are also applied to the areas of science, technology, and industry, as well as to the social and environmental dimensions relevant to defense that contribute to the country's sustainable development

[2017] FINANCIAL AND DEFENSE RESOURCES The period between the publication of the 2002 Defense Book and this new version has seen significant developments in the way the State allocates resources to finance its functions and policies, how it spends them, and how it delivers them

[2018] This has resulted in a change in the vision of the economic problem of defense, both in form and substance, adapting to the new paradigms of management of public resources and the methodological and technical requirements that the administration of resources destined for the modernization of defense has demanded

[2019] Concepts such as transparency, fiscal responsibility, and the modernization of budget management for the entire public sector have been complemented, in the case of the defense sector, by innovative methodologies in terms of accountability, such as the publication of the financial statements of the armed forces, which are compiled in centralized evaluation and information systems for budget management

[2020] This is a process that is underway and should be consolidated in a few years

[2021] Defense is a public good that requires state funding: first, because it fulfills a basic social function for the existence of society and, second, because it is a good whose provision cannot be provided by the market

[2022] Market allocation mechanisms have no practical application in the case of defense because, unlike what happens with other goods, there is no rivalry in its consumption – everyone receives defense equally – and no one can consume the defense good exclusively to the detriment of others

[2023] Modernization of State Management Since 2002, four laws have been enacted that have a significant impact on the way the State manages its business: Law No. 19,863, which, among other things, establishes regulations on reserved expenditures; Law No. 19,886, on public supplies or purchases; Law No. 20,128, on fiscal responsibility; and Law No. 20,285, on access to public information or transparency

[2024] These regulations are complemented by a countercyclical fiscal policy of structural surpluses, the adoption of accrual-based accounting in government accounting, stricter oversight by both the Comptroller General of the Republic and the Chamber of Deputies, and the execution of institutional and ministerial audits according to more comprehensive guidelines

[2025] All of the above has meant that defense institutions have adopted the new legislation, with some exceptions related to the country's security, adapting processes and procedures for allocating resources, preparing their budgets, and managing the resources allocated to them

[2026] Furthermore, beyond the aforementioned legal regulations, the sector has sought to bring the resource allocation system closer to defense planning

[2027] Over time, the new budgetary instruments being used or designed for future use will make it possible to better link force development, expressed in the planning of medium- and long-term weapons system acquisitions, with the volume of financial resources necessary to meet operational requirements

[2028] A better correlation between planning and spending will facilitate maintaining military assets at a readiness level commensurate with the country's fiscal capabilities and the requirements of our defense, with the criteria inherent in a joint vision of force readiness and employment serving as a guide for the budgetary process

[2029] Defense Spending and Other Macroeconomic Variables Defense spending is a controversial concept

[2030] As an expression of the resources that society allocates to defense, this concept provides the country and the international community with a vision of the State's commitment to achieving external security that allows it to protect the national population, safeguard its sovereignty, preserve its territory, and advance the achievement of its interests and objectives

[2031] For this reason, it is convenient and necessary to clarify what this expense means and what its amount is

[2032] First, the budget of the Ministry of National Defense and its dependent institutions is geared toward very diverse objectives, encompassing, in addition to the production of the social good that is defense, other state functions such as security in maritime activity and air navigation, the country's cartographic and oceanographic development, internal security—specifically, citizen security—and health services provided to military personnel, their pensioners, and the community

[2033] Furthermore, the Armed Forces pension system is a special system, distinct from the individual capitalization system that most Chileans must adhere to

[2034] It covers the contingency of retirement after twenty years of service and, with special features, covers common social contingencies, such as widowhood, orphanhood, occupational accidents and illnesses, and others

[2035] This system is not included in the budget of the Ministry of National Defense, but is part of the budget of the Ministry of Labor and Social Security

[2036] However, some internationally used calculation methodologies include the forecast of the passive sector of the Armed Forces in the general concept of defense spending

[2037] In the Chilean case, pension spending has had a sustained increase and since 2003 has exceeded spending on active personnel in military institutions

[2038] This fact considerably increases Chilean defense spending and, to a certain extent, distorts the figures for military spending in the strict sense, or "spending actually incurred in defense functions

[2039] In short, the concept of defense spending applied to different national realities around the world can present serious methodological difficulties for its general definition

[2040] However, the economic characteristics of a pure or perfect public good such as defense mean that its demand must be socially determined, which allows the methodological terrain to be limited to universally applicable criteria

[2041] Specifically, its relationship with the Gross Domestic Product (GDP), with the defense spending of neighboring countries, with the relative prices of the goods needed to produce defense, and with the country's integration into regional security systems

[2042] See Chapter XV

[2043] “Military Strength and Intent: Its Indicators”. Defense Spending and GDP Considered in relation to GDP, it can be stated that Chile’s defense spending has been systematically reduced over the last two decades, reaching a level comparable with other countries internationally

[2044] The concept of “fiscal contribution” is used as the effort made by the country to finance defense

[2045] The figures for this concept tend to coincide over the long term with those for the concept of “defense spending” or expenditure actually incurred on the defense function (military spending

[2046] There may be observable differences in the comparison of both indicators year to year, which are due to various factors

[2047] The actual annual expenditure on the defense function may vary depending on cash balances, transfers intended to finance activities of the Armed Forces other than the defense function, and payments deferred to other budget periods

[2048] Chart 51 shows the “fiscal contribution” to defense in relation to GDP, allowing us to conclude that, despite the volume of resources allocated to replacing obsolete weapons systems since 1990, defense has reduced its demands on the economy and the Treasury

[2049] In other words, the country's development and economic growth have made it possible to reduce the percentage of defense spending, or military spending in the strictest sense, relative to GDP

[2050] The reduction in the economic effort required to produce defense has been achieved despite the fact that the country's own development generates pressure for spending to grow

[2051] For example, military personnel's salaries must be consistent with the salaries of the rest of the public sector, and these, in turn, must be consistent with those of the private sector

[2052] Likewise, replacing equipment that has reached the end of its useful life represents a significant technological leap, with higher costs explained not only by the acquisition of more modern equipment, but also by the need for more qualified and more expensive, albeit smaller, personnel

[2053] However, when looking at the relationship between military and defense spending and GDP over the long term, it is clear that spending trends are linked to the contingencies Chile has faced and the changes that have occurred both in the country and in the region and the world

[2054] In fact, Figure 52 shows that the problems that dragged on throughout the 20th century, and became critical in the 1970s, led to a sharp increase in spending and economic effort to fund defense

[2055] As indicated, since 1990 there has been a sustained decline in military spending or spending on defense, despite specific increases in the ordinary budget originating from salary increases between 1997 and 1999, and the ups and downs produced by the performance of Law No. 13,196, Reserved Copper

[2056] Regarding the impact of this law, the curve reflects, for example, the expenditure generated since 2005 by the increase in the international price of copper and the greater availability of resources that this entailed, allowing for more resources to be allocated—albeit in a contained manner—to complete the Armed Forces' equipment replacement plans

[2057] In short, for the years 2007 and 2008, the spending figure is between 1.2 percent and 1.3 percent of GDP

[2058] Analyzing the fiscal contribution to defense in relation to total fiscal spending, as shown in , it can be seen that this contribution is declining in a context of rising fiscal spending and slower growth in military spending

[2059] That is, over time, the relationship between fiscal contribution and fiscal expenditure has become smaller

[2060] Methodologies for calculating defense spending In addition to the difficulties in defining a univocal and universally applicable concept of “defense spending”, various methodologies have been designed for its calculation

[2061] The best known are those of the International Monetary Fund (IMF), non-governmental organizations such as the Stockholm International Peace Research Institute (SIPRI) and the London-based International Institute of Strategic Studies (IISS), specialized publications such as those of the British publishing group "Jane's," and the measurement methodology used by the United States Department of State

[2062] However, not all of these methodologies adequately reflect the reality of defense spending in different countries, distorting the image of some states, including Chile

[2063] • ECLAC methodology to calculate defense spending For this reason, in conjunction with Argentina, and to clear up the conceptual debate on this matter, the Economic Commission for Latin America (ECLAC) was asked to design a common standardized Chilean-Argentine methodology to measure military or defense spending

[2064] Once approved by the governments of both countries in 2001, a joint effort was made, with the support of ECLAC, to produce a series of data between 1996 and 2004 calculated using the criteria established therein

[2065] As is known, the ECLAC methodology considers three expenditure aggregates: G1 corresponds to General or Basic Military Expenditure; G2, known as Expanded Military Expenditure, is composed of basic military expenditure plus state pension contributions; and G3, which corresponds to Total Military Expenditure, adds to G2 the development and military industry activities not included as expenditure in the regular budget

[2066] G3 is therefore total defense spending or general defense spending

[2067] Shows the figures for the three levels of expenditure aggregates between 1996 and 2004 (in red, ECLAC methodology), and between 2005 and 2008 (in blue, calculation by the Ministry of Defense with data from the Ministry of Finance-Chile

[2068] In a separate exercise from the one carried out by ECLAC with its methodology, the Budget Directorate (DIPRES) of the Chilean Ministry of Finance (MINHDA-CHILE) measured military spending between 1989 and 2006, using the “Functional Classification of Central Government Expenditures” constructed by the United Nations

[2069] The criteria used by the Ministry of Finance to define military spending were similar to those used by ECLAC to calculate G1, and it is interesting to note, when comparing Chart 55 with the previous one, that the spending figures recorded in relation to GDP are very similar

[2070] GRAPH 55 BASIC DEFENSE EXPENDITURE ACCORDING TO DIPRES/MINHDA-CHILE (UN Classification

[2071] The Defense Budget The sources of financing for national defense are varied

[2072] The two main sources, which generate over 95 percent of the defense's financial resources, correspond to the fiscal contribution included in the annual Public Sector Budget Law, in national and foreign currency, and the funds from the so-called Copper Reserve Law

[2075] The characteristics of the financing sources are dissimilar

[2076] Some of these funds are part of the annual budget and others are not

[2077] Certain funds also have a specific purpose, while other funds can be used for a wider variety of purposes

[2078] The budget structure of the Ministry of National Defense is organized into chapters by Undersecretariat, which include the budget of the Armed Forces institutions, and complementary chapters, such as the budgets of health organizations and other support agencies, which manage independent budgets, with specific objectives and income from different sources

[2079] In addition, there are chapters referring to services and general directorates that are directly linked to the Ministry in budgetary matters
